Foreword
Most people think of history’s marvels of engineering as products of 
stone, bronze, or iron—­the works of vast, conquering empires. I once 
held that view too. Years ago, before I became actively involved in my 
tribe and embraced my spiritual and cultural heritage, I traveled to 
many great monuments and World Heritage sites—­the Egyptian pyr-
amids, the Roman Colosseum, the Athenian Acropolis, the Great Wall 
of China—­picturing them as the ultimate architectural masterpieces.
But when I first saw the geometric earthworks of the Hopewell 
culture in Ohio, built by Native Americans who were once considered 
“savages,” I instantly recognized what great feats of genius they are. Ev-
erything I had read in books and been taught in numerous educational 
settings about our Ancestors was challenged, and I could no longer be 
silent and not speak out on behalf of these wonders and their builders.
Here, laid across the landscape not with concrete but with 
Mother Earth, one basketful at a time, were squares, circles, and 
mounds with repeated placements of breathtaking and unques-
tionable precision. They were not monuments to a single ruler but 
communal masterpieces depicting their builders’ connections to 
each other and to the cosmos. It became clear to me that we have 
been ignoring, minimizing, and eliminating the hidden history 
of this continent—­stories of brilliance, community, and spiritual 
depth that now need to reshape our understanding.
Southern Ohio is the place to discover these stories. Most of 
these amazing places are located here—­including the eight now 
designated as UNESCO World Heritage sites and known as the 
Hopewell Ceremonial Earthworks. They give testimony to an 
extraordinary civilization. They reveal a profound architectural 
genius by creating sacred art from Mother Earth, combined with 
knowledge of the Sky World above.
This book invites you to step onto that sacred ground, to seek 
that profound spirituality by experiencing these places in their 


--- PDF PAGE 6 ---

xi
x
Foreword
rich cultural, historical, and landscape settings. Author John E. 
Hancock combines the appreciative eye of a well-­traveled architect 
with braided strands of ideas from thirty years of personal experi-
ence and collaboration with tribal scholars, leaders, and citizens, 
along with numerous other content experts who help illuminate 
the genius of this Indigenous civilization.
The geometrical and astronomical acumen of these ancient peo-
ple is staggering. Newark’s Octagon Earthworks align perfectly with 
the complex 18.6-­year moonrise cycle, a triumph of observation that 
surpasses any other on the planet. This was not a lucky or accidental 
coincidence; it was a deliberate, monumental expression of a deep-­
seated scientific and spiritual understanding. These intricate land-
forms required sophisticated knowledge and precision layout tools. 
They stand as a testament to the power of communal cooperation, 
where thousands of Native Americans worked together over genera-
tions to shape Mother Earth into a celestial calendar.
Traveler’s Guide to Ancient Ohio takes you along paths away from 
the busy roads of our modern and often frenetic lives, and toward 
an older and more profound history. The beauty of visiting these 
sites is that if you stand still, and wait long enough, they will reveal 
themselves slowly. As you walk the path of any of these earthworks, 
you won’t just see piles of dirt. You will feel the peacefulness, the 
tranquility, the inspiration of a spiritual connection to ancient cos-
mologies. By engaging with these places directly, you are doing 
more than sightseeing. You are participating in a quiet revolution 
of understanding. You are replacing centuries of misinformation 
with firsthand knowledge and reverence for a truly remarkable and 
inspirational civilization. And you will walk away inspired, wanting 
to be a better person.
The legacy of the earthwork builders is not one of crumbling 
empires but of enduring wisdom laid out across the land itself. 
They built not for conquest but for ceremony, for gathering, and 
for connection—­with each other, with the land, with their Creator, 
and with the universe. As you journey through this guide, you will 
be invited to see not only the remains of a great civilization but a 
living testament to a different kind of human genius—­an uncom-
mon genius. It is a legacy that still has much to teach us about what 
truly matters.
Chief Glenna J. Wallace
Eastern Shawnee Tribe of Oklahoma
Preface
“I Had No Idea.” One day in the mid-­1990s, after I had been teaching 
the architecture of the world’s great civilizations at the University 
of Cincinnati for fifteen years, a new graduate student came to my 
office and said, “I want to do my thesis on the ancient earthworks 
of Ohio.” And I said, “The what?” When he showed me the beauti-
ful lithographs in E.G. Squier and E.H. Davis’s 1848 volume, Ancient 
Monuments of the Mississippi Valley, I was, like many others before 
and since, astonished: Lying right out there across our southern 
Ohio landscapes were the remains of huge, enigmatic works of  
architectural, geometrical, and astronomical genius.
With family, friends, or students in tow, I hit the back roads of 
southern Ohio to find them. But many, so beautifully delineated in 
the nineteenth century, were gone—­erased by modern development 
or agriculture. And many of the surviving ones were hard to see—­
forested, or degraded, or just too large to comprehend. The ones that 
could be seen offered an unusual, subtle architectural experience, 
unlike any other monuments I had ever encountered. Moreover, 
these places seemed to be known only to archaeologists; no one in 
my field had heard of them, nor, as I soon learned, had most of the 
Indigenous descendants of their builders.
The Ancient Ohio Trail. I was motivated to share the story, but 
if these magnificent places were to be brought out of obscu-
rity for public audiences, they would need two things: visual-
ization tools to make them comprehensible, and interpretive 
voices from diverse perspectives. So, with funding from the 
National Endowment for the Humanities and others, I led in-
terdisciplinary teams at the University of Cincinnati to design 
digital media programs where computer graphics showcased the 
sites’ elusive scope and conceptual power, and where Indigenous 
leaders and other scholars complemented what the archaeologists 
were telling us about their history and meanings.


--- PDF PAGE 7 ---

xii
xiii
Preface
Preface
These projects took us out among the region’s historic towns 
and scenic landscapes, revealing rich, varied evidence of Ohio’s 
complex histories and diverse geographies, as well as its brilliant 
ancient inhabitants. Those travel experiences persuaded me that 
the equivalent of a Michelin Green Guide to southern Ohio was 
needed. This book is the result: It is an expanded update of those 
media projects and our Ancient Ohio Trail website. Readers may 
also detect an homage to all those well-­worn Michelin guides still 
on my bookshelf from the European travels of my earlier career.
The Hopewell Ceremonial Earthworks. In 2023, eight Ohio earth-
work sites were inscribed on the UNESCO World Heritage List—­
specifically for their landscape design genius, and for their testi-
mony to the distinctive American Indian culture that built them 
two millennia ago. World Heritage status implies that visitors’ ex-
periences of these places—­with their scenic, historic, and interpre-
tive contexts—­should be both regionally coherent, and at a global  
curatorial standard. My aim is to help visitors appreciate their 
often-­subtle forms and their nearly incomprehensible scope, to-
gether with the range of ideas that enrich and ground their mean-
ings. Some content and themes here will overlap with my (and 
several collaborators’) 2026 re-­edition of those eight sites’ UNESCO 
nomination document, entitled Hopewell Ceremonial Earthworks, 
but the two volumes have very different emphases.
Acknowledgments. After thirty years, there have been far more 
collaborators in this work than can be named here. I must begin, 
though, with my late, brilliant, Ancient Ohio Trail coauthor Cath-
ryn Long, many of whose beautiful phrases remain in these pag-
es, and my University of Cincinnati digital media team leaders 
Jose Kozan and Elizabeth Bartley. Many of the key scholars, tribal 
leaders, and others whom we interviewed are quoted here, or ap-
pear in the linked videos—­including Brad Lepper, Bret Ruby, Marti 
Chaatsmith, and Chief Glenna Wallace. Many of these same people 
(and more) were also central to the World Heritage effort, and for 
this book project they continued to be generous with their time, 
expert advice, and local knowledge.
Others provided special encouragement toward the long-­
term goal of regionally curated tourism, notably Nancy Stranahan,  
Melody 
Young, 
Melinda 
Huntley, 
and 
the 
Ohio 
History 
 
Connection’s Jennifer Aultman and Jerry Dannemiller. For route 
testing, I must thank Buck Niehoff, Hope Taft, and friends, who,  
over several consecutive weekends in 2021, walked this book’s  
World Heritage Route with heroic logistical support from  
Gary Meisner. For help with new site maps, photographs, and videos,  
I am indebted to Aaron Keirns, Rich Bitting, Steve Plattner, and 
Nathan Harris. Janice Radlove provided enormous help with both 
route maps and page designs. Finally, I thank Liz Scarpelli and Pam  
Wissman at the University of Cincinnati Press for their confidence in 
this project, and Beth Pratt, Tyler Balli, Jeff Kallet, and Laura André 
at Ohio University Press for their professional expertise throughout 
the process.
John E. Hancock
Professor Emeritus of Architecture
University of Cincinnati
August 25, 2025

The Region and Its History
1
The Region and Its History
THE REGION
A Brilliant Civilization. Two thousand years ago, here in southern 
Ohio, Ancestors of today’s American Indians created the world’s 
largest concentration of monumental earthen architecture—­works 
of unparalleled scope, genius, and precision. For 400 years, their 
ideas dominated the cultural and religious life of half the conti-
nent. Much evidence of their huge ceremonial centers and their 
rich cultural life remains across this region, well managed in 
public parks and preserves, and increasingly illuminated today 
by the perspectives of the modern Tribal Nations who are among 
their descendants. Immerse yourself in the heartland of ancient  
America, where astonishing earthen monuments are still here to 
tell the stories—­of their ancient origins, their modern interpreta-
tions, and their landscape settings.
A Complex History. This book is about the earthworks themselves 
but also situates them in other contexts. The precise enclosures, 
effigies, embankments, mounds, and walled hilltops reflect the 
lives and knowledge of their builders, but later centuries have 
added other histories. Two hundred years ago, Ohio was the focus 
of American expansion, and during the decades around 1800, the 
surveyors and settlers who crossed the Allegheny Mountains en-
countered these intact but forest-­covered monuments—­admiring 
and measuring them, but also misinterpreting and destroying 
them. Ohio soon became the focus of war and repression against 
American Indians, even as mounds were being looted for treasure, 
and as many now-­vanished sites were being documented in some of 
America’s first pioneering archaeological efforts.
Mound Hill Cemetery, near Amesville, is one of many settler burial grounds quietly  
acknowledging the commemorative power of mounds (see page 187).


--- PDF PAGE 9 ---

2
3
The Region and Its History
The Region and Its History
A Varied Landscape. Southern Ohio holds layers of geological and 
cultural history as rich as anywhere else on the planet. The deep 
geology of the region has left a wide variety of landscapes and  
ecologies. Glaciers carved broad expanses that had long before 
been sedimented beneath an ancient sea. Rivers carved narrow 
canyons or were reoriented in gigantic, ancient valleys. Today, 
eroded bedrock canyons shelter exotic species. Geological and eco-
logical zones meet, with often dramatic scenic consequences and 
unusual ecological abundance. The region’s brilliant Indigenous 
peoples thrived in these forests and prairies, and among these hills 
and rivers. They built earthworks whose subtle forms seem to echo 
the hills, valleys, and waterways.
POINTS OF VIEW
Ways of Knowing. The question “What are the earthworks?” does 
not have a single answer. When I first set out to learn about them in 
the 1990s, it seemed the discourse belonged only to archaeologists. 
The earthworks were routinely spoken of “as archaeological sites.” 
But the Native people I met saw them instead “as meaningful ances-
tral places.” At the time, these differing viewpoints felt contentious—­
Western science versus Indigenous tradition, and with emphasis 
on the “versus.” But since then, respectful collaborative approaches 
have been developing, and innovative blends of archaeological and 
Indigenous knowledge are beginning to illuminate the earthworks 
and their builders’ culture in new ways.
What follows in these pages are concise versions of the gen-
eral knowledge from both these domains, plus others, summarized 
from three decades of reading, interviewing, conversing, working, 
and touring with most of the leading site experts, with many Indig-
enous persons both in Ohio and Oklahoma, and with other scholars 
in related fields.
The Traveler’s View. As I toured these places, often with visiting 
students, architects, and scholars from around the world, I no-
ticed we were seeing them in yet another way—­“as cultural heritage 
destinations.” The stories from those initial “as’s” now lived within 
place-­experiences where human meaning and identification, spa-
tial movement and sensation, visual form and detail, embodied 
memory and anticipation, contextual settings and landscapes, all 
operated at once within an open interpretive engagement. In other 
words, the sites were now functioning “as works of architecture.”
World Heritage inscription now underscores this second pair 
of “as’s” because like their iconic counterparts from other global 
cultures—­the Parthenon, the Great Wall of China, Machu Picchu, 
and the rest—­Ohio’s earthworks will now tell their many stories in 
this way—­as the experienced, embodied touchstones of their mak-
ers’ brilliant civilization.
Positioning This Book. Each of these four points of view (and others) 
suggests questions and prioritizes approaches. But they are cumu-
lative and complementary, since the earthworks are all these things 
and more. My background as an architectural educator, traveler, and 
tour leader defines the central goal of this book which is to interpret, 
summarize, and present general knowledge and other related ideas 
(mostly from others) within an engaging, appreciation-­enhancing, 
spatially organized, full-­regional touring experience.
HOW TO USE THIS GUIDE
Nine Curated Routes. The region’s three international airports (John 
Glenn Columbus International Airport, CMH; Dayton International 
Airport, DAY; and Cincinnati/Northern Kentucky International Air-
port, CVG), and main interstate highways (I-­70, I-­71, and I-­75) connect 
quickly and easily to the routes, each of which is anchored by a major 
city or an important historic town. The World Heritage Route (divided 
East and West) connects the seven publicly accessible, UNESCO-­
inscribed Hopewell Ceremonial Earthworks. Branch routes follow 
the valleys of the Ohio River’s principal, south-­flowing tributaries to 
connect other major earthwork sites like Miami Fort, Serpent Mound, 
Marietta, and other points of interest. The recommended routes 
avoid interstates, as well as strip and chain development zones (easily 
found when needed), favoring scenic drives and historic towns. Fol-
low them in any direction, sequence, or depth. Link and customize 
them for any adventure through the Ohio Valley’s deep histories—­
rewarding journeys lasting from one day to three weeks, or more.
Wayfinding and Orientation. This book provides street addresses 
suitable (and tested) for standard car or smartphone navigation sys-
tems. These systems recognize many destination names, but not all.

The Region and Its History
The Region and Its History
Turn-­by-­turn suggestions are given here only when a certain  
experience is being recommended. Obtain information about hours 
and admissions (because it may change often) from the managing 
agencies’ websites, which are cited in each case. Earthwork site 
trail maps take you to numbered locations keyed to paragraphs—­
generally related to what is visible at that spot. Consult posted trail 
guides to confirm details or learn more.
Ratings and Seasons. Towns, sites, features, and other destinations 
are rated to help with prioritizing. Three stars means “worth the 
journey” or “don’t miss this.” At the earthwork sites, these judg-
ments mainly consider the architectural experience—­how vivid 
is the combination of seeing the earthworks and appreciating their 
meaning or formal impact—­and the text generally gives the ratio-
nale. The forms can be elusive, as some are in partial ruins, forest 
covered, too large to see across, or all of these. The time of year and 
time of day matter; for forested sites like Fort Ancient, October to 
April is best. The beauty of any site is most evident early or late in 
the day when shadows help to reveal their subtle forms.
Other Resources. Of course, this region has much more to offer—­
other themes and topics such as aviation history, the Underground 
Railroad, and many places of historical and architectural interest, 
especially in the larger cities. These are beyond the scope of this 
book but easily researched elsewhere. Museums include county 
historical societies (notably those in Warren and Ross); important 
regional museums in Portsmouth, Marietta, and Newark; and the 
major metropolitan or statewide institutions in Cincinnati, Day-
ton, and Columbus. Perhaps most significant for this topic is the 
Ohio History Connection’s Columbus facility, the Ohio History Cen-
ter, as well as that agency’s many historic sites across the region. 
With only a few exceptions, food and lodging suggestions are also  
beyond the scope of this book, but are readily found on county 
tourism websites, as well as online booking and rating services.
A Word About Naming. All place names in this book are in Ohio un-
less otherwise noted. The names of the major earthwork-­building 
cultures (Adena and Hopewell) are Western colonial impositions, 
and out of respect for current Native American preferences, they 
are never used here to refer to Indigenous Ancestors—­to any peo-
ple or tribe—­only to things (such as earthworks, time periods, 
practices, objects, and so on). Similarly, this book reflects a general 
(though not unanimous) agreement that our predecessors on this 
continent, and modern tribal citizens, may be referred to respect-
fully as Native American, Indigenous, or American Indian—­but 
never with the term “Indian” alone.
EARLY CHRONOLOGY
Ohio’s Bedrock. The land that is now Ohio lay beneath an ancient 
sea around 400 million years ago. The formation and erosion of 
mountain ranges to the east (now the Appalachians) and the drying 
seas left broad swaths of limestone in the west (beneath Cincin-
nati or prominent in the Clifton and Rocky Fork Gorges), as well 
as broad sandstone ridges to the east (Blackhand Gorge and the 
Hocking Hills region). The ancient sea and swampy forests also left 
deposits of coal and salt in the southeastern counties, which sup-
ported the state’s early industries.
VISITING SACRED PLACES
Native sacred places are . . . where Native people go to pray, to sing, to 
dance, for the good day, the precious earth, the blessing waters, the sweet 
air, and a peaceful life for all living beings.  .  .  . The best advice for the 
public at these places may be found in the Cheyenne instruction “Walk 
gently through life.” The protocols for Native sacred and historic places are 
the same as the unwritten rules for guests at a monument, cemetery, royal 
garden, or grand palace.
—Suzan Shown Harjo (Hodulgee Muscogee, Cheyenne)
The walls of Fort Ancient 
are at their most beautiful 
in low Sun angles from 
October to April.

The Region and Its History
The Region and Its History
A Diagonal Seam. Today, the main geographic structure of the re-
gion is generally flat terrain to the northwest (the glacial till plains), 
and intricate hills and hollows in the southeast (the Appalachian 
Plateau). These zones meet along a distinct boundary running diag-
onally from roughly Serpent Mound to Chillicothe, to Newark, and 
on to the northeast. This line is the northwestern edge of the Appa-
lachian Plateau, a zone of layered rock pressed upward by the for-
mation of the Appalachian chain and since deeply carved by rivers 
and streams. West of this line, the three major eras of glaciation—­
all of which stopped in nearly the same place—­reshaped the land-
scape and redirected the rivers between 3 million and 11,000 years 
ago. Driving from west to east today (approaching Granville or 
Chillicothe, for example) the front range of these unglaciated hills 
can be quite prominent.
The Preglacial Teays River. Before the first Pleistocene glacier 
(about 2.6 million years ago), the Ohio lands were drained by the 
Teays River, a huge waterway that rose in the high Appalachians, 
came down through West Virginia’s New River Gorge and Kanawha  
Valley and past Huntington to near Portsmouth, and then went 
north past Columbus and west across Indiana and Illinois to join the 
Mississippi. Two huge valleys between Chillicothe and Portsmouth 
are the remains of two of the Teays system’s ancient channels. 
Most of the region’s other large valleys, such as around Cincinnati, 
once carried its north-­flowing tributaries. By about 1 million years 
ago, the advancing glacier had dammed up these rivers, forming a 
huge lake which, as it spilled over, carved new channels heading 
southwest, including today’s Ohio River.
Till Plains, New Rivers. The flatter landscapes to the northwest are 
the areas covered by all three glacial advances. By their final retreat, 
about 12,000 years ago, they had left deep layers of debris (glacial 
till) across the land, directing new rivers to flow south toward the 
newly carved Ohio Valley. The Lower Scioto, and to a lesser extent 
the Great Miami, are the clearest examples of these new, smaller  
rivers taking over the huge valleys of the preglacial Teays system. 
The layered structure of these Teays-­age valleys provided ideal sites 
for the geometric earthworks—­perhaps most clearly visible at the 
Hopeton Earthworks. The large, flat, upper terraces were the flood 
plains of the ancient rivers—­well drained by deep gravel deposits, yet 
well above flooding by the current, smaller rivers.
Natural Wonders, Rich Ecologies. The ancient seas and rivers,  
the actions of the glaciers, and the varied topography created an 
abundance of natural wonders: rock-­faced canyons, wide-­open 
prairies, pockets of glacial-­era plants, remote dark-­Sky regions, 
deep forests, beautiful clear streams, abundant and diverse flora 
and fauna. Today, this makes for beautiful scenery and offers 
settings for recreational pleasure. But for millennia, these rich 
and abundant overlapping ecologies generously supported the  
Indigenous cultures that grew and thrived here in what we now call 
Ohio.
People 
Arrive 
(13,000 
to 
8,000 
BCE). 
The 
first 
people 
 
appeared in this region while glaciers still covered much of North  
America. They arrived from Asia by the ancient Bering Land Bridge 
to Alaska, or by boat, and spread, over many generations, across 
the continent. Now called “Paleoindians,” they were in the Ohio  
Valley as early as 13,000 years ago, living in mobile bands,  
gathering plants, and hunting. Their distinctive spear points have 
been found in the bones of long extinct ice age animals like the  
mastodon. They were skilled stone workers and discovered the  
beautiful rainbow-­colored stone from Ohio’s Flint Ridge, used by their 
descendants for centuries and still prized by flintknappers today.
Viewed from Chillicothe’s Grandview Cemetery, the huge, ancient Teays River Valley stretches 
southward, now carrying the much smaller Scioto.

The Region and Its History
The Region and Its History
Ecological Transition (8000 to 1500 BCE). When the glaciers melted, 
the tundra and pine forests of eastern North America were replaced 
by the ecological environments we know today: hardwood forests 
threaded by many rivers and streams. The large ice age animals be-
came extinct or moved northward, replaced gradually by deer and 
other woodland animals. The new environment (of this “Archaic” 
period) also presented a wealth of nuts, fruits, plants, and edible 
roots. People began to plant seeds and tend gardens, making east-
ern North America one of only a handful of places on Earth where 
agriculture began without outside influence. Over several millen-
nia, the changing flora and fauna, the forests and rivers, the hills 
and valleys, gave rise not only to new ways of life, but also to new 
beliefs—­a Woodland cosmology—­about the meanings and relation-
ships in the environment.
THE EARTHWORK BUILDERS
Adena Culture (1500 BCE to 100 CE). The first burial mounds in 
the Ohio Valley reflect new skills and customs. People still moved 
periodically, but they began to make pottery and erect thousands 
of great earthen burial structures around the Ohio River and its 
tributaries, showing a strong sense of community. Archaeologists 
named this culture “Adena” after Thomas Worthington’s Chilli-
cothe estate, where a mound excavation in 1906 revealed its typ-
ical characteristics. These people produced beautiful ceremonial 
objects and developed elaborate mound architecture, including 
circular ditches, pavements, and walls. Later Hopewell practices 
overlapped with the Adena, in years and in territory; they may have 
been the same people, or near neighbors with different views but 
mutual respect.
Adena Architecture. The markers of the Adena culture are the thou-
sands of burial mounds across the region, including many in West 
Virginia and Kentucky. Most often, they stand alone in river val-
leys or on hilltops. The Miamisburg Mound is the largest example 
in Ohio, and Marietta’s Conus Mound is the best surviving exam-
ple with an intact ring and ditch around it. Their profiles vary, but 
generally they are either spherical or conical. This book features 
several other Adena mounds—­for their unusual form (Norwood), 
exceptional beauty (Zaleski), or dramatic setting (Shrum). Despite 
much continuity from these Adena traditions, the Hopewell in-
creases in the size and complexity of earthworks—­and the quantity 
of ceremonial artistry—­were sudden and dramatic.
Hopewell Culture (100 BCE to 400 CE). Great new inspirations ush-
ered in Ancient Ohio’s golden age. People began to assemble over 
many generations, creating complex earthworks, enormous in 
scale, precise in geometry, and often aligned with celestial events.  
WATER, EARTH, AND SKY
The traditions of the Indigenous Ancestors in this Great Valley of the 
Ohio were intimately connected to the natural world—­to the riverine 
landscapes and the creatures of the forest. American Indian religion, ways 
of life, and beliefs about the world still hold a respectful kinship with all 
these things. A widespread and long-­standing idea connects all things 
within a three-­layered conception of the universe—­the Sky is the Above 
World, the Earth we live on is in the middle, and the Beneath World is a 
watery domain. Creation stories tell of the land—­the Earth—­being brought 
up out of the water by an animal, often to be placed on a turtle’s back. 
Each of these layers has its kindred creatures, but also its associated 
spirit powers: The Thunderbirds live in the Above World, and the Great 
Serpent or the Underwater Panther lives in the Beneath World. Mounds and 
earthworks (and the things placed in them) reflect these beliefs in 
multiple ways. Those connections are apparent when water is part of an 
earthwork’s design, or when small woodland creatures adorn the bowls of 
ceremonial smoking pipes whose columns of smoke rise into the Sky.
The now-­lost 
Biggs mound in 
South Portsmouth, 
Kentucky, was 
an example of 
an Adena mound 
with a surrounding 
ditch and ring.

13
The Region and Its History
The Region and Its History
Beneath carefully mounded layers of Earth, they left elaborate buri-
als of their leaders and loved ones, accompanied by beautifully 
crafted objects that reveal supreme artistic skill and the great reach 
of their trade networks. Burials indicate that there were no royal fam-
ilies or institutional hierarchies. The society’s leaders earned their 
power and expertise in a variety of areas; these would have included 
supervising the design and construction of the elaborate earthwork 
complexes. From their southern Ohio heartland, Hopewell practices 
and interactions spread across much of North America. This culture 
is called “Hopewell” because its richest ceremonial site was found 
on Mordecai Cloud Hopewell’s farm, just west of Chillicothe, in the 
1890s.
Hopewell Architecture. The huge Earth-­walled enclosures were of 
two types: those surrounding irregular hilltops (Fort Ancient) and 
those enclosing precise geometric spaces (the sites at Newark, or 
the Seip and Hopeton Earthworks). Gateways into the spaces could 
be complex (Fortified Hill), extraordinarily monumental (Newark’s 
Great Circle), or subtly sophisticated (Newark’s Octagon). Beneath 
mounds and walls lie the remains of monumental timber struc-
tures: Open woodhenges or multichambered ceremonial halls 
housed the people’s elaborate ceremonial life and the burials of 
their deceased kin. Those remains (both people and buildings) 
were often burned before being buried. Ponds, pits, and ditches 
were lined with stone and clay to create water features.
Hopewell Genius. Many sites across the region used a common di-
mension of 1,054 feet (the diameter of Newark’s Observatory Circle, 
now called the “OCD”) or its simple divisions. The near-­pinpoint 
accuracy of these repetitions, even between sites that are many 
miles apart, shows how construction knowledge, methods, and 
equipment must have been extraordinarily sophisticated. Astro-
nomical alignments were equally precise: Diagonals of several 
squares align to solstice sunrises and sunsets. Newark’s Octagon 
Earthworks, High Bank Works, and Fort Ancient are the only set of 
monuments on Earth so far known to calibrate the key risings and 
settings of the Moon during its very complex 18.6-­year cycle.
Fort Ancient Culture (900 to 1650). After about 400 CE, forests began 
reclaiming the geometric and hilltop earthworks. The focus of com-
munity life shifted from these vast ceremonial centers to growing 
villages. By about 900 CE, the large-­scale cultivation of corn was 
ushering in a whole new type of economy. These developments 
were shared with groups along the Mississippi River—­notably at 
Cahokia (near today’s St. Louis) and other large cities of the time. In 
the central Ohio Valley, they have been named the “Fort Ancient” 
culture because one of their villages was built near the much older 
(Hopewell-­era) walls of Fort Ancient.
Effigies and Villages. These village dwellers continued to build 
burial mounds, but more distinctive were their effigies—­Granville’s 
Alligator Mound (which is more likely the Underwater Panther), 
and of course the Great Serpent Mound in Adams County. Both have 
been dated to around 1100 CE, and so were the last major American 
Indian earthwork projects in the region. Substantial villages with 
ditched enclosures and a corn-­based economy were likely a pattern 
that endured into the lives of the Shawnee and Miami Tribes at the 
time of contact, a story well told at SunWatch Village near Dayton, 
and at Great Council State Park near Xenia.
CONTACT, MIGRATIONS, CONFLICTS
Horrific Plagues. Within just a few decades after Columbus and de 
Soto arrived in North America, more than 80 percent of its Indige-
nous population died—­victims of fast-­spreading European diseases 
to which they had no immunity. This was easily the most tragic event 
in the history of the continent. Along ancient trails and trade routes, 
the new diseases moved much faster than the new people did, so 
when the first Europeans finally arrived in the Ohio Valley, the 
Hopewell Earth-­walled 
enclosures combined 
huge geometric shapes, 
most often circles and 
squares, as here at the 
Hopeton Earthworks 
near Chillicothe.


--- PDF PAGE 15 ---

14
15
The Region and Its History
The Region and Its History
damage had already been done. Especially with the death of elders, 
tribal traditions weakened and knowledge was lost. People watched 
desperately as the old, reliable cures failed against the new diseases. 
All this sudden death and loss may help explain why, when Europe-
ans first encountered the Natives here, they could learn so little from 
them about who had built the earthworks, or why.
Trade and Migration. By the 1600s, not only had the ancient earthwork-­
building traditions faded, but large tribal migrations were underway. 
In what are called the Beaver Wars, the Iroquois were expanding into 
the Ohio Country from their homeland in upstate New York, scatter-
ing other tribes. They were after wider territories from which to sup-
ply the fast-­growing colonial and European market for furs, which 
they traded for manufactured goods including weapons. Meanwhile, 
other tribes were arriving in the area, having been displaced by the 
steady expansion of colonial settlement in the east and south. The 
first French and British traders to cross the Alleghenies encountered 
Miami, Wyandot, Ottawa, Shawnee, and Delaware (Lenape) people, 
among others. While no tribe is uniquely connected across the cen-
turies to the earthwork builders, all Eastern Woodland tribes share a 
common heritage, with ancestral sources in—­and some continuity of 
belief with—­the Adena, Hopewell, and Fort Ancient cultures.
The French and Indian War. By the 1750s, the struggle between France 
and Britain for colonial dominance in North America became open 
warfare, and from Nova Scotia to Virginia, both sides relied on military 
alliances with various Tribal Nations. Their traders and negotiators were 
in the Ohio Country setting up shop in large Indigenous settlements 
such as Lower Shawneetown at today’s Portsmouth. By 1758, the British 
had taken the Ohio lands from the French, and they ultimately won 
Canada five years later. Some of the Natives and colonials learned each 
other’s ways and languages. The lives of famous adventurers Daniel 
Boone and Simon Kenton reflect the complex, shifting relationships 
between the Euro-­Americans and the Indigenous peoples—­trade, 
diplomacy, capture, and occasionally friendship; then settlement and 
resistance; but ultimately war, and finally Removal.
Encroachment and Conflict. The 1750s to ’70s saw many skirmishes 
as more explorers and settlers pushed westward, first into Kentucky  
(then part of Virginia). During these decades, the Shawnee capital 
(chillicothe, or chief’s town) at Portsmouth was forced to retreat twice 
to locations farther up the Scioto. In 1774, on the eve of the American 
Revolution, escalating violence triggered a conflict called Dunmore’s 
War, in which Indigenous people fought unsuccessfully to defend their 
lands south of the Ohio against takeover by Virginia’s colonial militia. 
The peace treaty was signed on Ohio’s Pickaway Plains, near Circleville. 
Shawnee lands were being steadily threatened and encroached upon, 
and the tribe’s chillicothe had to move, for a third time, farther west to 
the Little Miami (now Great Council State Park, near Xenia).
The American Revolution. After 1776, these conflicts were part of the 
frontier theater of the Revolutionary War, and the Shawnee aligned 
with the British as a strategy to resist American colonial settlement 
in their homeland. Within a few years, the Americans were routing 
the British and their allies, and the Shawnee abandoned their town 
on the Little Miami too, as US government troops under George  
Rogers Clark (Daniel Boone among them) approached. Although the 
Ohio Country was not officially opened until the new nation won 
it from Great Britain in 1783, settlers were already pouring in. Fort 
Harmar was built in 1785 (at Marietta), initially to prevent pioneer 
squatting, but Fort Washington, just four years later (at Cincinnati), 
was built as an outpost to supply America’s wars against the tribes 
into the next decade.
The Northwest Indian War. All tribes in the region were threatened 
with losing their way of life and ability to feed their families—­the 
Shawnee, Miami, Lenape, Wyandotte, and Ottawa, among others. 
Many united to form the Northwestern Confederacy to resist Ameri-
can encroachment. Raids, murders, and reprisals on all sides ensued 
and escalated into all-­out frontier battles. Because the British were 
still the tribes’ allies, this warfare can be seen as a last phase of the 
American Revolution, only ending with the defeat of the Confeder-
acy in 1794 at the Battle of Fallen Timbers in northwest Ohio. The 
next year, the Treaty of Greenville removed the tribes entirely from 
southern Ohio, removed the British from everything south of the 
Great Lakes, and opened the future five-­state region of the Northwest 
Territory for American expansion.

The Region and Its History
The Region and Its History
SETTLEMENT AND REMOVAL
Surveyors and Settlers. When the American Revolution ended in 
1783, the new nation had little cash, so used its vast new territo-
ries as revenue sources or as payments to the war’s veteran offi-
cers. The former occurred in southeastern Ohio when Congress 
chartered the New England–­based “Ohio Company of Associates” 
to buy and resell for profit vast areas around what became Mari-
etta and Athens. The latter occurred in southwestern Ohio when 
the Virginia Military District (the area between the Scioto and Little 
Miami Rivers) was given in large parcels to that state’s Revolution-
ary War veterans. Surveying had already begun by the mid-­1780s, 
but within twenty years of the tribes’ defeat in 1794, settlers were 
pouring in across the region—­first on Ohio River flatboats, and then 
along Colonel Ebenezer Zane’s new road—­and most of the region’s 
main towns were established. The forests came down quickly for 
log houses, firewood, furniture, and agricultural fields. Road net-
works developed (many following well-­established Indigenous 
routes) with coach stops at regular intervals.
Motives and Ideals. The availability of so much inexpensive land 
attracted all kinds of settlers, squatters, and speculators—­with var-
ious combinations of economic and moral motives. Quakers and 
abolitionists from the southern states, as well as some Virginia 
gentry like Thomas Worthington, wanted to leave slavery behind. 
Commerce-­oriented farmers and craftsmen were attracted out of 
the former colonies for a new start on fertile and abundant lands, 
as were recent arrivals on this continent escaping war and hard-
ship in Europe. Settlements grew rapidly among the newly opened 
fields, and rudimentary industries were founded, first those in sup-
port of agriculture such as tanning, meat and produce processing, 
and equipment manufacturing. On February 19, 1803, an act of 
Congress made Ohio the seventeenth state in the Union, with the 
new town of Chillicothe as its first capital.
Indian Removal. Despite the wars, treaties, and expulsions through 
the 1790s, many American Indians remained in Ohio. Only after Con-
gress passed the Indian Removal Act of 1830 did the last tribal rem-
nants leave the state—­by force, in their own “Trail of Tears”—­headed 
for designated “Indian Territories” west of the Mississippi (Kansas and 
Oklahoma at the time). The act was controversial, supported mainly 
by southern states like Georgia (which was in a bitter conflict with the 
Cherokee) and then-­president Andrew Jackson. It passed the House 
of Representatives by the thinnest of margins, with many Northern-
ers of the Whig Party—­among them Ohio’s congressional delegation—­
opposing it. Nonetheless, in what the world would now recognize as 
genocide or ethnic cleansing, American Indian tribes were forcibly 
removed to Oklahoma or other points west by the 1840s, under very 
cruel conditions and with massive losses of life along the way.
Regions and Architecture. Ohio’s early settlement histories have left 
distinct cultural and architectural identities in different regions of 
the state: Marietta and southeast Ohio from its New Englanders, 
and Chillicothe, Cincinnati, and southwest Ohio from its Virgin-
ians. (Northeast Ohio was an outpost of Connecticut.) Lancaster 
and Somerset’s distinctive town squares mark a Pennsylvania influ-
ence across the east-­central part of the state, as do the large bank 
barns—­built into slopes for ground entry on two levels. The earli-
est surviving house type, of course, is the log cabin; many survive, 
though some are now siding-­clad but still recognizable for their 
proportions. More common are the 1800s I-­houses (three to five 
TECUMSEH
The great Shawnee leader was present at the Battle of Fallen Timbers 
and, after watching his older brother die in the fighting, resolved to form 
a new Native confederacy to continue the struggle. He had already gained a 
reputation for leadership in unifying tribal resistance and for bravery in 
battles against the US Army in southern Ohio. After their defeat at Fallen 
Timbers, most other tribal leaders thought their only path to peace was 
to sign the Treaty of Greenville, giving up their southern Ohio lands. But 
Tecumseh refused. With his brother Tenskwatawa, called “The Prophet,” he 
worked  heroically in the early 1800s to persuade the region’s tribes to unite 
and push the settlers back across the Appalachians. But in late 1811, troops 
led by William Henry Harrison destroyed Tecumseh’s base at Prophetstown, 
Indiana, and with it his dream of a united Native resistance. Tecumseh then 
joined English forces in the War of 1812, still trying to turn back the tide of 
settlement, but was killed in the 1813 Battle of the Thames in southern Ontario.

and one room deep), also visi-
ble in old town centers and out 
along the older routes. In larger 
towns like Chillicothe and Lan-

style of choice for wealthy res-
idents during the height of the 
canal era from the 1820s to ’50s.
Canals and Industries. The ex-
plosion of town populations and commerce got a huge boost after 
1820 with the construction of two canal routes linking the Ohio 
River with Lake Erie—­from where, using New York state’s new Erie 
Canal, Ohio products could much more easily reach coastal mar-
kets. The Miami and Erie Canal connected Cincinnati, via Ham-
ilton and Dayton, up the western part of the state to Toledo. The 
Ohio and Erie Canal connected Portsmouth, via Chillicothe and 
Circleville, to Newark and on to Cleveland. By mid-­century, new 
industries developed, especially iron production in the Hanging 
Rock region of southeast Ohio. Plentiful resources there included 
limestone, coal, and timber for the furnaces. Places named “fur-
nace” dot the region today, notably the restored Buckeye Furnace. 
Ohio’s once-­robust iron industry fed the Union army’s supply 
chains during the Civil War but then gave way to larger operations 
elsewhere. Also by that time, railroads were making the canals ob-
solete, and they were finally decommissioned around 1900.
EARTHWORK ENCOUNTERS
Wonderment and Destruction. Meanwhile, during the tumultuous cen-
tury of war and settlement from 1750 to 1850, the newcomers were 
coming upon the huge, forest-­covered earthworks—­long earthen 
lines, parallel walls, perfect circles and squares, and rimmed hill-
tops. Since the sites’ final use for Hopewell ceremonies more than a 
thousand years before, the combination of erosion and forest depo-
sition had maintained their forms nearly intact. Though many of 
the early settlers were impressed, and a few, like Circleville’s Caleb 
Atwater, set out to study them and advocate for their preservation, 
the rapid growth of towns and the clearing and plowing of agricul-
tural fields began to take a toll. Many of the most magnificent earth-
works were gone by the 1820s. They often stood in ideal spots for 
cities—­high terraces with good river access (like Marietta, Ports-
mouth, or Cincinnati)—­or occupied exceptionally rich farmland. 
Innumerable mounds were also cut down by treasure hunters.
Myths of the Moundbuilders. Tragically, all this destruction co- 
incided with the decades of warfare against the Native inhabitants, and 
their forced Removal. Many of them had regarded the earthworks as 
sacred ancestral places, which they were now forced to abandon. They 
couldn’t (or didn’t) offer any clear explanations of who had built them 
and when, so the newcomers were quick to interpret them according to 
their own ideas. Many were named “forts” for their resemblance (later 
disproven) to defensive walls and moats. More importantly, neither 
the settlers nor most scholars at the time could imagine that they were 
built by Ancestors of the tribal groups in the area. Racism and igno-
rance led many Euro-­Americans to concoct bizarre theories that they 
were built by a long-­lost and unrelated race of “Moundbuilders”—­or 
maybe transplanted Egyptians, or Israelites, or a Welsh tribe, or the  
Vikings. This confusion persisted until the early 1900s, and it even 
continues in some quarters today, as when they are attributed to 
space aliens.
Early Appreciation. The founders of the United States knew and 
admired the earthworks in the Ohio Country. George Washington 
and Thomas Jefferson both argued for their preservation. Jeffer-
son’s Secretary of the Treasury, Albert Gallatin, was an especially 
eager scholar of ancient America, and one of the few at the time 
who rightly attributed them to Native Americans; he expressed 
disgust for people who belittled the achievements of the region’s 
Indigenous peoples. The first systematic description of the major 
earthwork complexes was by Caleb Atwater, who had been ap-
pointed by Jefferson as the postmaster at Circleville—­a town 
which at the time still stood inside its perfect, double-­ringed, 
namesake earthwork. His study, with accompanying plans, was 
published in 1820 by the American Antiquarian Society and re-
ceived praise from Jefferson.
Cincinnati’s Harriet Beecher Stowe House, 
completed in 1833, is a three-­bay example of 
the century’s common I-­house type.

The Region and Its History
The Region and Its History
Beginnings of Archaeology. Public interest in the earthworks was 
at its peak by the mid-­1800s, when two citizens of Chillicothe, 
Ephraim G. Squier and Edwin H. Davis set out to survey the earth-
works of the entire Mississippi and Ohio river system. With support 
from Gallatin, their work, Ancient Monuments of the Mississippi Val-
ley, became the first publication of the new Smithsonian Institution 
in 1848. Squier and Davis assembled their magisterial volume with 
maps, plates, and descriptive detail amassed from many skilled 
collaborators throughout the state—­still among the best records we 
have for many sites now destroyed. Squier was a newspaper editor, 
and Davis was a physician. The latter profession seems to have lent 
itself to amateur archaeological interests: Charles Metz and Daniel 
Drake, who did pioneering work in the Little Miami Valley and Cin-
cinnati, respectively, were also physicians, as was James Salisbury, 
who with his brother Charles carried out the most accurate survey 
of the Newark Earthworks around 1860.
Scientific Archaeology. Modern archaeological methods began in 
Ohio with Frederic Ward Putnam’s work in the 1880s and ’90s, at 
sites in the southwestern part of the state—­notably Serpent Mound, 
which he helped save from destruction, and the Turner and Foster 
sites along the Little Miami. A professor at Harvard University, Put-
nam is now generally known as the “father” of American archae-
ology. He collaborated with a local enthusiast named Warren King 
Moorehead, who investigated thoroughly at Fort Ancient and suc-
cessfully lobbied the Ohio legislature for its preservation. Moore-
head also worked (for Putnam) at the Hopewell Mound Group, 
where his crew found the huge quantities of culture-­defining art-
istry that astonished visitors to the 1893 World’s Columbian Ex-
position in Chicago. Well into the 1900s, more systematic mound 
excavations, especially at the Seip Earthworks and Mound City, 
yielded more and better knowledge about the culture—­but also de-
stroyed the mounds, only a few of which were restored afterward. 
Sadly, this work also destroyed the Ancestors’ resting places.
STEWARDSHIP AND SIGNIFICANCE
Management and Research. The Ohio History Connection, the Na-
tional Park Service, and other park systems and agencies manage 
the major sites today, so their preservation, access, and skilled 
maintenance are assured. Yet many other sites are still in private 
hands, threatened by modern development (and not mentioned 
in this book). Meanwhile, two new focus areas are transforming 
twenty-­first-­century archaeology. One is remote sensing technol-
ogy such as magnetometry that can show certain features under 
the ground without digging—­for example, precise earthwork out-
lines or the most promising locations for targeted excavation. The 
other is the search for often-­elusive domestic areas in the spaces 
outside the ceremonial earthworks, where more evidence of the 
people’s everyday lives, social relationships, population levels, and 
food ways might be found.
American Indian Involvement. 
Since the 1990 passage of the Na-
tive American Graves Protection 
and Repatriation Act (NAGPRA), 
site managers and interpreters 
have been expanding their col-
laborations with tribal leaders 
and Indigenous scholars. The 
preparations for World Heritage 
inscription (beginning in 2008), 
and most efforts since, have seen 
significant Indigenous participa-
tion. The sites’ management agencies, university centers and depart-
ments, the state’s government, and others now collaborate through 
many programs and opportunities with Ohio’s long-­displaced Tribal 
Nations as they reconnect with their Ohio homelands. Such renewed 
Indigenous leadership in telling their histories and interpreting their 
cultures is showcased well, for example, at the Great Council State 
Park.
The Question of Artifacts. Since 2022, artifacts uncovered by a cen-
tury and a half of excavations have been withdrawn from view 
in most museums because according to many Indigenous beliefs 
they are regarded as sacred—­even powerful beings—­buried by the 
Ancestors for a reason and not meant to be seen again. Many of 
Jarrod Burks, of the Heartland Earth-

these pieces have become iconic symbols of the Hopewell culture—­
especially the animal figure platform pipes and the mica hand. But 
out of respect for the ongoing consultation process on this ques-
tion, no images of ancient objects appear in this book, except in 
artistic abstractions which are not generally considered to capture 
their power.
World Heritage Criteria. The 2023 World Heritage inscription of 
the eight Hopewell Ceremonial Earthworks is based on what  
UNESCO calls their “Outstanding Universal Value” to all of human-
ity. This group of sites meets two criteria specified by the World Her-
itage program: They give testimony to a distinctive culture (criterion 
iii), and to their builders’ creative genius (criterion i). The unique 
culture story here is that a society dispersed among small settle-
ments and organized without rigid hierarchies exerted continent-­
wide influence and created such monumental public works. The 
unique genius story here is that these works exhibit an astonishing 
scale, precision, and detail in their geometric layouts, and in their  
multiple alignments to the Moon’s positions during its complex 
18.6-­year cycle.
Earth, Art, and Culture. Across global cultures, earthworks are a 
unique and profound form of architecture. Forming the Earth into 
an ordered, sculpted artifice, usually at a huge scale, always evokes 
powerful ideas. Consider the great gardens of Renaissance Eu-
rope or Imperial China—­or our own massive interstate highway 
embankments. Each rearrangement of the earth’s surface reveals 
profoundly what a human society believes and values. Experienc-
ing Ohio’s earthworks can help remind us how designed landscapes 
convey a unique aesthetic and poetic power—­both for their huge 
scale and for their use of the Earth itself as a material. Artists like 
Robert Smithson and Michael Heizer, or landscape architects like 
George Hargreaves (who designed the University of Cincinnati’s 
earthwork-­laced campus) understand fully that artistry with the 
Earth has a special kind of beauty and significance.
Find that beauty and significance among the hills and valleys of 
southern Ohio, while touring the monuments of one of the world’s 
most brilliant Indigenous civilizations.
RETURNING TO THE MOUNDS
Across the thirteen moons on Turtle’s back, across multiples of centuries, the 
Mother Mounds are calling to their children: “Come home . . .” They call, sing, 
coo, echo, hum. They infiltrate our sleeping and our waking dreams with 
whispers of thoughts they make as if our very own: “Come home . . . it’s time. 
Come home to rest your hearts in the layered folds of Mother’s skirts.”
—­Monique Mojica, Founding Member of Native Earth Performing
Arts, Toronto (Guna and Rappahannock Nations, Canada)
At the University of Cincinnati, students walk among a series of winding earthworks and 
massive mounds designed in the 1990s by landscape architect George Hargreaves.

World Heritage Route
West
The World Heritage Route connects Ohio’s most important ancient 
earthwork sites inscribed on the UNESCO World Heritage List in 
2023. Anchored by several historic towns, it links to seven branch 
routes for exploring the rest of the region. The western half pres-
ents a cross section of Ohio’s geological history, while the eastern 
half approximates an ancient roadway that connected Chillicothe 
and Newark—­the Hopewell culture’s two richest ceremonial 
centers.
LEBANON
With several historic districts, Ohio’s oldest inn, a fine museum, and 
a vibrant downtown, Lebanon makes a pleasant and convenient 
hub for exploring the Miami Valley region and southwest Ohio.
Historic Center. Downtown Lebanon is anchored by the legendary 
Golden Lamb Inn (27 South Broadway St.), Ohio’s oldest continu-
ously operating business. Its eighteen guest rooms tell many stories, 
having hosted several US pres-
idents and other distinguished 
guests. 
Along 
well-­preserved, 
tree-­lined downtown streets are 
many cafés, antique and spe-
cialty shops, and other amenities, 
and the adjacent historic neigh-
borhoods 
present 
beautifully 
restored houses from various pe-
riods. On weekends, the Lebanon 
Mason Monroe Railroad offers 
scenic tours embarking from the 
nearby station. For information 
on accommodations and other 
unique points of interest in Lebanon and Warren County, visit

Largest Playground.
Harmon Museum. Across from the Golden Lamb stands the large and 
impressive museum of the Warren County Historical Society (105 
South Broadway St.), housed in the historic Harmon Hall and a large 
addition. Exhibits tell engaging stories of this area’s antiquity, early 
history, and national importance. The museum’s collection of Shaker 
archives and artifacts is the finest in the Midwest (one of the sect’s

textile and Indigenous artifact collections are also quite extraordinary. 
The facility encompasses four art galleries, a research library, and a 
recreated town square with period commerce, including an intriguing

FORT ANCIENT ACCESS ROUTES
Two I-­71 interchanges near Lebanon are signposted for Fort Ancient. Exit 32 leads to the 
more scenic approach, via State Route 123 from the west, although a steep hairpin turn 
into the Little Miami gorge makes it impassable for large vehicles or trailers. The route 
from Exit 36 approaches instead from the north and east on level ground. Both enter 
the site on State Route 350 through impressive gateway openings in the earthen wall.  
The latter also passes between Fort Ancient’s Twin Mounds.
FORT ANCIENT EARTHWORKS
Set high above a narrow gorge of the Little Miami River, the most 
spectacular and well preserved of the Hopewell-­era hilltop enclo-
sures encircles over 100 acres with its earthen walls and ponds.
Orientation. The earthen walls, the museum, and the site entrance 
are all prominent from the highway entrance (6123 SR 350, Oregonia). 
Opening times vary seasonally and admission is charged; details are 
on the Ohio History Connection website. A walking tour of the site’s 
most outstanding features will take two to four hours. In its forested 
setting, the undulating earthen forms present themselves best from 
October through April and preferably in sunlight. The enclosure has 
three parts: the roughly triangular South Fort, the narrow isthmus of 
the Middle Fort, and the larger North Fort where the museum and 
parking lot are located. Except to the northeast, the walls follow the 
rims of steep, wooded hillsides, accompanied by constructed ponds 
along their interiors. The walls are most visible in the Middle Fort, 
and most spectacular at the site’s three major gateways—­especially 
the remote South Gate.
History. The Hopewell-­era walls were built in two or three phases, 
generally from south to north, and then largely disused until a later 
Indigenous culture (from about 1150) built a substantial village in the 
South Fort. Around 1800, the first settlers began turning Fort Ancient 
into a tourist attraction and brought the new Lebanon-­Chillicothe 
Turnpike (now State Route 350) through the site. A Philadelphia peri-
odical published a description in 1809, and it appeared again in Caleb 
Atwater’s 1820 report. Later in the century, a railway stop and hotel 
were built at the bottom of the hill, beside the river. The preserva-
tion of Fort Ancient was assured in 1891, after archaeologist Warren 
King Moorehead convinced the Ohio legislature to make it a state 
memorial. But it has seen many uses. For example, troops in training 
for World War I practiced “storming the walls”—­probably the near-
est it ever came to being a real fort. Although the astonished early 
settlers could only assume it was built for defense, we now recog-
nize that no effective fortification ever had eighty-­four openings or 
its moat on the inside. Like the other Hopewell hilltop enclosures, 
it was a place of special ceremony, its elevated position perhaps con-
necting it with the Sky World.
Several of Fort Ancient’s most dramatic wall formations are visible in the narrow Middle 
Fort, where the gateways between them open to steep, plunging ravines.

World Heritage Route: WEST
1 Museum and Garden. The Ohio History Connection’s museum, 
near the parking area, offers site orientation, a small shop,  
restrooms, and meeting spaces. The extensive exhibits present the 
history of American Indians in Ohio from their earliest postglacial 
arrival; through the Adena, Hopewell, and Fort Ancient cultures; 
to nineteenth-­century Euro-­American settlement and the resulting 
conflicts, including the tragedy of Indian Removal. A fenced gar-
den and reconstructed Hopewell-­era house help visitors envision 
everyday life for the earthwork builders. Its design is based on one 
of several posthole patterns found during the 1997 museum expan-
sion; those houses predated the enclosing of the North Fort. Re-
mains of houses and craft workshops indicate that people also lived 
farther out to the northeast.
2 North Gate and Twin Mounds. From the parking lot, a paved lane 
passes through the twenty-­three-­foot-­high eastern walls of the 
North Fort. This is the only part of the perimeter where the sur-
rounding terrain is generally level, and the only place where the 
builders created their water features outside the walls rather than 
on the interior. Out here, the large Twin Mounds frame the ancient 
approach to the North Gate; today it seems natural to assume that 
was the highway opening, but in antiquity the axis defined by these 
The identical Twin Mounds stand outside the North Fort and frame the modern highway 
approaching from the east.

30
31
World Heritage Route: WEST
World Heritage Route: WEST
mounds directed people instead to the first opening south of the 
road. The Twin Mounds are spatial markers, not burial mounds, 
and are a variation on the earthwork’s two older major gateways 
which are formed by mound-­like rises atop the walls themselves.
Parallel Walls. Beyond the Twin Mounds, parallel walls once 
headed out to the northeast for about a quarter of a mile. Culti-
vation had left them difficult to trace by the 1840s. They followed 
the highest elevation of the plateau, between the headwaters of the 
two streams, to where a small mound once stood just beyond Mid-
dleboro Road. In an area near the walls, buried piles of obsidian, 
flint, and copper suggest that artisans were crafting objects for use 
in the ceremonies. Archaeologist Patricia Essenpreis discovered in 
1990 that from each of the Twin Mounds, the people also dug deep 
trenches out toward the nearby streams. These trenches carried 
spring water down to those streams which, by flowing into the river 
below, completed a watery perimeter for the entire site.
3 Foursquare Mounds. Inside the North Fort, the level space is 
punctuated by four low, limestone-­covered mounds. They form 
a square averaging 520 feet on each side—­about one-­half of an 
often-­used Hopewell dimension of 1,054 feet (called the “OCD,” and  
explained on pages 95–96). One mound stands near the parking 
lot, another just inside the North Gate, and another across the  
highway. Completing the square is a final mound near the site 
caretaker’s house. The stone in their original coverings showed  
evidence of burning—­it was oxidized, red, and crumbly, and its  
fossils had turned white. Apparently, huge fires burned dramati-
cally atop these four stone-­surfaced mounds. The square space they  
define seems to have been kept clean and free of buildings, suggest-
ing it was sacred. The current stone caps on the mounds protect the 
originals beneath.
Alignments. Two of the square’s mounds are backsights for astronom-
ical alignments. The northwest one aligns through one of the eastern 
gateways with the winter solstice sunrise. From the southwest mound, 
alignments through three gateways align with the northernmost ris-
ing positions of the Sun (summer solstice) and Moon (both its min-
imum and maximum; see page 92). Trees would have been cleared 
to allow open views from the mound to these three key astronomical 
alignments, through the gateways 
and out to the northeastern horizon. 
Today, the solstice gateway opening 
(just north of the highway) and the 
woods beyond it are kept clear to 
allow for special sunrise-­watching 
celebrations held here every June 21.
4 Moorehead Circle. The biggest discovery at Fort Ancient in recent 
memory is the evidence of a giant, ceremonial woodhenge (ring of 
timber poles) in the North Fort, now called the Moorehead Circle. 
A magnetometry survey first brought it to light in 2005, and investi-
gations followed over several summers by teams from Wright State 
University led by archaeologist Bob Riordan. Rings of posts about 200 
feet in diameter encircled an area which had been partly built up and 
leveled (toward the ravine to the north); fitted with alternating strips 
of stone and gravel; and centered on a large deposit of bright, red-­
orange, burned soil brought in from elsewhere. This red central fea-
ture aligns across the nearest mound of the Foursquare to the gateway 
marking the summer solstice sunrise. Despite its many unanswered 
questions, the Moorehead Circle has confirmed that the earthwork’s 
interior was the setting for elaborate ritual facilities.
5 Middle Fort. To build the walls, people scraped up much of the 
topsoil from the level interior of the North Fort. Today, standing 
water is common and mowing is difficult during wet seasons. Mov-
ing down the road along Fort Ancient’s spine, the walls begin to 
close in on both sides, transitioning to the narrow Middle Fort, 
where their undulating beauty and variety become more evident. 
On the left are three flush-­laid stone circles, just before the road 
passes between two crescent mounds. Nearby, also on the left, are 
some of the site’s most dramatic ponds. The archaeologist Bob Con-
nolly found evidence that the isthmus—­from the crescents to the 
Great Gateway—­was substantially constructed to connect the two 
large plateaus. They filled in three gullies to accomplish this, using 
an amount of soil equal to all of the site’s earthen walls combined.
In the early 2000s, archaeologists uncovered a 
startling deposit of bright, red-­orange, sterile soil 
at the center of the Moorehead Circle.

World Heritage Route
World Heritage Route: WEST
Construction. To create Fort Ancient, generations of people must 
have worked consistently toward a common goal—­a shared concep-
tion of the site’s design, purpose, and meaning. The first step would 
have been clearing the forests—­by itself a huge undertaking with 
the techniques and tools available (girdling, fire, stone axes). They 
scraped soil off the surface and moved it, one basket load at a time, 
to fill ravines and form the eighty-­four walls and gateways. They 
dug 131 ponds and lined them with clay and stone. They carried vast 
amounts of limestone up from the valley to reinforce the walls and 
gateways, to create stone rings and circles, and to build long runs of 
pavement. They also covered much of the outside wall surfaces with 
stone; when freshly built it may have gleamed in the sunlight.
6 Ponds. Fort Ancient was designed not only with Earth but with 
water. The walls can effectively enclose the space, but the builders 
also worked very hard to make water part of this boundary. When 
filled with rainwater or snowmelt, clay-­lined ditches became a nearly 
continuous necklace of reflective ponds—­perhaps to evoke views 
or entries into the watery Beneath World known from Indigenous 
traditions. Several still function well, presenting the constructed 
earthworks as if they are floating on the water. Even when dry, their 
positions and scope are apparent. (The largest of Fort Ancient’s sur-
viving ponds is in the northwest corner, but is not easily reached.)
7 Great Gateway. The most elaborate of the site’s three major pas-
sages is called the Great Gateway. Approaching from the Middle 
Fort, the road rises across the narrow constructed isthmus to the 
original north entrance to the South Fort, the oldest part of Fort 
Ancient. Beneath the ramp descending into the South Fort’s inte-
rior, archaeologist Warren King Moorehead found many ancient 
graves. Though this would seem disrespectful, Pokagon Band of 
Potawatomi citizen John Low tells of “a common saying I have 
Beautiful crescent mounds 
help form the passage 
through the Middle Fort,  
now framing the modern 
road as it approaches the 
Great Gateway.
Despite the 1930s efforts to drain them, many of Fort Ancient’s ponds still hold water in wet 
seasons, most visibly along the eastern side of the Middle Fort.
THE WATERY BENEATH WORLD
In wet seasons, when Fort Ancient’s ponds fill with water, they become 
reflecting pools, presenting a beautiful unity of the three levels of the 
Universe as it is understood in much of Woodland Indigenous cosmology. 
As we look down into the water, we see reflected the earthwork and the Sky 
beyond. There, all at once, are the Sky World, the home of the Sun and the 
Thunderbirds; the Earth World, brought into being for us to live on; and the 
watery Beneath World, from which the Earth and all creation were brought 
forth, and which is presided over by the Great Serpent and the Underwater 
Panther. The earthwork appears to rise from the water or to float on it, as 
in the many tribal versions of the Earth Diver creation story in which an 
animal brings up a bit of mud from beneath the water which then grows to 
become the habitable Earth.

heard all my life, that ‘We walk on the bones of our Ancestors’ to 
solemnize our connections to the ones who came before us, as well 
as to demonstrate our intimate connection to the land.” Just inside 
the Great Gateway, a small stone mound stands on the right; nearby 
was a long stretch of stone pavement (no longer visible). The mod-
ern picnic area now occupies the northeastern part of the South 
Fort, with a stone and timber picnic shelter built by Civilian Con-
servation Corps crews in the 1930s.
8 Village Site. The western arm of the South Fort was the primary 
occupation area for later societies who lived here. At least off and 
on between 1100 and 1550, a village stood here with a wooden 
stockade around it. These later people also had settlements down 
in the valley, but they had gone before the first Euro-­American set-
tlers arrived. According to the archaeologists’ tradition of naming a  
distinctive culture after the place where they first identify its key 
features, these later populations have been dubbed the “Fort An-
cient” culture—­to no end of confusion, as they did not build the 
Fort Ancient earthworks. They lived quite differently from the 
Hopewell-­era earthwork builders, gathering in substantial set-
tlements like SunWatch Village (see page 114), and growing large 
quantities of corn.
9 North Overlook. From the picnic area, follow the road to the 
right to reach the North Overlook, where a wooden platform atop 
the walls offers views of the Little Miami River and its narrow 
gorge. The vista highlights the special prominence of this location, 
and the importance of creating an elevated ceremonial place with 
such a strong visual tie to the river below. From here, a path with 
many stairs leads all the way down to the river and the Little Miami  
Scenic Trail alongside it today. Or, the Terrace Trail descends part-
way down the slope to a series of large, ancient cuts in the hillside 
(and abundant April wildflowers), then continues around to the 
South Overlook.
⓾ South Overlook. Another perimeter trail, the Earthworks Trail, 
leaves the North Overlook to follow the interior of the South Fort’s 
western and southern enclosure walls, passing down through two of 
the site’s deepest ravines. The ancient walls were built across these 
ravines, creating what must have been Fort Ancient’s largest and 
deepest ponds, albeit more prone to erosion. Returning to the large 
clearing, and along the southern wall, another wooden platform—­
the South Overlook—­offers more views of the forested valley.
⓫ South Gate. Near the South Overlook, a trail heads east into the 
woods to Fort Ancient’s most spectacular feature, the grand South 
Gate. Like the site’s two other major gateways, a pair of mounds 
define an entrance passage. Like the Great Gateway, they stand 
atop the wall, though here on a more massive scale. From the in-
terior, a ramp passes between what were once ponds, and up to 
the notch between the mounds. This is the site’s original monu-
mental threshold, and suggests an elaborate ritual passage: After a 
long journey, celebrants arrived on the river by canoe, climbed the 
stone path up the steep hillside, and entered the enclosure—­over

EN ROUTE TO THE PAINT VALLEY
From Fort Ancient to the Appalachian foothills, State Route 350 crosses a broad stretch 
of gently rolling farmland with few earthwork remains. This area was in between the 
major Ohio Hopewell regions of the Miami Valley (upstream from Cincinnati) and the 
Scioto River–­Paint Creek confluence around Chillicothe. Early settlers’ villages like 
Clarksville and Leesburg were founded soon after the American Revolution by Quakers 
from the South who objected to the continuation of slavery.
Virginia Military District. The lands between the Little Miami and the 
Scioto Rivers were Shawnee territory when settlement began. And 
yet they were surveyed, divided into parcels, and given by the new 
United States government to veterans of the Revolutionary War. 
The land was offered in lieu of cash payments, which the fledgling 
federal treasury could not afford. Surveyors used the traditional 
Virginia method of “metes and bounds” which set boundaries ac-
cording to various natural features (in contrast to the grid method 
used by the founders of Marietta). Today’s roadmaps display the 
peculiar geometries that resulted. Many of the town and county 
names also reflect the new arrivals’ Virginia roots. As the settler 
economy grew, many recipients of these original parcels became 
quite wealthy.
New Vienna. This small town clusters around the angular meeting 
point of three of those irregular early roads and retains a good 
number of historic houses. The town was laid out in 1827 and first 
called “Buzzard’s Glory” after the birds who flocked to the drying 
hides at a local tannery—­one of the essential early industries in 
most of these fast-­growing, early nineteenth-­century agricultural 
settlements. Just east and south of New Vienna is the high, glacial-­
till-­covered divide where waters flow either west into the Little 
Miami watershed, or east into the hillier terrain of the Paint and 
Scioto country.
TO THE BRUSH CREEK ROUTE
State Route 73 heads south out of New Vienna to Hillsboro, with some fine historic 
buildings, then on to the Great Serpent Mound in northern Adams County. Farther 
south are several biodiverse nature preserves. Return to the World Heritage Route via 
Sinking Spring, the beautifully intact hilltop enclosure of Fort Hill, and the Highlands 
Nature Sanctuary near Bainbridge (see map, page 133).
Greenfield. Founded on the banks of Paint Creek in 1799, Greenfield’s 
name evokes the beautiful rural countryside, where the larger hills 
foreshadowing the Appalachian Plateau just begin to rise. Many of 
the early settlers here and in nearby towns were motivated by their 
opposition to slavery, and the town became an important stop on the 
Underground Railroad in the early 1800s. A century later, it was a place 
of industrial innovation—­including the first African American-­owned 
vehicle manufacturer, the C.R. Patterson and Sons company. Green-
field’s first stone building was the 1812 Travellers Rest Inn, which also 
served as the town’s first post office. Relocated from its original site, it 
is now a museum and headquarters of the local historical society (set 
back from the corner of Jefferson 
Street and South McArthur Way).
ANCIENT ARTISTRY
Walking inside the Fort Ancient and Newark earthworks . . . one is confounded 
by a multiplicity of sensations: that here are the greatest works of art on the 
American continent . . . ; that here in the seductive Ohio Valley are perhaps 
the greatest art monuments in the world.
—­Barnett Newman, American abstract expressionist painter
(1905–­1970)
Greenfield’s 1812 Travellers Rest Inn 
is now a house museum overlooking 
a cemetery and Paint Creek.

PAINT CREEK STATE PARK
After Greenfield, the route enters increasingly scenic terrain en route to Bainbridge, 
either via State Route 41, or by taking Rapid Forge Road out of town and skirting the 
edge of Paint Creek State Park, which offers a wildlife area, clear streams, mature 
woodlands, hiking and biking trails, and a 1,148-­acre lake with recreational facilities 
and camping. From near US Route 50, Falls Road follows directly along the river into 
Bainbridge.
Bainbridge. Virginia surveyor Nathaniel Massie founded Bain-
bridge in 1805, after establishing Chillicothe nine years earlier. 
Massie helped to attract many of the area’s early settlers and be-
came a wealthy landowner and political figure during the early 
years of Ohio statehood. Bainbridge may be best known today as 
“the cradle of American dentistry,” since a local dentist opened the 
first dental school in the US here in 1825. At the Dr. John Harris 
Dental Museum, on West Main Street, the often frightening imple-
ments of tooth extraction (and other things for which dentists were 
then relied upon) are displayed.
The Paint Valley. Bainbridge marks the western end of the beau-
tiful Paint Valley, its rich, secluded farmland fully enclosed by 
high, wooded hillsides and a few striking cliffs. In antiquity, this 
valley held an especially dense concentration of geometric earth-
works, including Seip (pronounced SIPE) and Baum, with Spruce 
Hill commanding views high above its eastern end. Nearby along 
Paint Creek’s North Fork lies the spectacular Hopewell Mound 
Group (reached via Maple Grove Road). At the valley’s eastern end, 
Canoe and kayak liveries along Paint Creek  
offer visitors an opportunity to travel  
this “Valley of the Ancients” by water as  
the earthwork builders did.
SHAWNEE LAND
At the onset of Euro-­American settlement, much of southern and south-
western Ohio was the home of the Shawnee Tribe. When the settlers began 
carving new trails through the forests and establishing their farms and 
villages, the land belonged to Indigenous people who had arrived here 
at least two centuries before—­already driven from their homes farther 
east and south by colonial expansion. The ensuing conflicts, in which the 
Shawnee, Miami, and other tribes were fighting not only for their lands, 
but for their entire way of life, ultimately ended with their forced Removal 
to Oklahoma and other states by the 1840s. Local lore has it that the Paint 
Valley’s red ocher had been used by the Indigenous people for their 
symbolic body and face markings, and that the settlers found it to be a 
substitute for paint and used it on their barns and other buildings. Either 
may help explain the origins of the stream’s name.
SHAWNEE REMOVAL
Chief Glenna Wallace of the Eastern Shawnee Tribe 
of Oklahoma tells the tragic story of her people’s 
forced Removal from Ohio: We, my people, walked 
on foot. They refused to go by river craft. They 
were afraid of the river craft. They were afraid that 
diseased blankets might be given to them. They 
were afraid the river craft might catch on fire. And 
so they said, “We will leave Ohio exactly as we came to Ohio.” And that was on 
foot, and that was on horseback, and of course there would have been some 
wagons. And that’s a journey of about 700 miles. About 30 percent of our 
people died along the way. Each tribe has its own Trail of Tears, even though 
that term may not have been named until later and is usually associated 
with the Cherokee. And that trail of tears didn’t necessarily end whenever 
we arrived at the place where we were supposed to go, because just before 
1900, my tribe was down to the number of sixty-­nine people. So, it truly was 
an example of almost total genocide. (Link to video is on page 205.)
