History. A community of perhaps 200 people used this earthwork 
enclosure over several generations as a civic and ceremonial cen-
ter. Over time they erected two great three-­chambered halls, sev-
eral specialized buildings, and the huge Earth-­walled enclosure. 
The three-­part geometric layout suggests affinities among the com-
munities at Seip and at four other similar geometric earthworks 
nearby. After about 400 CE, the forest reclaimed the site. After set-
tlement, the land was farmed for generations before being studied 
or preserved. Through a combination of bequests and purchases, 
the central portions of the earthworks came under the control of 
the Ohio History Connection, which led excavations of the two 
large mounds in the early 1900s and faithfully restored the larger 
one. The remaining portions joined Hopewell Culture National 
Historical Park when it was founded in 1992.

The creek—­really a small river—­is popular with paddlers, meander-
ing through the valley before entering an otherwise inaccessible 
canyon on its way to joining the Scioto River below Chillicothe.
SEIP EARTHWORKS.
Three huge, perfect geometric figures combine to enclose eighty-­
nine acres—­one of five earthwork complexes built in Ross County 
using the same shapes. Two large burial mounds at Seip contained 
some of the Hopewell culture’s finest ceremonial artistry.
Orientation. Three miles east of Bainbridge along US Route 50 (7058 
US 50 Scenic, Bainbridge), a cluster of large trees marks a small 
parking area for the earthworks, now a unit of Hopewell Culture  
National Historical Park. The site is open during daylight hours with 
no admission charge, and visibility is good any time of day or year. 
The scene is dominated by the large restored mound in its serene 
valley setting, approached from the parking area through a peace-
ful grove of trees. Restored walls of the large circle frame a small 
gateway; visible traces of the wall extend toward the small brick 
house to the left. The National Park Service maintains a planting 
program to help visitors trace the remainder of the huge geometric 
The Seip Earthworks are centered on the large Seip-­Pricer Mound, seen here from a restored 
section of the large circle’s northern rim.

World Heritage Route: WEST
World Heritage Route: WEST
3 Craft Buildings. In the 1960s and 1970s, Ohio History Connection 
archaeologists Raymond Baby (pronounced BAH-­bee) and Martha 
Otto conducted one of the first non-­mound investigations at any 
Hopewell earthwork. They uncovered the floors and posthole pat-
terns of a complex of buildings north of the Seip-­Pricer Mound. 
These were not dwellings or ceremonial spaces, but special pur-
pose buildings, possibly craft workshops. Markings on the site 
today outline their floor plans, which echo the distinctive Hopewell 
building shape found at other sites—­rectangles with rounded cor-
ners and double rows of poles along the longer sides.
The posthole pattern found beneath the Seip-­Pricer Mound was similar to this one  
uncovered in the 1970s at the Liberty Earthworks south of Chillicothe.
1 Thomas Blackstone House. From the parking area, the immensity 
of the three-­part geometric enclosure is not yet apparent, though 
the large Seip-­Pricer Mound is visible in the distance. Several de-
cades of farming had begun to degrade the walls before Thomas 
Blackstone built a small brick house in the 1850s. It sits directly 
on a portion of the large circle and served for a time as a school. 
This structure and its two outbuildings remind us that from the 
earliest decades of settlement, most newcomers thought of these 
little-­understood mound and earthwork complexes as merely farm-
land—­to be cleared of trees, plowed, and planted, year after year.
2 Large Circle Gateway. Seip’s three major geometric shapes can 
begin to be envisioned from a small opening in the northern rim of 
the larger circle. The smaller circle is to the right, and the square is 
in the far-­left distance behind the large mound. Analysis of LiDAR 
(light detection and ranging) and magnetometry images show the 
square’s sides to approximate the Hopewell standard dimension  
of 1,054 feet. The large circle’s diameter is 1.5 times this length. 
The center points of the three large figures are connected by an 
obtuse angle that is exactly bisected by the center line of the major 
northeastern reentrant gateway (indicated on the site map shown 
on page 41). All this points to the sophistication of the builders’ con-
trol over geometric construction at a huge scale and at distances 
separated by many miles.
Tripartite Earthworks. Seip’s three large shapes very closely match 
those built at four other earthworks within twenty miles. Huge, 
precise circles and squares were used throughout the Hopewell 
world, but this set shows the ancient architects at their most ad-
vanced. Though laid out in various arrangements, all included 
sixteen-­acre circles, twenty-­seven-­acre squares, and forty-­seven-­
acre larger circles. Their builders obviously had well-­refined 
techniques of measurement, layout, and execution. Of the five, 
Liberty, Frankfort, and Works East are gone, but Baum’s scant re-
mains lie just a few miles down the valley near where it appears 
to close at the base of Spruce Hill. Not visible on the ground today, 
and not extensively studied, it remains on private land. The Seip-­
Baum pair prompt us to wonder at the placement of two such sim-
ilar complexes literally within sight of each other in this beautiful 
valley.
Five earthwork complexes in Ross County had three nearly identical geometric elements, 
with matching areas, but in each case combined differently.

5 Seip Conjoined Mound. The unrestored remains of another large 
mound, also excavated in the 1930s, lie to the northeast of the Seip-­
Pricer Mound. Its form combined three lobes, each correspond-
ing to one of the interconnected rooms of a large timber-­framed 
building. Here, archaeologists found more graves and artifacts, 
some of them similar to those from the larger mound. The tallest 
of the mound’s three sections stood thirty-­two feet tall and had a 
stone staircase climbing to its summit. Beyond the remains of the 
mound, to the northeast, the National Park’s mowing pattern out-
lines the large circle’s distinctive reentrant gateway.
NATIVE CEREMONY
At the core of American Indian cultural values is the understanding that all 
things—­humans, animals, plants, rocks, water, air—­are living, are connected, 
and deserve respect. Everyday tasks are marked by honor and appreciation, 
reflected in ceremonies. Ceremonies are also acts of healing, and therefore 
“medicine” in Native traditions that continue to the present-­day—­healing 
our minds, and maintaining strength in our bodies, our families, and our 
communities. Ceremonies instruct us on ways for living a good life, and 
maintaining harmonious relationships with other people and the natural world. 
The legacy of the Hopewell tradition shows the importance of ceremonial life 
in all things—­extravagance in material artistry, precision in earthwork design, 
and elaboration in the rituals of death and burial. Marking the paths of the 
Sun and Moon, the earthworks determined the times for ceremonies, when, as 
still today in Native communities, large gatherings occurred in public spaces, 
accompanied by feasting, gifting, dancing, and ceremonial games.
—­Marti L. Chaatsmith, Associate Director of the Newark Earthworks
Center at The Ohio State University (Comanche, Choctaw)
4 Seip-­Pricer Mound. The focal point of the site today is the second-­
largest mound ever built by the Hopewell culture. Excavations here 
in the 1920s and ’30s uncovered some of the people’s most beau-
tiful, distinctive artistry—­a clay head, a tortoiseshell swan, and 
copper plaques with remnants of fabric still clinging to them. The 
cloth fibers (also fur and feathers) survived nearly two millennia 
underground through the effect of the copper salts. Five large effigy 
smoking pipes were laid down on one layer of the mound, likely 
imported from Tennessee. This ceremonial artistry was found 
among graves, fire basins, and deposits on the floor of what had 
been a large, timber-­framed, three-­chambered building. They took 
down the building and then built up, in layers, the mound’s final 
form—­30 feet high and 240 feet long, capping and encircling it with 
large stones to deter erosion.
Timber Temples. The grand wood-­framed structure whose remains 
were buried under the mound was 113 feet long, precise and sym-
metrical in its gridded layout, and nearly identical to another at the 
Liberty Earthworks, nearby in the Scioto Valley. Many activities and 
Domestic life took place in spacious interi-
ors with benches and hearths, while many 
other daily activities happened outdoors.

EN ROUTE TO CHILLICOTHE
US Route 50 winds through this scenic valley and the nearby village of Bourneville 
where, at the east edge of town, stands one of the route’s several stately Virginia-­style 
mansions. The forms and proportions of such houses, always of brick or the local 
Berea Sandstone (also called Waverly), derive from 1700s Virginia, and reflect the 
tastes as well as the agricultural wealth of their owners—­post–­Revolutionary War 
landowners or their near descendants, who sent their abundant crops and livestock 
to the economic hub of Chillicothe, with its newly built canal.
SPRUCE HILL EARTHWORKS
Though difficult to access, and with only subtle, low, stone walls, 
the level summit of Spruce Hill is encircled by the largest Hopewell-­
era enclosure known.
Orientation. The steep hill just southeast of Bourneville (best 
seen from a cemetery about a half mile east of town) was ringed 
by low stone walls between 1 and 400 CE. At both ends of the 140-­
acre hilltop, the walls form unique reentrant gateways. From the 
top of Spruce Hill, magnificent views of the valley extend in all 
directions, which, in antiquity, would have encompassed a string 
of magnificent geometric earthworks: a circle near Bourneville as 
well as Baum and Seip. Access to the hilltop is via a steep climb up 
its northeastern slope, though beyond that point there are no devel-
oped trails or signage. The Arc of Appalachia’s website gives hiking 
information and thorough descriptions of the site’s archaeological 
and ecological significance. The built remains are hard to detect. 
While the gateways at both ends of the enclosure are visible, most 
of the surrounding walls are too low to readily appreciate.
Ancient Fires. Spruce Hill’s most intriguing story concerns evidence 
of intense fires built along its rim. They reached temperatures that 
can only be created in enclosed chambers. Chunks of vitrified soil 
The steep, striated cliffs of Copperas 
Mountain, visible to the east from Seip 
Earthworks, provided a variety of unusual 
and useful minerals.
Squier and Davis’s map of Spruce Hill,  
from Ancient Monuments of the  
Mississippi Valley, depicts its unusual  
reentrant gateways.
6 Small Circle and Ellipse. West of the large mound, the park’s 
mowing pattern defines the smaller circle (it’s actually quite large, 
extending far across the open field), and the small, interior-­ditched 
ellipse standing just inside the wall connecting the small circle to 
the square. In these western portions of the site, recent magnetom-
etry surveys show surviving traces of many subsurface features—­
more circles, post patterns, and rounded-­corner structures—­most 
of them unknown to the early nineteenth-­century surveyors, and 
yet to be investigated.
7 Square. The park’s landscaping and adjacent mowed path-
way outline the large square at Seip. Magnetic and LiDAR traces 
show that it was a perfect geometric figure with gateways at the 
midpoints of each side and at its corners. The sides are 1,079 feet, 
approximating the standard Hopewell length. The level terraces 
here step down slightly, because this location was more suscep-
tible than most geometric earthwork sites to extreme flooding 
throughout its geological history. Squier and Davis speculated that  
catastrophic floods could have been caused here by land slippage 
on the steep cliffs just across the river—­probably both before and 
after the earthen walls were constructed. A mowed path leads from 
the square to an interpretive platform by the riverbank.
Copperas Mountain. The great Hopewell earthworks were often 
placed in proximity to striking natural features. Visible about one 
mile to the east of Seip are the precipitous straited cliffs of Cop-
peras Mountain, a 350-­foot-­high exposure of Ohio and Bedford 
Shales, overlaid with this area’s characteristic Berea Sandstone. 
Minerals occur on the exposed face, including dolomite, barite, 
quartz, calcite, and pyrite. Efflorescence on the cliff face yields 
other compounds, some suitable for dying cloth. Other deposits in 
the valley here include alum (aluminum or potassium oxide) and 
the red ocher credited with suggesting the river’s name.

SPRUCE HILL FIRES
National Park ranger Bruce Lombardo describes the evidence of the huge 
flaming spectacles that once burned atop Spruce Hill: In the 1930s, Emerson 
Greenman, an Ohio Historical Society archaeologist, did an excavation up at 
Spruce Hill. In his field notes he mentions finding chunks of clay, baked clay, 
that have the impressions of logs on them, and where those log impressions 
are in the clay, the surface of the clay has been vitrified, has been melted and 
glazed. It is possible that the Hopewell built some kind of log-­and-­rock-­and-­
soil pyre, burned it, and as the logs burned out they 
would have created these open spaces through the 
pyre. Well, you’re up on top of a drafty ridge, with a 
very good wind, and these open spaces may have 
acted as flues, as drafts. And inside those pyres, you 
may have reached temperatures where you melted 
sandstone and quartz. (Link to video is on page 205.)
The Hopewell Mound Group’s geometric, hilltop, and irregular walls and ditches enclosed 
a huge number of mounds and other features.
preserved. The trail drops into the vast open meadow where sur-
rounding walls and interior mounds are not easy to detect, although 
the 127-­acre extent of the site is evident from the surrounding tree 
lines. Informational signage throughout the site is abundant and 
helpful. The wide profile of the largest mound (Mound 25, left unre-
stored after excavations) anchors the space of the large field, while 
a few other mounds and a large circle are identified by signage. The 
major walls and mounds are defined by mowing.
History. This hallowed ground was probably in development and 
use over the entire period of Hopewell influence and then, like the 
others, reforested between about 400 and 1800 CE. Caleb Atwater 
illustrated the earthwork in 1820, as did Squier and Davis again in 
the 1840s, by which time farming was well underway. Later came 
two rail lines, one of which is repurposed today as a recreation-
al trail. Excavations of the largest mound yielded many artifacts 
for an exhibition at the 1893 Chicago World’s Fair. The collection 
was so spectacular that the whole culture was given the name of 
the site’s owner—­a Chillicothe merchant named Mordecai Cloud 
Hopewell. The land continued to be farmed until its acquisition, 
just in the past few decades. It became part of the National Park in 
1992. New research and remote sensing imagery continue to reveal 
more startling and superlative features in this amazing place—­by 
all evidence the most important in the Hopewell world.

melted and recrystallized. Massive bonfires atop this prominent 
plateau would have made for an impressive spectacle, visible from 
all the major ceremonial centers in the Paint Valley and its tribu-
taries. Evidence from many earthwork sites points to intense burn-
ing, but Spruce Hill’s early fame among the colonial settlers had an 
unusual twist—­stoking a popular fantasy that Vikings or other Iron 
Age Europeans had once forged implements here.
 HOPEWELL MOUND GROUP
This vast complex was the Ancient Ohioans’ most revered sanctu-
ary—­a place of astonishing complexity, spectacular artistry, and the 
greatest variety and proliferation of mounds the culture ever created.
Orientation. Hopewell Mound Group (4731 Sulphur Lick Rd., Chilli- 
cothe) is easily reached either from Bourneville via Maple Grove 
Road, or from Chillicothe via US Route 50 and Anderson Station 
Road. The site is open daylight hours with no admission charge. 
From the parking area, a two-­mile loop trail leads to the site’s 
main features, beginning with a climb up the northern hill. Across 
the hilltop, the walls and ditches of the Great Enclosure are well

SACRED LANDSCAPES
The vast majority of Indian tribal religions  .  .  . have a sacred center at a 
particular place, be it a river, a mountain, a plateau, valley, or other natural 
feature. This center enables the people to look out along the four dimensions 
and locate their lands, to relate all historical events within the confines of this 
particular land, and to accept responsibility for it.
—Vine Deloria, American historian and theologian (1933–­2005,
Standing Rock Sioux)
Most of the northern  
hilltop perimeter of the large 
enclosure is well preserved, its 
walls and ditches dipping in and 
out of several small ravines.
A virtual reconstruction offers an orientation to 
the Hopewell Mound Group’s many mounds and  other 
features, and to the vast, strange splendor of this ceremonial 
place. Though now the “type site” for the culture, 
it was anything but typical. (Link to animation is on page 205.)
1 First Wayside. With a picnic shelter and restrooms nearby, the 
parking area focuses on a clearly marked trailhead with generous 
introductory signage. The initial, concrete-­paved trail segment 
leads to a wayside on the left providing seating, a bronze relief map 
of the earthwork, and the first vista through a clearing in the trees 
showing the spatial extent of the site. Although no earthwork ele-
ments are readily visible, the astonishing scale of the original en-
closure is indicated by the distant forested perimeter.
2 North Overlook. The trail enters the forest and climbs the hill-
side before opening into a large clearing (beneath an electrical 
transmission tower). An overview of the earthwork may be imag-
ined from here, with the subtle rise of the largest mound’s remains 
in the right-­hand distance just before the road. The walled Great 
Enclosure extended southward beyond the road to today’s tree line 
at the far edge of the site. This huge space, bounded by earthen 
walls and dotted with many mounds, was the ceremonial center of 
the entire Hopewell world.
3 Northern Walls and Ditches. From the clearing onward, remnants 
of the well-­preserved earthwork walls and exterior ditches are vis-
ible intermittently, and sometimes dramatically, through the trees; 
some of their vernal pools regularly retain water. Unlike the flat ter-
race below, this hilltop was never plowed, serving instead as pasture 
and woodlot throughout the nineteenth and twentieth centuries (a 
pond from its agricultural days survives nearby in the woods). The 
trail swings far to the north, crossing a couple wooden bridges, be-
fore returning to the best views of some of the largest and most im-
pressive walls; it then descends to the level terrace below.
4 Western Wall. Only a portion of the defining wall survives along the 
western edge of the Great Enclosure. It is quite low and buried in the 
trees along the small, intermittent stream as it approaches the road. 
The ancients redirected a small spring in order to help maintain water

The Effigy’s Burials. Much of this wealth of exotic materials and 
masterful artistry accompanied the 102 Ancestors who were laid 
to rest beneath the effigy. All was prepared with the fullest ritual 
and care. Though early excavations of these sacred remains can-
not be undone, their records and the artifacts themselves tell us 
much about the people, their leaders, their beliefs, and their ways 
of life and death. Some of the burials were first burned in another 
location, then the remaining ash and bone swept together and re-
deposited here. Most were buried unburned, though, stretched out 
in log tombs. Inside buildings, some of the tombs were covered 
with small mounds. When the buildings were decommissioned, all 
the remains were subsumed within the larger mound.
6 Flint Mound (Mound 2). Some mounds were built to cover not 
burials but huge deposits of unworked materials. (One mound at 
the Mound City, for example, covered a fire basin filled only with 
thirty pounds of galena—­lead sulfide—­all left in its shiny, crystal-
line form.) At the Hopewell Mound Group, mounds were dedicated 
to mica slabs and to a huge pile of obsidian pieces. And Mound 2 
held over 8,000 nearly identical flint discs, laid down carefully in 
two layers and set in fine gravel on the floor of a building. They 
came from quarries in Indiana, Kentucky, and beyond, and were 
arranged here in little bundles, each about as many as one person 
could carry.
Material Deposits. On the floor of Mound 25, the people placed two 
Great Deposits of objects, many ritually broken and burned. The 
variety was astonishing, as is the complex precision with which 
they were sometimes arranged. Contrasting dualities positioned all 
the black obsidian, for example, in the eastern deposit, and all the 
white pearls in the western one. Two other deposits, left under a 
small mound near the hillside, also showed a deliberate contrast—­
all the mica was in one, laid on a square tablet, while all the copper 
was in the other, laid on a round disc. To those who left them, the 
Magnetometry of Mound 25 shows four 
rectangular shapes along its sides, perhaps the stylized 
paws of a crouching panther.

completing a water boundary for the site, as at Fort Ancient.
5 The Effigy (Mound 25).The largest mound was a triple lobed form 
over 500 feet long. In the nineteenth century it was called “The Ef-
figy” and now, more prosaically, “Mound 25.” It was constructed 
over the carefully laid remains of elaborate rituals, as well as spec-
tacular artistry and regalia. The builders removed the topsoil to 
reach a clay layer, and then mixed clay and water to create a hard 
floor surface—­it’s been called “Hopewell concrete.” Over several 
generations, ceremonies were held here, some inside buildings 
and some out in the open on the prepared floor. They dug pits, built 
fires, and covered certain areas with stones or multicolored clay. 
They brought many precious things to the site—­beautiful objects 
crafted in materials from far away like obsidian and copper—­often 
breaking and burning them before placing them in ritual deposits.

THE COSMOLOGICAL PLAN
The architecture of the earthworks, whether it’s a simple oval, a square, or 
a circle combined with a square, or some other kind of geometric figure, 
really is an attempt to create, on Earth, a cosmological plan. And these 
things are situated and oriented in such a way as to, in a sense, re-­create 
the cosmos on Earth. The basic rationale for doing something of this sort is 
that many different peoples are being brought together, who do not have all 
the institutions for keeping them more or less settled and off each other’s 
backs. So under the plan of the heavens on earth, you now create the context 
in which people can meet, and undertake ceremonies, and gossip, and 
exchange various things, all with the gods looking at them.
—­James Brown, Professor Emeritus of Anthropology and
Archaeology, Northwestern University
the western edge of a perfect square with the usual midpoint and 
corner openings. As at other sites nearby, the square was com-
posed of noticeably red soil. Dr. Edwin Davis noted in 1845 that “the 
wall of the square is wholly of clay, and its outlines may be easily 
traced by the eye, from a distance, by its color. . . . That (the walls) 
have been subjected to the action of fire, is too obvious to admit of 
doubt.” Mowed trails follow the full perimeter of the square and 
return toward the first wayside and the parking area.
 CHILLICOTHE
This historic town, in its striking setting, was the first capital of 
Ohio, having taken its name from the Shawnee capital (or chilli- 
cothe) it displaced. Centuries earlier, this convergence of valleys 
was the capital, or sacred heartland, of the far-­reaching Hopewell 
world.
History and Setting. The name Chillicothe derives from the Shaw-
nee “Chalahgawtha,” which means “principal town”—­the seat of 
the tribe’s chief. But this fine setting, on a large bend in the Scioto 
River amid some of the region’s most distinctive landscapes, was a 
place of primary importance both long before, and afterward. The 
huge, ancient Teays Valley emerges here from the Appalachian Pla-
teau, and the prominent, mountain-­like Logan Range defines the 
dual deposits probably symbolized two parts of a whole, like night 
and day, or summer and winter—­or signs of unity between groups, 
ideas, or individuals. They were placed among burials, but not con-
nected with any particular one.
7 Large Circle Woodhenge. Comprehensive remote sensing sur-
veys carried out in the 2010s brought to light a 410-­foot-­diameter 
circular ditch and embankment corresponding to the earthen 
ring recorded here by Squier and Davis in the 1840s. The images 
also showed a line of regularly spaced postholes along the ditch’s 
inner edge. Excavations have since confirmed that the earthen ring 
traced the outline of a huge, dismantled woodhenge similar to the 
one found at the Stubbs Earthworks in Warren County in 1998 (see 
page 132). The circle had four symmetrically placed gateways, com-
plete with causeways across the inner ditch. In the center, a set of 
four carefully positioned Earth ovens were large enough to bake 
feasts for the assembled crowds, 
which may well have gathered 
to witness the alignment of the 
midsummer sunset through the 
circle’s northwestern gateway.
Small Circle. A little to the north of the large circle, a smaller, 100-­
foot circle was first detected in 2001, when a National Park Service 
team began their early remote sensing survey work inside the Great 
Enclosure. One day, a startling image appeared: the trace of a long-­
lost earthen ring and ditch, completely invisible on the surface. It 
had also been invisible to Squier and Davis when they recorded 
the site in the 1840s. Later magnetometry surveys revealed a set of 
four Earth ovens (also carefully positioned) outside the circle and 
directly aligned with its eastern gateway; they appear identical to 
those in the center of the larger circle.
8 Eastern Wall and Square. Just past the remains of another large 
mound (Mound 23), the Great Enclosure’s eastern wall and its ac-
companying ditch are faintly perceptible running perpendicular 
from the road, aligned with the clearing on the hilltop. They form

The Temple of the Winds near Fifth and Paint Streets is the most elegantly 
detailed of Chillicothe’s many outstanding Greek Revival houses.
The historic center of 
Chillicothe  preserves 
an unusually rich and

northeastern horizon. For the Shawnee, this was one in a series of 
several chillicothes as the tribe was being forced westward by settle-
ment. (Another is commemorated today at the Great Council State 
Park; see pages 106–107.) Many centuries earlier, this was the center 
of Ohio’s ancient earthwork-­building culture, and the town stands 
today among several of its most spectacular ceremonial sites.
Ohio’s First Capital. The present town was founded in 1796 and four 
years later was named the capital of the eastern half of the North-
west Territory. In 1803 it became the new state’s first capital. Pros-
perity came early to this new American settlement on the banks of 
the Scioto, a gateway for settlement and commerce in the Virginia 
Military District stretching westward from here to the Little Miami 
River. Early culture and commerce were influenced by wealthy, war-­
veteran landowners from Virginia, whose huge estates produced 
cattle, pigs, and corn in abundance. In 1831, the new Ohio and Erie 
Canal came through town, creating decades of prosperity as a com-
mercial hub for the vast agricultural lands opening westward.
The Heart of It All. The intersection of Main and Paint Streets marks 
the center of more than sixteen blocks of diverse, interesting his-
toric architecture, much of it already standing in the 1840s when 
Chillicothe newspaper editor Ephraim Squier and local physician 
Edwin Davis collaborated here on their Ancient Monuments of the 
Mississippi Valley, the first publication of the newly established 
Smithsonian Institution. Historian Roger Kennedy aptly called 
Ohio’s first capital the “Delphi of North America”—­no less for its 
remarkable concentration of Greek Revival architecture than for 
its status as the heartland of the brilliant Hopewell culture, whose 
influence was spread across much of the continent sixteen centu-
ries earlier. That status is well reflected in a local saying: “If Julius 
Ceasar had had an embassy in North America, it would have been 
in Chillicothe, Ohio!” The Ross-­Chillicothe Convention and Visitors 
Bureau, from its website or offices (230 North Plaza Blvd.), can pro-
vide further information on where to stay and eat, and what to do.
Downtown Historic Districts. Chillicothe has preserved much of its 
nineteenth-­century urban fabric, especially along Paint, Second, 
and Water Streets. An unusually large concentration of pre–­Civil 
War commercial buildings, plus superb specimens of historic 
houses, churches, public, and commercial buildings line the 
nearby streets. Especially impressive are the many Greek Revival 
examples, dating from the heyday of the canal era. Several line 
West Main and Second Streets, but the finest are on South Paint 
Street: the unusual and delicately detailed Temple of the Winds (at 
#134); the large mansion next door (on the corner of Fifth Street); 
and the Atwood House (now a fine bed-­and-­breakfast, at #68); all 
dating from the late 1840s. Quaint old storefronts line Water Street, 
once the canal route. James Emmitt’s giant, canal-­side warehouse 
(from the 1850s) still stands on Mulberry Street. Paint Street is a

Ross County Heritage Center. A collection of buildings just off South 
Paint Street unifies the various facilities of the Ross County Histor-
ical Society (45 West Fifth St.). The museum offers docent-­escorted 
tours at a modest fee. The artifact exhibits fill a historic mansion, 
telling the stories of Indigenous antiquity, early Northwest Territory 
and statehood history, Chillicothe’s days as Ohio’s first capital, and its 
canal-­era commercial prominence. The county’s role in the nation’s 
wars is told through important historical figures, and especially 
Camp Sherman, the massive World War I training camp north of 
town. Two beautiful scale models depict Chillicothe’s historic char-
acter during its early settler days and after the arrival of the railroads. 
A collection of unique historical vehicles (including a bandwagon, 
hearse, and early fire engine) round out the exhibits. The adjacent 
Knowles Log House portrays the conditions of daily life for the area’s 
early settlers. The McKell Library holds extensive collections of rare 
books, manuscripts, photographs, and other historical documents.
Story Mound. Near the center of a residential district northwest of 
downtown (452 Delano Ave.), stands the Story Mound. Partially ex-
cavated in 1897, it revealed one burial and evidence of a circular sub-­
mound building. It resembles a more famous mound that once stood 
about a mile to the northwest, at the foot of the hill on which Thomas 
Worthington built his Adena Estate. There, in 1901, William Mills of 
the Ohio Historical and Archaeological Society (now the Ohio His-
tory Connection) identified the characteristics that came to define 
the Adena culture, including certain burial practices and a circular 
building plan. Called the Adena 
Mound, it also contained an ap-
parently unique pottery object, 
the now-­iconic Adena Pipe—­an 
eight-­inch figure of a man wear-
ing a headdress, loincloth, and 
feather bustle, his knees bent as 
if he’s dancing.
Belleview Avenue. From near 
the corner of Fifth and Walnut, 
Belleview Avenue angles up the 
hill to the southwest. This road 
was once a section of Zane’s 
Trace (on its way from Wheeling 
to Maysville; see page 79), connecting what is now Marietta Road 
northeast of town with State Route 772 heading southwest beyond 
Paint Creek. It passes early mansions like the 1826 Italianate-­Greek-­
Revival hybrid called Tanglewood, with its unusual rooftop light 
monitor, curving staircase, and graceful Ionic porticos. Across the 
street (239 Belleview Ave.) is the entrance to Grandview Cemetery, 
where Felix Renick, Thomas Worthington, and other prominent 
founders are buried, and from which there are fine views of the 
town, the nearby Logan Range, and especially (to the south) the 
broad, ancient Teays Valley framed between its prominent, unglaci-
ated hills (see photo on page 8).
Junction Earthworks. A grassy meadow at 618 Bellevue Avenue is 
filled with the intact foundations of a fascinating cluster of small 
geometric earthworks—­one of several such groupings recorded 
in the Chillicothe area in the 1800s. A loop trail connects a prodi-
gious variety of shapes including circles, crescents, rounded-­corner  
rectangles, and an apparently unique quatrefoil (a merging of four 
partial circles better known in Gothic architecture). Each figure had 
an interior ditch. Although the shapes are not always maintained by 
differential mowing, a free downloadable app by the Heartland Earth-
works Conservancy explains the site, envisioning it with animations

SAVING JUNCTION EARTHWORKS
The stunning magnetometry image of the Junction Earthworks helped 
persuade donors to save the site from destruction. Arc of Appalachia 
director Nancy Stranahan explains: That amazing image had an awful lot 
to do with saving Junction Earthworks. The fact that that image went public, 
and showed people something that they had really never seen in modern 
times—­the revelation of these nine figures scripted on the land, becoming 
visible again—­was just contagious. And in that excitement that spread across 
Ohio, knowing that the auction was coming up so quickly, people became 
very generous. And we were able to raise $300,000 in just eight days, which 
has never happened before or since for our organization! (Link to video is on 
page 205.)
Despite no surface relief today, 
the shapes at the Junction 
Earthworks, including its unique 
quatrefoil, stand out clearly in 
magnetometry data.
Thomas Worthington’s Adena Mansion outside Chillicothe was designed by America’s first  
professional architect, Benjamin Henry Latrobe.
are evident from two clifftop promontories—­overlooking both the 
narrow, forested canyon to the south, and the distant, open, level 
horizon to the northwest.
Adena Mansion and Gardens. On the prominent hill just northwest 
of town (847 Adena Rd., Chillicothe) stands the stately home of 
Thomas Worthington, Ohio’s first US Senator and a two-­term gov-
ernor. He built the house between 1802 and 1807 and later named 
it Adena, which he understood to refer, in Hebrew, to a place with 
a “delightful situation.” His wealth and his idealism for what the 
new lands in the West could become are reflected in his ambitious 
choice to hire America’s first professional architect, Benjamin 
Henry Latrobe, to draw the plans (on the advice of his friend and 
political mentor Thomas Jefferson). The design drew on many in-
fluences, thanks to Latrobe’s extensive travels and education—­from 
West Virginia stone manor houses like the one Worthington grew 
up in, to villas by the Italian Renaissance master Andrea Palladio. 
The Ohio History Connection has restored the house and gardens 
since acquiring them in 1946. A modern museum and education 
center interprets the life and history of the Worthington family and 
early-­1800s Ohio.
Thomas Worthington. Born into a well-­to-­do family in Charles-
town, now in West Virginia, in 1773, Worthington was among those, 
especially from the slave states, seeking a new start in the West.

COFFEE CUPS AND CAKES
All the ironies of the relationship between the founders and the Indians came 
together in the drawing room at Adena in 1807, when this thirty-­four-­year-­
old Virginia squire entertained, for coffee and cakes, Tecumseh, Blue Jacket, 
The Panther, and Round Head—­the leaders of an Indian confederacy which 
came to a bloody end seven years later at the Battle of the Thames, when 
Tecumseh was killed. But in this place, equals were dealing with equals, and in 
fact equals were so conscious of their equalness that the Indians made jokes 
about the coffee being distributed to them, pretending that they were savages, 
which they were not. They pretended, three of the four, that they didn’t know 
how to handle coffee cups and cakes, though all of them had spent plenty of 
time in the communities in which coffee and cakes were frequent artifices. 
After the little action of pretending that they didn’t know how to do all this, 
they in fact spent a whole week around here, living with the Worthingtons and 
celebrating their common presence in the midst of a deep antiquity—­to which 
the Worthingtons were visitors, and the Indians were not.
—­Roger G. Kennedy, American Historian and Former Director
of the National Park Service
Mound City’s forms may be at their most beautiful when tinged with early sunlight on a  
frosty spring morning.
Tecumseh Outdoor Drama. The story of the great Shawnee leader 
Tecumseh is retold in an outdoor drama every summer at the Sugar-
loaf Mountain Amphitheatre (5968 Marietta Rd., Chillicothe), from 
early June to early September. Tecumseh’s name in Shawnee means 
“shooting star,” suggesting the brilliant but brief career of this leg-
endary leader, remembered for his heroic resistance to the confis-
cation of his people’s lands and the destruction of their way of life.
MOUND CITY
This cluster of restored mounds sets a solemn and peaceful mood, 
as a place of reverent memory. Now as the headquarters of the Na-
tional Park, it offers an optimal orientation to the Hopewell heart-
land’s monumental antiquities.
Orientation. Mound City lies three miles north of downtown Chilli-
cothe (16062 SR 104, Chillicothe) in a wooded enclave between two 
prisons. The parking area and the Hopewell Culture National His-
torical Park visitor center face an iconic view of the mounds (at its 
best on a sunlit early morning). A low earthen wall encloses two 
dozen mounds; its two gateways suggest an axial passage from the 
visitor center to the steep banks of the Scioto River beyond. Eight 
constructed pits surround the enclosure. Some of the mounds 
held skillfully crafted ceremonial artistry including effigy smoking 
pipes, copper sculptures, and shimmering blankets of mica.
His idealism was reflected in his estate, but also in his many polit-
ical accomplishments—­as an advocate for Ohio statehood, a key 
author of its Constitution, and a senator and state representative 
advocating for the construction of the state’s canal system. He also 
respected the Natives and hoped for a peaceful future. The land of 
his beloved Adena grew to 25,000 acres and produced cattle, pigs, 
and Merino sheep, as well as a diversified mix of crops. The Great 
Seal of the State of Ohio was conceived here when Worthington and 
some friends looked out from the hilltop one morning (after a long 
night of cards, as the story goes) just as the Sun was rising over the  
wide Scioto Valley and the Logan Range beyond—­now Great Seal 
State Park.

about a variety of topics related to the earthworks and the daily life 
of their builders. Topics and their associated materials and artifacts

2 Borrow Pits. Large pits just outside the enclosure wall (especially 
prominent on the western side facing the parking lot) suggest they 
might have been “borrow pits” from which soil was taken to build 
the mounds. But these flat river terraces are formed from hundreds 
of feet of gravel—­the bed of the ancient Teays River—­lying beneath 
only a thin layer of topsoil. Holes this deep would have yielded very 
little soil suitable for building mounds. Investigations have now 
shown that these pits were lined with stone and clay to help them 
hold water—­a recurring theme in Hopewell site design also seen at 
Fort Ancient, Newark’s Great Circle, and elsewhere.
3 Enclosing Wall. In its day, Mound City was much more than just 
a silent “city of the dead.” The number of buildings and mounds 
grew over time here, maybe for as long as four centuries, and 
many generations. Many things went on here, including various 
building projects right up until the enclosing wall was built, and  
perhaps after. Large amounts of charcoal and deer bone found in 
the wall suggest festivals and feasting. The large enclosure reflected 
what may have been a new idea at the time, that sacred spaces  
should be framed by a geometric boundary. Perhaps this was  
the starting point for the many variations on this idea at other 
Hopewell earthworks, with the 
rounded-­corner rectangle de-
rived directly from the shape of 
the buildings beneath the indi-
vidual mounds.
Shriver Circle. Squier and Davis’s map pairs Mound City with a large 
circle to the south, whose invisible remnants now lie in a field and 
prison yard along both sides of State Route 104. When the highway 
was being widened, archaeologists investigated and found the cir-
cle’s twelve-­foot-­deep exterior ditch, lined with a foot-­thick layer of 
clay to hold the slopes and water. Associated burials in its central 
An artist’s collage depicts one of the 
large pits adjacent to Mound City’s 
enclosure wall, with its clay and stone 
lining still holding water as intended.
History. Mound City is the only surviving Hopewell necropolis—­a 
gathering of mounds commemorating the mortuary rituals and re-
mains entombed beneath them. The accumulated mounds, and the 
enclosing wall, reflect several generations of use before the site was 
left to forest cover after 400 CE. A Virginia veteran was granted the 
land in 1798, and within forty years the busy Ohio and Erie Canal 
passed nearby. When Squier and Davis surveyed the site in 1846, the 
forest still preserved most of the mounds, but soon after, the land 
was cleared and annual plowing began. In 1917, the federal govern-
ment bought the land for Camp Sherman, a World War I training 
camp. The army was shaving off all the mounds to build barracks 
when Henry Shetrone of the Ohio History Connection stepped in 
and asked that the central mound be spared. In the 1920s, after the 
camp was decommissioned, careful archaeological investigations 
were undertaken, and the mounds were restored.

earthworks in Hopewell Culture National Historical Park, including, 
besides Mound City, Seip Earthworks, Hopewell Mound Group, and 
the Hopeton Earthworks. Park service rangers provide a warm wel-
come, helpful knowledge, free informational materials, and periodic 
tours. A video program—­and a well-­stocked book shop—­offer further 
interpretive perspectives. In a small exhibit space, visitors can learn

4 Paired Mounds. Two mounds near Mound City’s western gateway 
(Mounds 12 and 13) cover the remains of two interconnected build-
ings. On the floor of one were several pits and clay basins, suggesting 
places of preparation for the more formal rituals and deposits next 
door. The larger building held elaborate burials; one group rested 
beneath shimmering blankets of mica along with many precious ob-
jects. Four other burials were accompanied by objects crafted from 
precious copper—­double-­headed vultures, an animal headdress 
with movable ears, and copper deer antlers. This dual-­chambered 
building may have been a model for other, larger versions like that at 
the Seip Earthworks (see pages 43–44).
Ceremonies. The buried wealth left under the mounds was the par-
aphernalia of solemn rituals and performances. Fires burned in 
shallow clay basins, central to ceremonies during which the dead 
were cremated and symbolic objects were ritually killed (broken 
or burned, or both) to be left with them. The ash and remains were 
swept up and placed carefully on the building floor, or on low earthen 
platforms. In a final ceremony, the people took down or burned the 
building and built up a mound over its remains and contents. While 
Mound City was in use, visitors would have seen functioning build-
ings here, standing among the mounds commemorating those al-
ready gone.
5 Pipes Mound. Under another mound (Mound 8), a large bag had 
been placed next to a clay basin; it was filled with ashes, beads,

HOPEWELL AGRICULTURE
National Park archaeologist Bret Ruby explains the scope and methods of 
Hopewell agriculture, based on evidence from Mound City and elsewhere: They 
participated in the cultivation, the domestication, of a whole suite of native starchy 
and oily seeded plants that contributed a significant amount of their diet. They 
weren’t living strictly on the fruits of nature; these people were clearing ground, 
planting crops, harvesting crops, and they were fully committed agriculturalists. 
The kind of agriculture they were practicing was probably swidden agriculture, 
where they’re moving, they’re clearing plots, using them for a period of years, 
probably not more than seven years or so, until the soil fertility was depleted, 
and then they’re clearing other plots. There’s evidence 
of this in the seed assemblages from Hopewell sites: At 
about this time you start to see a spike in forest-­edge-­
type species: elderberry, sumac, early successional 
species. They are clearing plots for agriculture, probably 
also clearing land for earthwork construction. (Link to 
video is on page 205.)
some copper items, and about 200 carved stone effigy smoking 
pipes, all purposely broken. The pipe bowls portrayed a variety of 
animals (and a few human heads), carved with accuracy, great art-
istry, and intricate detail. Another deposit of almost identical pipes 
was found at the Tremper Mound, forty miles south of Mound City 
along the Scioto River. The animals carved on the pipes are tradi-
tional figures in Eastern Woodland stories, creatures with their own 
will and power, including beavers, falcons, serpents, and turtles, 
among others. Edwin Davis excavated the pipes in the 1840s and, 
unable to find a domestic buyer, sold them to an Englishman; this 
explains why they are now exhibited in London’s British Museum.

of its precursor building’s framing posts indicated by markers set 
in the ground. Before 100 CE, Mound City began with just a few cer-
emonial houses like this one, their fire basins and doorways with a 
variety of orientations. Some of these had probably been disman-
tled (or burned) and mounded over by the time a second devel-
opment phase began. The people erected three central buildings,

THE BROKEN PIPES
Well, first of all, the pipes are not just an implement that we use to smoke 
sacred tobacco. We didn’t look at it as being a piece of art. We didn’t look at 
it as being an implement we could go “ooh” and “aah” to. We looked on it as 
alive. We needed that spirit, if it was a bear, or if it was whatever animal you 
had . . . in that pipe, that was alive. [And] . . . maybe that’s the catalyst of your 
ceremony. That’s the catalyst of your prayer. You can go no higher, you can 
never use it again, and so you break it. That’s the most ultimate—­this is not 
going to make sense in English—­the most ultimate, sacred thing you could do. 
The most sacred thing you can do, because that is your life right there, that’s 
your pipe.
—­Gerard Baker, retired National Park Service Superintendent
    (Mandan-­Hidatsa)
As seen from the visitor center in winter, Mound City echoes the profile of the Logan Range 
across the river, whose features also mark solar and lunar alignments from this spot.
creating a new ritual focus toward which at least seven more build-
ings were then oriented, and built the low enclosing wall to further 
strengthen the unity and formality of the site. The overall spatial 
order created by these final features may reflect an increasing or-
ganization and social ranking among the people.
Squares with Rounded Corners. The shape of Mound City’s enclos-
ing wall, in mathematical terms, is a “superellipse”—­though some 
prefer the more colloquial “squircle.” Hopewell sites and artistry 
use variations on this gently flare-­sided, rounded-­corner shape 
at all scales—­earthworks, building plans, crematory basins, and 
sometimes the artifacts left within them. Recent remote sensing 
surveys have revealed that these matching shapes are everywhere 
in the Hopewell world, traced in both building outlines and interior-­
ditched earthworks. Some now-­invisible earthworks were drawn as 
circles by nineteenth-­century surveyors, but remote sensing now 
reveals them instead to be this distinctly Hopewell form.
7 Central Mound. The focal point of Mound City is the nineteen-­
foot-­tall Central Mound (Mound 7). It covered the remains of a 
complex building: A sunken room, entered along a ramp, was cen-
tered on a shallow, clay-­lined basin recessed in the floor and baked 
red by many fires. Sometime later, leaving a set of posts in place, 
the people filled the room and built a new clay fire basin exactly 
above the old one. Upon a new floor, of puddled clay and sand, 
they erected a building and laid out ten cremated burials on log-­
supported earthen platforms, roofed with bark. Three elaborate 
burials in the Central Mound were clearly respected leaders, ac-
companied by precious regalia and other objects crafted in copper 
and other exotic materials.
Eastern Horizon. From the position of Mound City—­and most pre-
cisely the Central Mound—­the Logan Range forms a symmetrical 
profile along the eastern horizon (visible now only in winter). The 
peaks of the mountains at either end mark the alignment positions 
of the minimum northern and minimum southern moonrises (ex-
plained on page 92). The outer base points of those same mountains 
align to the summer and winter solstice sunrises. These phenomena 
turn Mound City and its eastern horizon into an astronomical sight-
ing instrument, connecting the orders of the cosmos to the rituals of 
the people who gathered here to celebrate the lives of their leaders 
and deceased kin.
8 Scioto Riverbank. A paved walk leads down to a viewing platform 
above the bank of the Scioto River, where many of the people arriv-
ing for the rituals at Mound City would have disembarked from their 
canoes. The fast-­flowing water and the steep, forested embankment 
created a dramatic threshold for their entry into the ceremonial 
enclosure. A notch in the steep hillside (now fitted with concrete 
stairs) approximates the effect of the Hopewell “graded ways” that 
monumentalized the riverine approaches to other earthworks—­for

ANCIENT ASTRONOMY
Archaeoastronomy scholar Anthony Aveni explains how an ancient 
society’s reasons for marking celestial alignments would have been 
connected to other aspects of their lives, like the changing seasons: 
I think it’s change of time that matters in many of these cultures, that one 
would mark those significant “hinges in the fabric of time,” hinges when 
critical changes take place, when food shortages might occur, when the 
animals who are normally killed might migrate or leave; these are the points 
of tension in the calendar. I think this is a marvelous 
way of connecting the ecosphere, the ecological 
environment, with the heavens. And maybe we 
modern astronomers tend to cut off the heavens 
from the rest of the world—­from the mountains, 
the water, the plants, the animals, to which, I 
think, most ancient cultures would have forged a 
connection. (Link to video is on page 205.)
example, Marietta’s Sacra Via (see page 201). Returning from the riv-
erbank, wander among the mounds and admire the way their forms 
shift in perspective, or take the trail through the woods north of the 
enclosure past sandstone blocks relocated from the nearby canal.
HIGH BANK WORKS
The principal significance of High Bank Works is its resemblance 
to Newark’s Octagon Earthworks, and the closely related precision 
of both sites’ lunar (and here also solar) alignments.
A Research Preserve. After many decades of farming, the walls of the 
High Bank Works are only faintly discernable on the ground today. 
Yet the exact outlines of the site’s interconnected circle and octagon 
appear on recent remote sensing images, making it clear this is a 
near twin with Newark’s Octagon Earthworks. Both are geometrical 
and astronomical masterpieces. While Newark and High Bank are 
about sixty miles apart, they were designed using similar principles. 
Their circles share the same diameter, and each harmonizes with its 
adjacent octagon—­at High Bank, the center points of the octagon’s 
eight sides all fall on a circle of equal diameter. Mainly of archae-
ological and astronomical significance, it is a research preserve 
located southeast of Chillicothe, and only open to visitors by special 
arrangement through Hopewell Culture National Historical Park.
Lunar and Solar Alignments. Professors Ray Hively and Robert 
Horn, of Earlham College (in Richmond, Indiana), have shown that 
High Bank, like Newark’s Octagon, encodes all eight of the horizon 
positions where the moonrise appears to reverse itself during its 
long 18.6-­year cycle (see page 92). But unlike Newark, High Bank 
also has alignments to the four solstices. The principal axes of the 
two designs are exactly ninety degrees to each other. The builders 
could not simply replicate Newark’s design, due to the difference 
in latitude, so they had to create here another equally complex and 
subtle instrument. The fact that there are two similar earthworks, 
built by the same culture, with so many shared features, and both 
aligned to the long, complex, lunar cycle, provides the strongest 
evidence of the extraordinary genius of Ancient Ohio’s Indigenous 
astronomer-­architects.
TO THE UPPER SCIOTO ROUTE
From the Chillicothe area, head north to Circleville, with a fine historic center, canal 
remnants, and an interesting (albeit vanished) earthwork; then on to the greater 
Columbus area with several mounds and earthworks, the state capitol, and the Ohio 
History Connection’s headquarters, museum, and historic Ohio Village (see map, 
page 150).
TO THE LOWER SCIOTO ROUTE
Southeast out of Chillicothe, follow the most impressive stretch of the ancient 
Teays Valley to Piketon and Wakefield, the important Tremper Mound, and on to 
the remains of the vast earthwork complex at Portsmouth, and its Southern Ohio 
Museum. Loop eastward to Jackson, the restored Buckeye Furnace ironworks, and 
the Leo Petroglyphs (see map, page 162).

World Heritage Route
East
Beginning with the Hopeton Earthworks, a companion site just 
across the Scioto River from Mound City, the route connecting the 
World Heritage–­inscribed sites continues toward the northeast. 
It skirts the edges of the Appalachian foothills and approximates 
an ancient roadway that may have connected Chillicothe and 
Newark—­the Hopewell culture’s two greatest ceremonial centers.
HOPETON EARTHWORKS
Just across the Scioto River from Mound City, a long trail to an ele-
vated vantage point offers the finest appreciation of the vastness of 
the Hopewell landscape. In the foreground of the broad valley vista 
lie the clearly outlined remains of a huge circle and square.
Orientation. The Hopeton Earthworks are accessible by car from 
Mound City (via a bridge north of the site), or from the center of 
Chillicothe (via North Bridge Street). A parking area (990 Hopetown 
Rd.) is open daylight hours. The trail climbs quickly to the second

scheme also defines a portion of long parallel walls that once led to 
the bank of an old channel of the river.
ELABORATION AND RITUAL
Comparative religion scholar David Cave explains why, across cultures, 
sacred spaces and ritual objects are generally so large, impressive, or 
elaborate: One of the things we find in representations of the sacred is the 
element of excessiveness. The sacred, the things that seem to symbolize 
great power and great mystery, is indeed just that: mysterious, powerful, to 
be treated with care, even associated with taboo, danger, because it’s outside 
the remove—­it’s outside of ordinary existence, ordinary things. And so, you 
find that in rituals, there’s always this degree of going over and beyond what 
could be done very quickly. Why must costuming be so elaborate? Why must 
the ritual be so detailed, and followed so precisely over such a period of time? 
Why must a structure be so grand, when, if we’re 
just going to be making a statement, we could do 
it in a much smaller form? No, the excessiveness is 
a way to demonstrate attention, draw attention, 
to make sure that the redundancy is built in, the 
elaborateness is built in, so that the sacred gets its 
due. (Link to video is on page 205.)

History. Construction methods 
and sequences have been more 
thoroughly studied here than at 
any other Hopewell earthwork; combinations of soil types and col-
ors, for example, were extraordinarily complex. Following the end 
of Hopewell practices, the forest returned until it was cleared for 
farming in the very early 1800s. Agriculture continued for nearly 
two centuries, and by the late 1900s its increasingly destructive 
methods had diminished the earthen walls to today’s subtle pro-
files. The site joined the National Park when it was founded in 1992, 
and visitor facilities opened in 2007. Viewed from the high terrace 
overlook, the mowing pattern maintained by the park vividly por-
trays the earthwork’s scope and form.
1 Glacial Terraces. The clearly terraced structure of this valley  
results from the eons of action by the preglacial Teays River. 
The parking lot is on the first terrace—­now the Scioto’s floodplain. 
The earthworks stand on the second terrace where the older, much
larger, north-­flowing river laid down deep, gravelly subsoils. All the 
geometric earthworks occupy the equivalent of this level in their re-
spective valleys, well beyond the flood levels of the current rivers.

World Heritage Route
World Heritage Route: EAST
3 Preserved Wall. Private owners farmed 
the Hopeton Earthworks for nearly 200 
years, running their plows right over 
the walls and gradually wearing them 
down. The destruction accelerated in the 
mid-­twentieth century with the advent of ever more powerful farm 
equipment, to the point where even the walls of the square, once 
12 feet tall, are now discernable only with some effort—­their soils 
spread out in both directions on the land. A section of the wall where 
the square and circle overlap stands today much more prominently 
than the rest because it was incorporated into a fence line and thus 
escaped the plow.
4 Remote Sensing Discoveries. Despite this agricultural degrada-
tion, old aerial photographs show the earthworks clearly, some 
with traces of the now-­lost parallel walls heading for the river-
bank. Light detection and ranging (LiDAR) images reveal the ex-
tent to which the shapes remain today, but most dramatic are the 
magnetometry maps created by the National Park Service and the 
Deutsches Archäologisches Institut from 2012 to 2017. They clearly 
show the foundations of the original walls, plus the evenly spaced 
postholes along the interior of the circle’s embankment wall—­
apparently the remains of a 1,000-­foot diameter woodhenge, the 
largest yet known.
5 Parallel Walls. From outside the northwest corner of the square, 
the Park’s differential planting program shows a section of the 
parallel walls that once led to the edge of the terrace. Parallel-­
walled roadways like this once existed at several other Hopewell 
sites (Newark, Fort Ancient, Portsmouth), but this is now the best 
place to envision the strange effect of these low (maybe 3 feet high) 
walls, nearly 200 feet apart, running parallel for thousands of feet. 
This broad roadway’s southwesterly orientation aligns toward the 
winter solstice sunset. From the same vantage point, the diagonal 
view back across the corners of the square aligns to where the Sun 
would have risen on that same winter solstice day. Solstice align-
ments across diagonals of squares also occur at Mound City, Seip 
Earthworks, and the Hopewell Mound Group.
(above) Magnetometry surveys of the 
Hopeton Earthworks revealed the evenly 
spaced postholes of a giant woodhenge 
corresponding to its large circle.
A 1938 aerial photo shows the outlines of 
Hopeton’s circle and square plus, at the 
lower left, remnants of the parallel walls.
6 Wall Construction. Returning toward the parking area along the 
western side of the square, the wall’s topographic relief becomes 
somewhat clearer at close range, especially where the old farm road 
crosses over it. Hopeton is the most thoroughly studied of the major 
Hopewell earthworks, with many years of targeted excavation pro-
grams by the National Park Service. Thanks to work led by archaeolo-
gist Mark Lynott, much is known about the processes of earthen wall 
construction. The people scraped away the topsoil and built fires on 
the prepared surface. Then they brought in soils of at least two con-
trasting colors—­yellowish for the core of the wall and its interior sur-
face, and a thick layer of reddish loam along the exterior face. Very 
similar color contrasts between interior and exterior have been de-
tected at other Hopewell earthworks, including Newark’s Great Circle.
EN ROUTE TO LANCASTER
Between the Hopeton Earthworks and the northern end of the Logan Range, State 
Route 159 heads northeast. This area would have been the likely terminus of a long, 
straight, ancient roadway connecting the earthworks around Chillicothe with those 
at Newark nearly sixty miles to the northeast. As far as Lancaster, skirting the edge 
of the unglaciated hills, it also approximates Zane’s Trace, the historic pioneer route.
From Shawnee Land to Statehood. Major Indigenous trails con-
verged on the distinctive landscapes of the central Scioto Valley, 
one of them approximating the probable ancient route from here 
to Newark now called the Great Hopewell Road. From their villages 
nearby, the Shawnee undoubtedly understood the clustered hills of 
the Logan Range as significant landmarks, and the concentration

Zane’s Trace. In 1796, Congress awarded an American Revolution-
ary War veteran, Colonel Ebenezer Zane, the contract to open a 
road from Wheeling, West Virginia (then part of Virginia) to Lime-
stone (now Maysville, Kentucky), to encourage settlement and to 
provide an alternative to the Ohio River route. In exchange for his 
road-­building services, Colonel Zane was given land parcels where 
the route crossed the major rivers—­at Zanesville (the Muskingum), 
Lancaster (the Hocking), and Chillicothe (the Scioto). Initially 
just a path in the forest defined mainly by felled trees, the route 
followed established Indigenous trails as much as possible. The 
newly formed state of Ohio began improvements to the route 
after 1803. The stretch approaching Chillicothe from Lancaster 
skirts the hilly edges of the Appalachian Plateau, and from south 
of Kingston it probably corresponds with today’s Marietta Road—­
aiming for Zane’s ferry crossing about where the Bridge Street 
bridge is today.
Tarlton Village. Halfway between Chillicothe and Lancaster along 
this route is one of Ohio’s oldest settlements. The village of Tarl-
ton was founded in 1801 as Newellstown, a principal stop on Zane’s 
Trace, making it one of the earliest commercial and lodging des-
tinations in the area. By 1816, the village had been renamed after 
General Banastre Tarleton, whose British troops were routed by the 
Americans in a 1781 Revolutionary War battle in South Carolina. 
Nye’s Tavern, a two-­story brick building dating from 1840, was an 
important commercial hub; the building still stands along the main 
street near the Zane’s Trace historical marker.
TECUMSEH’S CALL FOR UNITY
Will we not soon be driven from our respective countries and the graves of our 
ancestors? Will not the bones of our dead be plowed up, and their graves be 
turned into fields? . . . Shall we give up our homes, our country, bequeathed 
to us by the Great Spirit, the graves of our dead, and everything that is dear 
and sacred to us, without a struggle? I know you will cry with me: Never! 
Never! Then let us by unity of action destroy them all.
—­Tecumseh, Shawnee Leader

cestors. Centuries earlier, those same hills may have helped inspire 
the earthworks, as the winter view from Mound City’s matching 
profiles seems to suggest. And for the new American settlers and 
their leaders, with their visions of the West and statehood, that 
range of hills symbolized new beginnings—­even as it was soon to 
mean the end for the tribes then living here.
Great Seal State Park. That prominent 
mountain profile is now preserved within a 
1,682-­acre state park (4908 Marietta Rd., Chilli- 
cothe) commemorating Thomas Worthing-
ton and his friends’ inspiration for their new 
state’s official seal. These high sandstone hills 
are laced with twenty-­three miles of hiking, 
biking, and bridle trails. Their upper reaches 
provide views out to the west and north over 
Ohio’s flat, glaciated plains as far as Columbus 
on a clear day, and to the east and south across 
the rugged, forested hills of the Appalachian 
Plateau.
The Great Hopewell Road. The route from here to Newark approx-
imates the probable Great Hopewell Road, an ancient route sug-
gested by recorded remains near its northern end (see pages 96–97). 
In the 1990s, Ohio History Connection archaeologist Brad Lepper 
discovered a nineteenth-­century description confirming at least 
six miles of perfectly straight, parallel embankments extending 
from Newark’s Octagon and aiming precisely for Chillicothe. With 
additional evidence, including 1930s aerial photography and very 
recent LiDAR data, he has hypothesized that a processional way

Tarlton Cross Mound. A shady parking and picnic area north of  
Tarlton village (11615 16th Rd. SW, Stoutsville) is maintained by the 
Fairfield County Park District and open daily from dawn to dusk.  
A small concrete bridge from the Depression-­era Civilian Conser-
vation Corps spans the stream before the path climbs up a wooded 
ridge. The 90-­foot unique earthwork has the shape of a plus sign. 
Its presumed Hopewell origin has not been fully confirmed. The 
design is oddly positioned at the edge of a narrow, sloping ridge, 
and seems to have been shaped in an unusual way by the subtrac-
tion as well as the addition of soil. The downslope was formed by 
the removal of the dirt around it, which was then used to build up 
the cross. Just to the southwest stand four mounds (now mostly  
obscured among the trees) 
forming a perfect square, 
260 feet on its diagonals—­
roughly one-­quarter of the 
standard Hopewell dimen-
sion of 1,054 feet.
The plus-­sign-­shaped Tarlton Cross Mound is reached via a 1930s-­era concrete suspension 
bridge over a quiet stream.
The tall-­pillared Georgian Museum is perhaps the grandest of the many grand nineteenth-­
century mansions on the hill above Lancaster’s downtown.

LANCASTER
Lancaster’s historic center presents an unusual number of opulent 
public buildings and palatial houses, reflecting the town’s prodigious 
past as a transport hub and economic power—­most notably in the 
glass industry.
History and Setting. This beautiful small city was established by 
Ebenezer Zane in 1800, where his ferry carried travelers on his new 
road across the Hocking River. A Wyandotte Indigenous village pre-
ceding it on the site was the home of their well-­known Chief Tarhe, 
called “The Crane.” Many of the early settlers here were the Penn-
sylvania Dutch arriving from Lancaster, Pennsylvania, and they 
transplanted the name. The early days saw signs and newspapers in 
both English and German, and a variety of craft businesses. Canals 
heading west (toward Columbus, 1834) and south (to Athens, 1841) 
spurred growth, which the railroad accelerated after 1854.

World Heritage Route: EAST
World Heritage Route: EAST
Lancaster became a center of glass production, and the home of 
the Anchor Hocking glassware company since 1905. Surrounding 
roads in Fairfield County lead to many historical sites, scenic parks, 
and covered bridges. For visitor information, consult the county’s 
official tourism website (Visit Fairfield County) or stop in their of-
fices at 129 North Broad Street.
Historic Center. The city of Lancaster has a pleasant downtown dis-
trict with many shops, several important museums and historic 
houses, and very impressive public buildings. As one of the orig-
inal Zane’s Trace settlements, and like the much smaller village of 
Somerset (see page 191), it was laid out in the distinctive Pennsyl-
vania manner, with its town square formed by leaving open the 
corners of four adjacent city blocks. Downtown Lancaster has an 
unusually rich collection of fine historic house museums on the 
hills of Main and Wheeling Streets, all within easy walking distance 
of each other and the square. The neighborhood streets are lined 
with many more excellent historic houses.
Three House Museums. The Georgian Museum is a large, period-­
furnished mansion (105 East Wheeling St.) dating from 1832, with 
impressive woodwork and chandeliers, a three-­story cantilevered 
spiral staircase, and interesting antique collections. The Decora-
tive Arts Center of Ohio is a regionally significant arts museum 
housed in the 1835 Reese-­Peters House (145 East Main St.)—­a large, 
The beautiful Reese-­Peters House is now 
home to the Decorative Arts Center of Ohio, 
an important art museum.
Early nineteenth-­century mansions often 
featured cantilevered spiral staircases, like 
this one in Lancaster’s

beautiful, innovative Greek Revival masterwork. Besides perma-
nent collections, it presents temporary exhibitions and art educa-
tion programs. Lancaster’s most famous son, the Civil War general 
William Tecumseh Sherman, was born at what is now the Sherman 
House Museum (137 East Main St.), whose wood-­framed core dates 
from 1811; later brick additions showcase period furnishings and 
Civil War memorabilia. Also in Lancaster’s historic downtown, the 
Ohio Glass Museum (124 West Main St.) offers exhibits on the his-
tory of glassmaking both globally and locally, and showcases works 
and demonstrations by current glass artists.
Stonewall Cemetery. About two miles southwest of downtown Lan-
caster (2375 Stonewall Cemetery Road SW, Lancaster) stands one 
of the most unusual structures in all of Ohio. The huge, chamfered 
blocks forming this open-­air dodecagon’s dry-­laid sandstone walls 
are a marvel of craftsmanship. The tiny park is open dawn to dusk; 
the iron gate is usually kept closed but affords views of the inte-
rior. Nathaniel Wilson began construction of the seven-­foot-­high 
enclosure in 1828, and it was completed by his son after his death. 
The inscription over the entrance refers to Wilson as “one of the 
early Pioneers of the West, who 
emigrated from Cumberland 
County Pennsylvania and set-
tled near this place AD 1798, 
when all around was one con-
tinued and uninhabited wil-
derness.” Of course, Ohio was 
already inhabited, but that was 
not always the earliest settlers’ 
perception.
TO THE HOCKING ROUTE
Heading southeast out of Lancaster reaches the many natural wonders of Ohio’s 
Hocking Hills region, including the diverse natural areas of a multi-­unit state park, 
many picturesque tourism accommodations, and the historic towns of Logan and 
Nelsonville. Beyond are Athens, founded in 1801 as the home of Ohio University, and 
two villages with significant mounds—­Zaleski and The Plains (see map, page 174).

The exquisite Greek Revival Avery-­Downer House 
graces Broadway in the picturesque 
New England–­flavored college town of Granville.
The Alligator Mound, above Granville, emerges best from its hilltop site under low-­angled sunlight.
BUCKEYE LAKE
The Buckeye Lake resort area hugs the northwestern shores of the 
huge reservoir built as the high-­point gravity feeder for the Ohio 
and Erie Canal; its waters filled the canal both south to the  
Ohio River and north to Lake Erie.
Millersport. This small village, located just a bit east of State Route 
37 between Lancaster and Granville, was laid out in 1825 adjacent to 
the canal system’s new Licking Summit Reservoir (as Buckeye Lake 
was originally called). For the reservoir to feed the south-­flowing 
canal, workers had to cut a two-­mile-­long, sixty-­foot-­deep trench 
through the ridge south of town. Its arrow-­straight course remains 
one of early Ohio’s most impressive engineering feats. Deep Cut 
Road parallels this marvel, which is now wooded so visible only 
in winter. With its canal-­side position, the town of Millersport be-
came an important trading center. A well-­preserved section of the 
canal (on its way to the Deep Cut) 
runs through the center of town 
today, serving its new role as a 
fishing resort on Buckeye Lake. 
(For more on Ohio’s canal system, 
see Circleville’s Canal Park, page 
151.)
Buckeye Lake History. The new, the old, and the odd characterize 
the settlements along the constructed northern embankment of 
Buckeye Lake. The long history of this high-­ground reservoir be-
gins in the early 1800s when swamps here were drained and lined 
partially with huge limestone slabs taken from a large American 
Indian mound nearby. This was the highest point on the Ohio and 
Erie Canal, 317 feet above Lake Erie and 413 feet above the Ohio 
River. This meant, with the completion of the Deep Cut, that grav-
ity could feed water through the entire canal in both directions. By 
1900, the canals had been long abandoned, but there were lively 
amusement parks along the lakeshore. A state park and historical 
museum now tell the stories, and boat tours take visitors to the 
lake’s famed Cranberry Bogs.
GRANVILLE
This small college town was founded 
by New Englanders eager to create an 
environment that would help them 
feel at home; its exquisite historical 
ambience is very well preserved.
Historic Center. Broadway in Granville blends the character of a New 
England village, a college town, and an old Midwestern main street, 
with charming ambience, good shops, and cafés. The exceptional 
architecture includes the Greek Revival masterpiece Avery-­Downer 
House (now the Robbins Hunter Museum, 221 East Broadway), and 
several beautiful churches include at least one in a proper New  
England mode. Either of two excellent historic lodgings—­the Gran-
ville Inn or the Buxton Inn—­can provide a pleasant base for visiting 
all of central Ohio. Granville is also the home of Denison Univer-
sity, occupying the prominent hilltop above the village with its  
meandering drives and imposing academic buildings.
Alligator Mound. The greatest animal effigy mound in Ohio after 
Serpent Mound crowns a hill just east of Granville. Preserved by 
the Ohio History Connection, it is now supplied with a histori-
cal marker (across from 388 Bryn Du Dr., Granville). The crea-
ture sprawls across the grassy summit of a residential cul-­de-­sac 
at the end of a long, winding lane. The subtle undulations of the  
creature’s head and paws are best seen in the deep shadows of very 
early or very late sunlight. Coming out of the animal’s side is an 
The artificial valley called the “Deep Cut” 
carries the canal through a two-­mile-­
wide ridge south of Millersport.
