UNDERWATER PANTHER
This earthen creature with four rounded feet and a long, curling tail 
became known to early settlers as the “alligator,” but there are more likely 
possibilities for what it represented to the Indigenous people who created 
it. The spiraling tail closely resembles that of its Adams County counterpart, 
Serpent Mound, suggesting it may be instead the Underwater Panther who, 
like the Great Serpent, was a powerful creature of the watery Beneath World 
of American Indian cosmology. The Underwater Panther was said to have 
dark, dangerous powers, and a long tail that could unleash canoe-­swamping 
whirlpools.
NAMING GRANVILLE’S ALLIGATOR
Archaeologist Brad Lepper suggests how a conversation between an 
early settler and a Native person might have given Granville’s Alligator 
its improbable name: What if one of those pioneers had asked a Native 
American what that was. And if that Native American had used their own 
term—­the Ojibway term is “Mishewayshee”—­for Underwater Panther, and 
the European American was still struggling with 
the language and was like, “Underwater panther? 
Panthers don’t live underwater. What do you 
mean?” And if the Native American had said, “That 
spirit, that creature that lives in the water with a 
long tail and sometimes eats people,” the European 
would have said, “Oh, you mean an alligator!” 
(Link to video is on page 205.)
elevated, stone-­covered circle—­an altar where many fires burned. 
The views from this hilltop extend westward to the open plains be-
yond Granville, and eastward into the defined valley terraces where 
the Newark Earthworks were laid out. The builders of the effigy no 
doubt appreciated the prominence of this spot in encompassing the 
dual ecologies, and especially the nearby geometric earthworks—­
already 800 years old when this effigy was begun.
NEWARK
Today, the seat of Licking County surrounds the confluence of three 
upper tributaries of the Muskingum River. In antiquity, this valley 
held the largest geometric earthwork complex in the world. Two of 
its most majestic features survive.
History and Setting. The city of Newark was first settled in 1802, 
after General William Schenck surveyed the land for Revolution-
ary War veterans John and George Burnett and John Cummings 
as part of the United States Military District—­a land-­bestowing ar-
rangement for veterans similar to the Virginia Military District in 
southwest Ohio. Schenck named the place after his hometown of 
Newark, New Jersey. The town’s economy grew rapidly after 1825, 
with the construction of the Ohio and Erie Canal and its nearby 
Licking Summit Reservoir (Buckeye Lake).
Historic Downtown. Newark’s historic center is anchored by a 
well-­preserved Courthouse Square, with a good selection of shops 
and restaurants nearby. The former Home Building Association 
Bank—­a tiny jewel of a building from 1915—­is one of eight banks 
across the Midwest designed by the internationally famous early 
modern architect Louis Sullivan (1 North Third St.). Nearby, The 
Works Museum (55 South First St.) tells the history of Newark and 
its immediate region, from its earliest times (flint mining and 
mastodons) to its robust industrial and glassmaking heritage as the 
home of Heisey Glass and Owens Corning. Glassblowing demon-
strations and regular planetarium shows reinforce the museum’s 
science and industry themes. Information on the many attractions 
and accommodations in Licking 
County is available from the vis-
itor bureau’s office or the Explore 
Licking County website.
Exhibits in downtown Newark’s 
Works Museum tell the city’s stories, 
with special emphasis on its 
industrial and glassmaking heritage.

World Heritage Route: EAST
World Heritage Route: EAST
Two brothers named Salisbury made the most accurate survey of the Newark Earthworks 
in 1862, not long before most of the site’s features disappeared.
THE SALISBURY BROTHERS
James and Charles Salisbury surveyed the Newark Earthworks at the time 
of the American Civil War. Brad Lepper explains who they were: James and 
Charles Salisbury were interesting, and we know quite a lot about James, 
very little about Charles, other than he was James’s brother. Dr. James 
Salisbury was a physician and was wildly famous and popular for having a 
fad diet called the Salisbury diet, which consisted of drinking a lot of boiled 
water and eating a lot of protein—­meat. He invented Salisbury steak in fact. 
He . . . was a Civil War doctor; many of his patients were former veterans 
of the Civil War who’d subsisted on a steady diet of 
hardtack and coffee, and their digestive systems 
had been ravaged. So he invented Salisbury steak 
as sort of an easily digestible form of diet for them, 
but it became very popular of course. We all get 
Salisbury steak today. So he was a physician, but a 
dedicated antiquarian. And many of the dedicated 
antiquarians of this period were physicians, for one 
reason or another. (Link to video is on page 205.)
The Cherry Valley. When the first settlers came to this area around 
1800, they found huge numbers of mature cherry trees. Within 
a few generations, the trees had all been harvested and sent to 
Cincinnati to be turned into furniture, but the question remains 
how this concentration came to be. Cherries may have been part 
of the ancient feasts and ceremonies that brought people here to 
the earthworks. Perhaps they were grown here for that purpose, 
or they proliferated from celebrants disposing of the pits—­thereby 
seeding the huge groves that thrived for centuries. Though the 
trees are gone, the “Cherry Valley” name now applies to roads and 
businesses, including a very pleasant resort hotel.
The Newark Earthworks. The level plain southwest of downtown, 
across Raccoon Creek, was once covered by more than four square 
miles of elaborate and precise earthen architecture—­the largest geo-
metric earthwork complex in the world. Enormous enclosures were 
connected by walled roadways and linked to the surrounding water-
ways. Finding them buried in the forests, awed settlers described, 
measured, and mapped these mysterious monuments throughout 
the 1800s, even as most were being taken down for the growth of 
the town, its industries, and its infrastructure. But thanks to the fore-
sight of the town’s citizens, two of the most impressive features from 
ancient Newark remain today—­the Great Circle and the Octagon 
Earthworks—­helping visitors grasp the literally unbelievable scope, 
beauty, and precision of this unique ancient landscape. The impact 
of these monuments on visitors today is still stunning; they create an 
architectural experience like no other on Earth.
The Ancient Landscape. Mature oak-­hickory forests covered most of 
Ohio in antiquity and up to the time of settlement. But excavations 
at the Great Circle and other earthwork sites have shown that large 
prairie openings were also here, both naturally and with human 
maintenance. Open spaces would have been an essential feature of 
earthwork sites—­to facilitate any astronomical sightings as well as 
earthmoving and construction. Open prairie landscapes may have 
extended across the Newark area for hundreds or thousands of years 
before the earthworks. All hunter-­gatherer societies know that main-
taining such openings increases plant diversity, and the density and 
variety of game animals. A naturally occurring wetland in the center 
of town (still detectable as low-­lying land between the Octagon and 
Great Circle) would also have helped keep the forest at bay.

91
World Heritage Route
World Heritage Route
The Octagon’s huge interior is formed by eight perfectly straight, perfectly eye-­level walls.
Every 18.6 years, the Moon returns to its northernmost rising position, aligned with the 
central axis of Newark’s Octagon Earthworks.
OCTAGON EARTHWORKS
This huge perfect circle and adjoining open-­cornered octagon were 
built with precise geometries and level, artificial horizons—­used in 
antiquity to mark the complex movements in the positions of moon-
rises and moonsets over an 18.6-­year cycle.
Orientation. A small residential lane off North 30th Street leads to a 
parking area (125 North 33rd St., upper left on the Salisbury map), 
situated between the long walls of this astonishing site’s Observatory 
Circle and Octagon. The site is open during daylight hours, and a for-
mer golf clubhouse serves as the Ohio History Connection’s visitor 
center. A thorough walk of the grounds will take at least one hour. 
From the elevated viewing platform near the parking area, follow the 
giant circle to the left, as far as the Observatory Mound—­the point 
from which the most important lunar alignment occurs along the 
site’s principal axis. The Octagon’s elegant, level-­topped walls and 
open gateways wrap the opposite end of the site, as far as the terraces 
dropping toward Raccoon Creek, and all the way to 30th Street.
History. As the Hopewell architects’ most geometrically refined 
achievement, the Octagon Earthworks may be among the last of 
their projects. But within a few generations, the moonrise align-
ment vistas to which it is calibrated would have been engulfed  
by the returning forest. When the first colonial settlers came into 
the area around 1800, the local tribes, many just recently arrived in 
Ohio, knew or told little about these strange tree-­covered earthen 
lines. From the beginning, their mystery and size fascinated some 
Newark citizens, but many people only wanted to loot the mounds, 
or cut them down to build houses or factories. The Octagon was 
one of two major parts of the earthworks preserved by the citizens 
of Newark as public lands considered adaptable to other uses; the 
Octagon became a state militia camp briefly, and then a golf course 
for over a century.

World Heritage Route: EAST
World Heritage Route: EAST
THE MOONRISE EXPERIENCE
Fall 2005 offered the first opportunity for anyone to witness an axial 
moonrise at the Octagon following its discovery. Newark historian Jeff 
Gill describes the experience of being there: We went out, and I’ve got to 
tell you, we had been anticipating that moment for over fifteen years. And 
it still made the hair on the back of my neck go up to actually see the Moon 
rise, and to stop and think: We don’t normally watch moonrises. The Sun, 
we know when it rises, and it gives clear notice of its coming. The moonrise 
is a completely different beast: It sneaks up on you, and you don’t know 
it’s happening, unless somehow you know it’s 
happening and even then, when it happens, it’s just 
“ohh.” And so there we stood, there on the central 
alignment, and looking along where those walls 
said to us, whispered across the centuries: “There it 
will happen.” And when it did, it was just one of the 
most memorable moments of my life. (Link to video 
is on page 205.)
REGAINING ANCIENT KNOWLEDGE
Two professors from Earlham College, Ray Hively and Robert Horn, 
rediscovered the Octagon’s alignments in 1974, albeit inadvertently. They 
surveyed the site expecting to find solar alignments but instead found only 
the lunar ones—­all eight of them. The Moon’s long, complex cycle was not so 
thoroughly encoded by any other ancient monument on Earth. Ray Hively 
describes the significance of this achievement: The fact that this structure 
simultaneously encodes geometrical and astronomical information is an 
astounding and highly motivating discovery for any society that makes it. Even 
today, modern physicists are motivated in a sense by the same kind of dual de-
sire: that is, the desire, the passion in fact, to find a correspondence between 
mathematical symmetry and natural phenomena. That is a very powerful 
discovery, and I think that we’re dealing with something like that here.
1 Viewing Platform. A small, elevated platform near the park-
ing lot offers an orientation to the site, with explanatory signage 
and views over the earthen walls into the avenue connecting the 
giant Observatory Circle (at left) with the open-­cornered Octagon 
(at right). The precision of the walls is readily apparent, with their 
level crests and perfect profiles. The circle’s 1,054-­foot diameter is 
exact to within inches for its entire circumference. The dimensions 
of the two figures are perfectly harmonized: A square with sides 
equal to the circle’s diameter would fit exactly within the octagon’s 
four axial and cross-­axial gateways; another pinpoints the centers 
of four of its mounds. The circle may be entered through a modern 
entrance adjacent to the visitor center building.
2 Observatory Mound. The once-­in-­a-­generation moonrise align-
ment explains the position of Observatory Mound at the end of 
the site’s main axis. Yet it sits atop an unusual detail: Two halves of 
the circle don’t quite meet; instead they curve gently and appear to 
slide beneath the mound, emerging on the outside as small twin 
tails. The form suggests that a former en-
trance gateway—­a smaller-­scale version of 
the one at the Great Circle across town—­
was at some point covered over by the 
large mound, leaving only a small notch.
The Long Lunar Cycle. The movements of the Moon are complex. 
Nowadays, we notice that the Moon has different phases over about 
twenty-­nine days. But the builders of the earthworks noticed much 
more complicated lunar patterns: About every four weeks its ris-
ing position swings back and forth between the southeast and the 
northeast, but the width of that angle expands very slowly over 
about nine and one-­third years, and then contracts again at the 
same rate. The Moon’s setting position moves the same way across 
the western horizon. This creates eight points where the moonrise 
or set position appears to reverse direction, and all eight are marked 
precisely by these earthen walls and gateways. The northernmost 
moonrise aligns with the long central axis of the Circle and Octagon 
over a few consecutive months every 18.6 years. The design tells us 
this was probably their grandest and most anticipated festival.
Squier and Davis’s drawing of the Observatory Mound  
illustrates its apparent imposition over an earlier gateway.

the adjacent Octagon; and to set 
many dimensions at other sites 
throughout the region.
4 Raccoon Creek Terraces. The far northernmost sides of the Octa-
gon offer clear views of the natural terraces stepping down to Rac-
coon Creek. The geometric earthworks of the Ohio Valley region 
were always built on these high second terraces above the rivers, 
providing perfectly level surfaces atop well-­drained, gravelly soils. 
The steep, exposed slopes between the terraces suggest that the 
people came here for easy access to the soil needed to build the 
earthworks: Instead of digging it up out of holes, they could just 
pull it off the steep hillside into their baskets.
5 Octagon Gateways. The distance across the Octagon is even 
greater than that of the Observatory Circle, so viewing from one 
gateway to its opposite can be a perceptual stretch, especially with 
today’s trees, or when the air is hazy. Yet sighting along these walls, 
and across the space to the horizons beyond, was a major function 
of the site. At the Octagon’s cleverly designed gateways, the preci-
sion of this monumental architecture is at its most apparent: eight 
perfectly flat-­topped, slightly taller mounds block the vistas out-
ward from each of the Octagon’s open corners. We are both con-
tained and released, with subtle shifts in these effects as we move 
around the interior.
Geometric Precision. Geometrical 
harmonies exist not only between 
the Octagon and its connected 
circle, but farther out across the 
Newark complex as a whole. The 
centers of the Observatory Circle 
GRANDMOTHER MOON
Nipahuma, our mother who goes by night, the first mother, the mother of all 
mothers, nurtured her children, and then when her purpose was complete 
she returned to the spirit world; but before she left she told first man and 
woman that she would never forget them. She continues to watch over us at 
night as the Moon. The children promised to remember Grandmother Moon 
whenever she appeared in the Sky.
—­Hitakonanu’laxk, Lenape Storyteller
The Moon is a woman. We identify her as “she.” One of the Canadian sisters 
has taught me that when our people go, our females, and that Moon is full, 
they’re dancing around the Moon. And that’s when we have our ceremony, 
and we talk to them, and we send them our prayers, so they can take them on 
to the Creator. But every time I see the full Moon, I think about, you know, our 
Ancestors—­my mother and all of them, dancing around the Moon. And that’s 
a good thought, a good thought.
—­Linda Poolaw, Delaware Grand Council Chief
The Octagon’s main axis and one of its walls 
are aligned to the Moon’s northern and 
southern maximum rise-­points; other key 
lunar rise and set positions are aligned along 
other walls and between gateways.
The Octagon Earthworks’ perfect geometry 
is harmonized by a dimension of 1,054 feet—­
the Observatory Circle’s diameter—­a unit 
widely used in Hopewell earthworks and 
abbreviated today as the “OCD.”

here (doubled to the diameter of 1,054 feet), or others matching it, 
apparently got a lot of use—­to set the position and main corners of

ANCIENT GENIUS
The people who built these mounds were brilliant. Their genius lies in 
combining complexity and simplicity simultaneously. Their mathematical 
and astronomical complexities challenge our mental capacity, while 
simultaneously their simple (forms) evoke a calming, soothing, and in some 
instances spiritual effect.
—­Glenna J. Wallace, Chief of the Eastern Shawnee Tribe of Oklahoma
An aerial view of Newark’s Great Circle Earthworks under winter snow reveals its central 
mound and its large gateway at the upper right.
and the Great Circle are six OCD’s apart, as are the centers of the 
octagon and the square. Imaginary parallel lines joined the corners 
of the square and those of the octagon, more than a mile away. In-
credibly, these two sets of long parallels also point to the minimum 
and maximum southern moonrises. Circles and squares also share 
matching areas and perimeters. These startling relationships re-
veal a deep mastery of empirical geometry, and they suggest the 
whole complex may have been conceived according to a single uni-
fying idea rather than through incremental additions.
6 Small Circle. The southeastern gate of the Octagon opens near 
a beautiful small circular enclosure, the best-­preserved earthwork 
of its kind. It likely corresponds to the plan of a preceding timber 
structure—­perhaps a woodhenge, or an arbor like the one found at 
Fort Hill (see page 146). Nineteenth-­century plans of the Newark 
complex show many such small circles, especially along the road 
that headed east from the Octagon. Surviving low embankment 
walls (nearby next to 30th St.) once continued into a passage con-
necting the Octagon with the square—­part of the complete system 
of low walls surrounding and linking all the major components of 
the Newark complex.
7 The Great Hopewell Road. The wall fragment nearest the small 
circle is the beginning of another road. As shown on the Salisbury 
map, it made a couple quick bends and then headed off in a per-
fectly straight alignment to the southwest toward Chillicothe—­the 
Hopewell culture’s other greatest ritual center—­probably arriv-
ing somewhere between the Hopeton Earthworks and the Logan 
Range. Brad Lepper’s hypothesis that this was a pilgrimage route 
was inspired by his discovery of the Salisbury brothers’ 1862 map 
and their accompanying description, in which they told of having 
followed the pair of earthen lines for at least six miles from this 
spot. Other nineteenth-­century witnesses recorded them for even 
longer distances and always aligned in the same precise direc-
tion—­an “undeviating course” as the Salisburys put it.
GREAT CIRCLE EARTHWORKS
Standing majestically in a peaceful city park, this gigantic circu-
lar enclosure and its grand gateway are enhanced by a continuous 
inner ditch, doubling their apparent height, and by a beautifully 
undulating mound in the center.
Orientation. City streets connect the Octagon Earthworks with the 
Great Circle, about a mile away (455 Hebron Rd., Heath; bottom 
center on the Salisbury map). The park is open daylight hours 
and has three parking areas. For the small museum’s open times, 
check the Ohio History Connection’s website. The main southeast-
ern parking lot lies between two giant borrow pits—­the sources of 


--- PDF PAGE 57 ---

98
World Heritage Route: EAST
World Heritage Route: EAST
The Great Circle’s monumental gateway platform is framed by the site’s tallest walls and 
deepest ditches.
much of the soil used to build the earthwork. A paved path rises 
toward the museum, directly opposite the Great Circle’s monumen-
tal gateway. In the circle’s center is a symmetrical mound with two 
wings, called “Eagle Mound.” In the open parkland to the north and 
east are two reconstructed segments of the low walls that once sur-
rounded the entire Newark complex.
History. The circle’s design is typical of many earlier Adena earth-
works—­a ring with an interior ditch and gateway toward the east—­
suggesting it may be the oldest element of the Newark complex. 
From its first recorded sighting by American settlers, the Great 
Circle has been marveled at, preserved, and renamed, through 
uses both recreational and military. Early settlers called it the Old 
Fort when they found it in the forest, although when Caleb Atwater 
described it in 1820, he astutely noted that having the “moat” on 
the inside would not be much help for defense. Soon after, it be-
came the Licking County Fairgrounds and was later used to muster 
troops for the Civil War. Spectacles continued into the early twenti-
eth century. In the 1930s, the Civilian Conservation Corps removed 
the amusement park structures and restored the earthworks to 
their mid-­1800s dimensions.
1 Museum and Bronze Model. The 1970s museum building stands 
on the axis of the circle and faces it across a small plaza, where a 
bronze tabletop model depicts the Newark Earthworks complex as 
mapped by Squier and Davis in the 1840s. It helps visitors connect 
what they are seeing (the grand gateway of the Great Circle) with 
the entire four-­square-­mile scope of the ancient complex, includ-
ing the surviving fragment of the square and the Octagon Earth-
works across town. The museum offers a mural, interactive video 
program, and other interpretive materials.
2 Grand Gateway. From the museum’s small plaza, the monumen-
tal entrance to the Great Circle presents a dramatic scene. Here, 
people coming from other parts of the complex, along the several 
parallel-­walled roadways, would enter one of the most dramatic 
entry portals anywhere in their cultural world—­a grand act of pas-
sage preparing them for the ceremonies at the circle’s heart. Pass-
ing across this platform, between the site’s tallest walls and deepest 
ditches, creates a monumental threshold experience comparable 
to or even exceeding Fort Ancient’s South Gate.

SPIRIT BARRIERS
Robert Hall, in his 1997 book, An Archaeology of the Soul: North American 
Indian Belief and Ritual, describes the widespread Indigenous tradition 
that water can block the passage of spirits—­souls of the dead or other 
manifestations are considered unable to pass through or over water. Water 
formed into a circular shape (as at the Great Circle), or along a boundary 
(as at Fort Ancient), could be especially powerful and provide a protected 
zone. Part of the water’s effectiveness may also have been its reflective 
surface: Taboos about mirrors that can capture a spirit or soul are 
widespread across many global cultures.
Water-­Filled Ditch. The circle’s inner ditch effectively doubles the 
monumental impact of the walls. New evidence suggests that it was 
designed to hold water, like the ditch or pit features at other sites. 
It may evoke that watery Beneath World or, since it is continuous 
here, another Indigenous tradition—­the Spirit Barrier. The ancient 
clay and limestone lining apparently held water for centuries, be-
cause at least two witnesses reported water standing in it after 1800. 
But in the long run, maintenance would have been required, be-
cause the subsoil here is hundreds of feet of glacial sand and gravel 
into which any amount of water would otherwise just vanish—­as it 
does today. (At Mounds State Park in Anderson, Indiana, described 
on pages 116–117, a miniature version of the Great Circle still holds 
water in its interior ditch.)
3 Eagle Mound. An elegantly undulating mound lies at the center of 
the Great Circle. The settlers named it Eagle Mound for its winged 
shape. Excavations in 1928 revealed the posthole pattern of a long 
building with screen walls extending from its two eastern corners. 
Inside, a rectangular clay basin held fires, a sign of ritual activity, 
although unlike the grand lodges found at other sites, no human 
remains were laid here. Scraps of shiny mica littered one end of 
the floor. When they finished using the building, the people filled 
the fire basin with white sand, leaving little else behind except a 
pair of copper shapes. They dismantled the wooden structure, or 
burned it, and covered its floor with Earth. The mound’s shape was 
restored after the excavations.
4 Circular Wall. An excavation through the Great Circle wall in 
1992 suggested how the construction may have been done. First, 
the builders placed a set of point mounds around in a circle. Then 
they dug the ditch and formed its reddish-­brown soil into a ring. 
Finally, they brought yellow gravelly clay from deep pits nearby and 
placed it along the circular wall’s interior surface. Building tech-
niques probably had both spiritual and practical meaning: The yel-
low clay smoothed and supported the inner wall and helped direct 
water into the ditch, but the color, if it were somehow maintained 
in its exposed condition, would also have made for a more dramatic 
ritual space. The combination of reddish soil on one side of a wall 
and yellowish on the other was also used at several other Hopewell 
earthworks—­suggesting shared meanings perhaps as important as 
the watery circle itself.
The winged form of Eagle Mound covers the posthole pattern of a ceremonial building, 
oriented toward the earthwork’s northeastern gateway.
When it still held water, the Great Circle’s interior ditch would have created a continuous 
mirror image of the earthen wall, and the sky beyond.

EARTH, SOIL
These people built all their durable monuments out of soil—­the Earth—­one 
basket load at a time. But this was clearly a lot more than just dirt for them. 
For one thing, they understood it very, very well—­the exact properties of 
different types: for good drainage, or for holding water, or for maintaining 
different slopes. They often layered it in different colors or textures, or mixed 
it with sand or gravel. Sometimes they burned it to make it red. But they also 
knew the soil for reasons beyond just its physical or aesthetic properties: It 
was the Earth itself, and full of meaning. It gives life; it consumes the dead. 
And in the earthworks, it marked their understanding of the heavens, of their 
community, and of their territory.
Low embankment walls, like this one 
restored in the park just north of the 
Great Circle, once formed the Newark 
Earthworks’ continuous perimeter.
The large Fairmount Mound and a small church 
stand together in a cemetery southeast of 
Newark, not far from the Dawes Arboretum.
Wright Earthworks. Few other remnants of the ancient Newark 
Earthworks complex survive. Not far from the Great Circle (158 
James St.), two short roughly perpendicular walls are preserved—­a 
fragment of one of the square’s sides and part of the connector wall 
once leading toward the ellipse. The walls seem small, so when we 
imagine the complete square—­about the size of nine city blocks—­
their function as an “enclosure” must have been very subtle. The 
destruction began here as early as the 1820s, when the Ohio and 
Erie Canal came through. Less than twenty years later, Squier and 
Davis reported, “The ancient lines can now be traced only at in-
tervals, among gardens and outhouses. . . . A few years hence, the 
residents upon this spot will be compelled to resort to this map, 
to ascertain the character of the works which occupied the very 
ground upon which they stand.”
Cherry Valley Ellipse. Nothing survives of ancient Newark’s burial 
ground. An elliptical enclosure northeast of the square, it was the 
cemetery of the earthwork’s builders, comparable in both function 
and grandeur to Mound City. Eleven conical mounds, plus a small 
ring and a rectangular platform, surrounded a large, irregular, con-
joined mound at the center. One set of burials here was covered in 
sheets of gleaming mica; another held the extraordinary “Shaman 
of Newark” figurine. Canal construction, then the railroad and its 
adjacent factories, destroyed the ellipse and its mounded tombs 
very early. Old records show that all this 
destruction unearthed large quantities 
of objects made of copper and other 
precious materials—­now all lost.
Construction Methods. Building the Newark complex eighteen centu-
ries ago required moving more than seven million cubic feet of Earth. 
It would have taken several generations, or an enormous number of 
workers, or both. Except where there was an unusually convenient 
source, like those soil cliffs along Raccoon Creek beneath the Octagon, 
mining and transporting Earth was a lot of work. They extracted it with 
pointed digging sticks, or hoes made with a deer shoulder blade tied 
onto a handle, and then transported it from its source in single basket 
loads. The apparent requirements for specific soil types—­each for its 
color, texture, or permeability—­could mean bringing it in from several 
locations, and over considerable distances. With the small popula-
tions, even during organized gatherings, we must assume that every-
body was somehow involved in these massive public works projects.
5 Processional Ways. Stone steps and a bridge (built during the site’s 
entertainment days) cross the northern arc of the Great Circle into 
open parkland. To the northeast, two long sections of embankment 
walls have been restored. Low walls like these once encircled the en-
tire Newark complex, as shown on the Salisbury map. Without crossing 
these lines, celebrants had only three points where they could enter, 
and all three suggest ceremonial approaches from water. Once inside, 
the people were channeled from one specialized enclosure to another, 
among the elements of what Brad Lepper has called “a gigantic ritual 
machine.” To imagine the crowds—­pilgrims and their leaders—­moving 
in processions from one space to another suggests the reenactment of 
a flow of power, or elaborate multi-­stage rituals reflecting fundamen-
tal, cosmological cycles.

World Heritage Route: EAST
THE SHAMAN OF NEWARK
The only work of Hopewell artistry known to be from the Cherry Valley 
Ellipse is an extraordinary fist-­sized figure of a spiritual leader dressed in 
bear skin regalia and holding a human head in his lap. The bear’s head 
is on his head, and the bear’s claws are on his hands. He’s wearing a pair 
of the large Hopewell-­style ear spools, as is the decapitated head. The 
figure has no base, meaning there is no way to set it down; it was apparently 
designed to be held in the hands whenever it was out in the open. The 
position of the bear’s face on top of the head allows anyone holding the 
figure to enact its transformation—­from human to bear and back again—­
simply by tilting it slightly up or down. (Out of respect for Indigenous 
sacred patrimony, no image is provided here.)
Dawes Arboretum. Six miles south of downtown Newark (7770 Jack-
sontown Rd., Newark), the 2,000-­acre Dawes Arboretum presents 
a landscape wonderland. Hiking and driving loops lead among 
tree and plant specimens of all kinds—­including a native bald-­
cypress swamp, a Japanese garden, and a large restored wetland. 
One trail (requiring special permission) leads past a small mound. 
Daweswood, the 1867 home of the park’s founders, is now a mu-
seum. About three miles south and east of Dawes Arboretum, just 
off old US Route 40, stands the large Adena-­era Fairmount Mound 
(9161 Fairmount Rd., Thornville). At fifteen feet high and eighty feet 
in diameter, it impressively crowns a high ridge, along with the pic-
turesque Fairmount Church and a large cemetery. It has not been 
excavated.
TO THE MUSKINGUM ROUTE
Travel scenic roads to the important Hopewell earthworks preserved in the town of 
Marietta—­itself a significant historical settlement. Along the way, near Newark, visit 
Blackhand Gorge and the Flint Ridge Ancient Quarries; then Glenford Fort earthworks 
and Somerset. The rest of the route follows the Muskingum River (with its historic 
hand-­operated lock system), via McConnelsville and Stockport, to its confluence with 
the Ohio River at Marietta (see map, page 188).
4
Upper Miami Route
Head north out of Lebanon to reach ancient, scenic, and historic 
sites along the upper reaches of the Little Miami River, especially 
the Great Council State Park, the spectacular Clifton Gorge, the Pol-
lock Earthworks, and the unique towns of Waynesville and Yellow 
Springs. The greater Dayton area, besides its world-­famous aviation 
heritage, boasts the restored Fort Ancient–­era SunWatch Village. 
Nearby on a hill overlooking the Great Miami River at Miamisburg 
is Ohio’s largest Adena mound.

Upper Miami Route
Nearly anything that’s old is available 
among Waynesville’s many furniture, 
antique, and specialty shops.
Larger than life, a bronze statue of the Shawnee leader Tecumseh  
(by sculptor Alan Cottrill, 2024) welcomes visitors to Great Council State Park.
Waynesville. About nine miles northeast 
of Lebanon is the well-­known “antiques 
capital” of Waynesville. Well-­preserved 
historic buildings line Main Street, 
housing cafés and shops that have made 
this a major destination for antique and 
furniture buyers. More than fifty dealers include some with various 
specialties. Just outside of town is Caesar Creek State Park (8570 SR 
73, Waynesville), surrounding a 2,830-­acre lake with boating and a 
swimming beach. The rest of the park has forests, waterfalls, and 
cliff faces known for rare geological specimens and fossils. Between 
Waynesville and Xenia, leave the US Route 68 bypass to visit the vil-
lage of Spring Valley, where several picturesque buildings are pre-
served, some with unique local establishments. Nearby are canoe 
and kayak liveries along the Little Miami River (inquire locally).
Xenia. Much of the historic center of nearby Xenia (founded in 
1803) was destroyed by a catastrophic tornado in 1974—­with notable 
holdouts like the tall-­towered Greene County Courthouse, and the  
unusual Dodds Memorials building a block west (a lavishly orna-
mented transplant from the 1904 St. Louis World’s Fair, at 123 West 
Main St.). A few elegant period houses survive along East Second 
Street, and more along North King Street. Two miles northeast, in 
an outer subdivision, stands the Kinsey Road Mound—­a small, rare 
Adena mound still encircled by a ditch and ring. A mowed clear-
ing at the end of a residential cul-­de-­sac (next to 968 Mound Court, 
Xenia) leads to the low mound. The approach crosses over the best-­
preserved section of the surrounding ring and ditch; the rest is some-
what obscured by trees but most evident toward the left. Few such 
mound-­and-­ring combinations survive, the largest and finest being 
Marietta’s Conus Mound.
GREAT COUNCIL STATE PARK
This Ohio state park, in the tiny village of Oldtown, commemorates 
one of the several seats of the Shawnee Tribe during the time of 
settlement; a beautiful museum tells the stories of the Shawnee 
people—­here in Ohio and today in Oklahoma.
History and Setting. Around 1780, the Shawnee Tribe’s principal 
town (or chillicothe—­the home of their chief and council house) 
stood here, three miles north of Xenia near the banks of the Little 
Miami River. A state park at Oldtown (a translation of “Old Chilli- 
cothe”) commemorates the site (1580 US Route 68, Xenia). The 
Shawnee had been displaced twice from previous locations in the 
Ohio Country by encroaching settlement, and they were forced to 
move again after just three years here. (Local lore that this was the 
birthplace of their great leader, Tecumseh, is now in doubt.) The 
tribe’s council house once stood on the high ridge behind the vis-
itor center. Between the museum and the river, trails take visitors 
out among restored prairie landscapes, ecological displays linking 
past and present, and the extensive traces of the town plan revealed 
by remote sensing archaeology.
Shawnee Tribal Exhibits. Today there are three Shawnee Tribal Na-
tions based in Oklahoma. The Eastern Shawnee Tribe of Oklahoma, 
the Absentee Shawnee Tribe, and the Shawnee Tribe all collaborated 
on the Great Council State Park’s museum and its exhibits, which not 
only recount their Ancestors’ lives here and throughout Ohio, but also 
offer an expansive treatment of the region’s American Indian history, 
the lives of their great leaders, their encounters with early explorers 
like Simon Kenton and Daniel Boone, early trade relations, and the 
ensuing conflicts over land and settlement. The tragedies of forced 
assimilation and Indian Removal from Ohio are also covered, and 
stereotypes about Native Americans are persuasively challenged. The 
vitality of today’s tribes is presented with pride and celebration.

Upper Miami Route
Upper Miami Route
stone cliffs, rock shelters, a large Adena mound, and an impressive 
waterfall.
Orientation. Indian Mound Reserve, a Greene County park west of 
Cedarville, offers an extensive trail system. To reach the earthworks 
and mound, use the parking area adjacent to a restored log cabin 
(US Route 42 and Williamson Ln., Cedarville). Signs explain the var-
ious trails: One leads to the large, Adena-­era Williamson Mound on 
a hill beyond the creek, and another follows the gorge to an impres-
sive waterfall. The trail to the Hopewell enclosure heads east from 
the parking lot for about 1,300 feet, where it rises through one of 
the earthwork’s three prominent gateways. The earthen walls, the 
surrounding cliffs, and the creek are in dense forest, so the best 
time to visit is from October to April.
The Earthworks. The wall’s four 
segments close off the western 
end of a plateau which, for the 
rest of its perimeter, drops off 
over sheer cliffs above small rock 
shelters. For many years, beginning in 1981, archaeologist Bob  
Riordan worked with his Wright State University students to un-
cover how the Hopewell builders created this embankment wall. 
First, around 50 CE, they built up a low earthen wall across the ap-
proach to the plateau, extending it about 300 feet between the creek 
bluff on the north and the start of the limestone cliff on the south; 
they left one gateway in the center. It was carefully built: A thin ini-
tial layer of clay was laid down to prepare the surface, and then soil 
was brought in. Over about 100 more years, they made two separate 
additions to the wall, making it higher and leaving two more gate-
ways. The earthwork’s outer surfaces contained a lot of stone, and 
the gateway passages were paved and lined with limestone blocks.
THE SHAWNEE AND OHIO
Although her Ancestors were forcibly removed from Ohio long ago, Eastern 
Shawnee Chief Glenna Wallace describes why it matters that her people’s story 
be told here in their homeland: When we first came back—­and “we” meaning 
my tribe—­when we came with the ninety people in 2007, we really didn’t know 
how we would be accepted. We didn’t really know how we would be treated. 
We didn’t know what people would say about us. We really didn’t even want 
newspapers or the media to know, because it was like 
a religious pilgrimage to us. We left Ohio, but we are 
still alive. We are still a thriving culture. And it’s very 
important for us to preserve that culture, and to let 
people know that we didn’t die. We want our people 
to be remembered; we want our contributions to be 
honored. (Link to video is on page 205.)
Wilberforce. Very near Oldtown (east via Brush Row Rd.) is Wil-
berforce, the home of two historically Black schools: Central State 
and Wilberforce Universities. The latter was founded in 1856 as the 
first institution of higher learning owned and operated by African 
Americans. Its faculty and alumni have included many distinguished 
leaders and scholars. Wilberforce is also home to the National Afro-­
American Museum and Cultural Center (1350 Brush Row Rd.). The 
museum has one of the largest collections of African American 
materials in the nation—­manuscripts, artworks, civil rights mem-
orabilia, textiles, and other artifacts—­some of which have specific 
connections to Wilberforce and other Little Miami Valley locales 
important in the Underground Railroad. Southwest of town is  
Youngsholm (1120 US Route 42 East), the restored home of Charles 
Young, best known for his early-­1900s leadership of the African 
American army units known as “Buffalo Soldiers.” His home, now 
presented by the National Park Service, was also a focal point for the 
rich artistic, cultural, and political life surrounding the town and col-
lege at Wilberforce. (Nearby, Charleton Mill Road crosses Massie’s 
Creek on a recently restored covered bridge.)
POLLOCK EARTHWORKS
A large county park preserves one of the most thoroughly studied 
Hopewell earthworks, where forested trails lead to an earthen wall, 
The Pollock Earthworks enclose the 
western end of a plateau otherwise 
surrounded by sheer rock cliffs.
The principal gateway into the Pollock 
Earthworks greets hikers at the end of the

Upper Miami Route
Upper Miami Route
One of America’s oldest and largest surviving water-­powered grist mills stands above the 
scenic Clifton Gorge.
The Big Fire. The most refmarkable construction phase at Pollock 
was the building of a huge timber stockade along the earthen wall 
and extending far out along the northern bluff. Sometime after build-
ing this tall fence, they burned it, pushed it down, and immediately 
buried the smoldering remains. That undoubtedly impressive spec-
tacle was part of a Hopewell pattern of burning things—­buildings, 
stones, soil, sacred objects, deceased kin—­all to be returned to ash 
and smoke, to Earth and Sky. Whether the earthen wall, the mud-­
plastered stockade, or the intentional fire had a defensive or a cere-
monial function is not known.
EN ROUTE TO DAYTON
North of Cedarville, the Little Miami River flows through three beautiful contiguous 
nature preserves between the tiny historic village of Clifton and the picturesque 
university town of Yellow Springs. This small district is surprisingly rich in history, 
culture, and dramatic scenery.
Clifton Gorge. Five miles north of the Pollock Earthworks, via  
Cedarville, is the tiny village of Clifton, whose adjacent gorge was 
once named by National Geographic magazine as one of the nation’s 
fifty most beautiful places. At the end of the last ice age, a peculiar 
funneling of glacial meltwater carved a narrow crevice here through 
which the Little Miami River now rushes, framed by 110-­foot, vertical 
limestone cliffs and towering 300-­year-­old trees. The spectacle and 
its rare microclimates seem especially astonishing in this otherwise 
only slightly rolling agricultural landscape. From a small parking 
area (169 Jackson St., Clifton), a path follows the canyon’s rim above 
the rushing torrent. From another trailhead, off State Route 343, hik-
ers can reach the bottom and walk among huge boulders fallen from 
the cliffs above. In the early 1800s, several industrial operations har-
nessed the confined, concentrated power of the river here. One of 
the old mills, its huge wooden building, millrace, and waterwheel 
intact, now houses a popular restaurant—­the Historic Clifton Mill (75 
Water St.).
Yellow Springs. Three and a half miles west of Clifton is Yellow 
Springs, a picturesque and delightfully eccentric college town and 
former spa—­home to the recently revived Antioch University, which 
counts among its alumni many notables including Coretta Scott 
King, Rod Serling, Clifford Geertz, and Stephen Jay Gould. The his-
toric town center is filled with interesting shops and unique cafés. 
Near Yellow Springs, John Bryan State Park offers excellent rec-
reational opportunities including eleven miles of hiking trails, 
mountain biking, waterfalls, rock climbing, camping, fishing, and 
a covered bridge. The state park and its trails are contiguous with 
Clifton Gorge on the east, and the private nonprofit Glen Helen Na-
ture Preserve on the west. Glen Helen trailhead parking (503 Corry 
St., Yellow Springs) gives access to a network of trails in and around 
the wooded valley, with small waterfalls, a mound, and other inter-
esting features. The extensive Little Miami Scenic Trail, perfect for 
biking, walking, and running, passes by the park entrance.
Enon Mound. Seven miles northwest of Yellow Springs (via Tecum-
seh Rd.), at the edge of the village of Enon, one of Ohio’s largest 
mounds anchors a mid-­century subdivision aptly named “Indian

Mound Estates” (400 Mound Circle, Enon). The steep twenty-­eight-­
foot mass of the mound is only partially tree covered, so its fine 
profile is visible from many angles, as adjacent streets radiate from 
its 110-­foot diameter base. Almost certainly of Adena origin, it has 
not been excavated.
DAYTON
Although best known today for its aviation history—­the Wright 
brothers, and Wright-­Patterson Air Force Base—­this valley of the 
Great Miami and Mad Rivers also has important ancient places, 
easily accessible from the city’s downtown and riverfront parks.
History and Setting. This broad river valley site was settled in 1796 
by a small party who had moved up from Cincinnati. The area was 
called the “Mad River Country” after the large tributary joining the 
Great Miami here from the northeast. One Jonathan Dayton (who at 
age twenty-­six had been the youngest signer of the US Constitution) 
first acquired a large tract of land here. The settlement grew signifi-
cantly during the canal era, and over the next century it became 
a center of innovation and industry—­especially with the National 
Cash Register Company, founded in 1884, and the Wright brothers’ 
pioneering aviation work soon after. Several historic Dayton neigh-
borhoods date from the mid-­to-­late 1800s through the early 1900s.
Oregon Historic District. The oldest part of the city is a wedge-­shaped 
area southeast of downtown called Oregon: About twelve square 
blocks of narrow, shady streets, first laid out in 1815, are lined with 
closely set, picturesque, gable-­fronted brick houses. Oregon grew 
quickly after the Miami and Erie Canal opened in 1829, spurring the 
development of industries. Immigrants, especially from Germany, 
arrived in the 1830s. East Sixth, Green, and Tecumseh Streets have 
some of the oldest houses; Jackson and Van Buren have some of the 
grandest. Brown Street is named for a brickmaker, Thomas Brown, 
who encouraged (and supplied materials for) his neighbors to re-
place their log cabins with the more substantial structures here 
today.
Calvary Cemetery Earthworks. A large cemetery off South Patter-
son Boulevard, about three miles south of downtown (1625 Calvary 
Dr.), occupies the site of a hilltop earthwork that once overlooked 
the Great Miami River 160 feet 
below. Many of the cemetery’s 
beautiful curving drives and 
monuments are laid out across 
a series of strange, steeply rolling glacial ridges that would have 
formed the interior of the ancient enclosure. Cemetery construc-
tion destroyed most of the earthwork’s walls, but fragments remain 
along the northern edge atop the steep forested bluff. Openings into 
the woods offer seasonal glimpses of these surviving remnants; the 
most evident crosses a low point between two ridges: Look for the 
Froendhoff monument adjacent to the second (western) loop of  
St. Joseph Drive and proceed to the fence line. The earthwork is 
7 feet high and about 30 feet away, partially concealed by a dense 
honeysuckle thicket. It is on Carillon Historical Park property and 
not accessible.
Carillon Historical Park. Below Calvary Cemetery to the north, Car-
illon Historical Park (1000 Carillon Blvd.) spreads out behind its 
tall modern bell tower (offering daily concerts). A large welcome 
center introduces visitors to an outdoor museum of Dayton history, 
where a fine collection of early settlement structures and exhib-
its has been assembled, including the log-­built Newcom’s Tavern 
(Dayton’s oldest, from 1796), as well as a variety of early houses and 
industrial buildings. Many of the remaining structures develop a 
transportation theme: a steam 
engine from the National Cash 
Register Company, a canal lock, 
two bridges, a railway station, 
and commuter rail cars. Most 
significant is the amazing orig-
inal Wright Flyer III airplane 
flown by the brothers in 1905.
Carillon Historical Park preserves 
several of Dayton’s oldest build-
ings, including Newcom’s Tavern, 
built in 1796.

meticulously reconstructed houses typical of the relatively settled 
Fort Ancient culture that flourished in the Ohio Valley 800 years 
ago. Thorough archaeological work began in the 1970s, uncovering 
the full village layout. A scale model in the museum shows how the 
fence, houses, storage pits, and burials formed concentric rings 
around an open plaza. A period garden is maintained on the grounds 
today, and a festival ground hosts Native American events and cer-
emonies. SunWatch is managed by the Dayton Society of Natural 
History, whose Boonshoft Museum of Discovery (2600 Deweese 
Pkwy., Dayton) holds finds from 
the site’s investigations, plus nat-
ural history and archaeology col-
lections from many cultures.
Houses. The quality of the houses 
suggests a remarkable level of 
comfort, and their construction may also resemble the Hopewell-­
era house-­building techniques of a thousand years earlier. Each 
reconstructed building is based on a posthole pattern found during 
the site’s investigation. Twigs were woven between vertical posts, 
then plastered with mud—­a method called “wattle and daub.” The 
steep thatched roofs had deep overhangs to keep rain away from the 
walls. Built-­in benches inside were for sitting and sleeping; a central 
hearth was vented through the roof. Some had special uses: The Big 
House was a kind of community center, another was a men’s lodge, 
and the rest were dwellings of matrilineal families.
Shadows and Time. The modern name SunWatch is derived from 
the way a pole in the center of the plaza would cast its shadow into 
the doorways of certain houses at sunrise. The shadow’s cycle of 
movement, from one door to another throughout the year, marked 
out a calendar for agricultural and ceremonial purposes. The 
current central pole is forty feet high, estimated from the size of 
the original posthole, so its early morning shadow was long. All  
the villagers could watch, day by day, as its shadow approached the 
most important days of the year, notably the winter solstice and  
the times for spring planting and autumn harvesting.
Dayton Aviation Heritage. Many Dayton area sites recount and  
celebrate the city’s role in the invention and growth of aviation; 
information is available from the nonprofit Aviation Trail website, 
or at the Dayton Aviation Heritage National Historical Park visitor 
center (16 South Williams St.). The National Park Service manages 
several important sites commemorating the legacy of local bicycle 
makers and flight pioneers Orville and Wilbur Wright. These include 
the Wright Cycle Company complex and the adjacent museum (at 
the South Williams St. location), the nearby home of Paul Laurence 
Dunbar (the Wrights’ friend and famous African American poet), 
and the Huffman Prairie Flying Field and its interpretive center on 
the Wright-­Patterson Air Force Base. The rest of aviation history is 
vividly presented at the huge National Museum of the United States 
Air Force (1100 Spaatz St.), where over 350 air and space vehicles, 
plus other displays, fill in the story all the way up to current precision 
stealth technology.
Wright Mound Group. A memo-
rial monument to the Wright 
brothers stands in a hilltop park 
in front of the Huffman Prairie 
Flying Field Interpretive Center 
(2380 Memorial Rd.). Sweeping views overlook the Mad River, the 
Huffman Prairie where the Wrights did their testing, and beyond 
to the huge runways of the Wright-­Patterson Air Force Base. Along 
the northern and western edges of this small park and picnic area 
is a rare cluster of six small Adena-­era burial mounds. The largest 
is just over four feet high and fifty feet in diameter.
SUNWATCH VILLAGE
This partially restored period settlement is the best place in Ohio 
to see the town-­dwelling, house-­building, corn-­planting lifeways 
of the Fort Ancient culture; its modern name is derived from the 
sundial effect of poles positioned in the central plaza.
Orientation. Four miles southwest of downtown Dayton, on the 
right bank of the Great Miami River, this archaeological park (2301 
West River Rd., Dayton) includes an excellent museum and several 
Six mounds of various sizes line the edge 
of a peaceful park adjacent to the Wright 
Brothers Memorial and Huffman Prairie 
Flying Field Interpretive Center.

portrays the concentric planning of 
the village around its central, 
shadow-casting poles.

GROWING CORN AT SUNWATCH
Archaeologist Andy Sawyer explains how the residents of SunWatch Village 
grew their main staple food, along with other crops: Corn played a big 
role in the community here; in fact, it was probably the main reason they 
decided to settle down into rather permanent villages—­to take care of the 
corn crop. Historically, the way American Indians in this region grew their 
crops was they would make little mounds in their 
gardens, about a foot or so high, two or three feet 
around, and at the top center of the mound they 
would plant the corn. Once the corn sprouted, they 
would plant the beans. So they would kind of grow 
up together, and what would happen is, as the corn 
grew, that corn stalk provided a trellis for the beans. 
(Link to video is on page 205.)
The morning shadow of the 
village’s central pole enters 
the doorways of certain 
houses when it is time for 
planting or harvesting.
The perfect conical form of the Miamisburg Mound stands atop a high bluff, offering long 
views up and down the Great Miami Valley.
Anderson Earthworks. Although a bit far afield (100 miles northwest 
of Dayton), Indiana’s Mounds State Park is worth a trip (4306 Mounds 
Rd., Anderson, Indiana). Several well-­preserved earthen enclosures 
date from about 250 BCE to 50 CE, suggesting an Adena affiliation, but 
their forms are exquisitely Hopewell—­a rounded-­corner rectangle 
with an interior ditch and eastern gateway, and a 9-­foot-­high circular 
earthwork whose 300-­foot diameter and water-­retaining interior ditch 
make it a quarter-­scale version (and given its early date, a possible 
precursor) of Newark’s Great Circle. A unique “Fiddleback” (panduri-
form) enclosure lies nearby.
EN ROUTE DOWN THE GREAT MIAMI VALLEY
Southeast of Dayton, follow the old Dayton Cincinnati Pike along the river, or take 
State Route 4 toward Germantown, to the Miamisburg Mound and Carlisle Fort. From 
nearby Franklin, State Route 73 and Route 4 connect (via Middletown) to the Hamilton 
area and its earthworks—­treated in the next chapter.
Miamisburg Mound. Downstream from Dayton along the Great 
Miami River, on a high hill above Miamisburg’s historic center (900 
Mound Rd.) stands one of the two largest Adena-­era burial mounds 
in North America. (The other is in Moundsville, West Virginia.) It 
is certainly the most spectacularly situated, rising 68 feet above 


--- PDF PAGE 67 ---

119
118
Upper Miami Route
what is already a 100-­foot bluff. Its 54,000 cubic yards of soil cover 
one and a half acres under a perfect conical profile. The location 
is the highest point for miles around. Climbing the 116 stone steps 
to the top affords splendid views up and down the valley—­from at 
least the hills west of Hamilton to the tops of downtown Dayton’s 
skyscrapers. Limited archaeological work revealed the mound was 
built in stages, and contained at least two burial vaults—­one 8 feet 
from the top with a bark-­covered grave, and another 36 feet down 
with a log chamber but no human remains.
Carlisle Fort. Across the Great Miami from Miamisburg, between 
Germantown and the village of Carlisle, a trail in the Twin Creek 
MetroPark (8502 Chamberlain Rd., Franklin) leads to the remains 
of Carlisle Fort, a roughly triangular Hopewell hilltop enclosure. 
Called the Hopewell Trail, it climbs through an open meadow, then 
turns right along a well-­preserved three-­foot-­high earthen wall that 
seems to glide through the woods atop steep razorback ridges and 
around sharp corners. An especially impressive prow points to the 
southeast, down a long ridge. The steep ridges here have made this 
earthwork more susceptible to erosion than most others.
TO THE LOWER MIAMI ROUTE
Continue downstream along the Great Miami through Middletown to meet the Lower 
Miami Route at the Rentschler Forest Preserve just outside Hamilton, and on to Miami 
Fort, Greater Cincinnati, and the lower end of the Little Miami Valley. Or return via 
Franklin to Lebanon or Fort Ancient and the World Heritage Route.
5
Lower Miami Route
From either Miamisburg or Lebanon, follow the Great Miami River 
to its three surviving hilltop enclosures. Greater Cincinnati has 
several mounds and top-­tier cultural attractions. Its eastern sub-
urbs along the Little Miami once held the greatest concentration 
of Hopewell geometric earthworks outside of Chillicothe’s Paint-­
Scioto region, and although most are now lost, their stories are sig-
nificant. Overall, the rich cultural history of southwest Ohio offers 
much to enjoy.

Fernald Preserve. An engaging 
visitor center now anchors and 
explains this reclaimed natural 
area, once an infamous Cold War 
uranium refinery (7400 Willey Rd., Hamilton). Exhibits explore the 
site’s complex and contentious history—­from Indigenous artifacts, 
to farm families, to the proud workers who helped “win the Cold 
War.” Radioactive leaks led to media scandals, and after the US De-
partment of Energy’s decades-­long, several-­billion-­dollar cleanup, 
Fernald is now a restored prairie landscape with surprisingly spec-

lover’s paradise, eerie rectangular ponds echo the contaminated

A trailhead in the Rentschler Forest MetroPark, about four miles 
northeast of the city of Hamilton’s downtown waterfront (5574 
Rentschler Estates Dr., Hamilton), skirts a large meadow before 
entering the forest, where the enclosure’s southern perimeter wall 
appears. Inside, the site’s most distinctive feature remains intact: A 
100-­foot-­diameter earthen formation consists of two arcs holding a 
low earthen ring between them like two hands. The trail continues 
through the woods and arrives at a small sign, positioned at the “fin-
gertips” of this unique gateway design. The larger walls in the dis-
tance are at the “wrists.” In the foreground lies the 75-­foot-­diameter 
earthen circle, though its 2-­foot height can be hard to see in the for-
est undergrowth. Another park entrance (via Reigart Road) leads to 
a pleasant picnic area with amenities beside the river, and a pictur-
esque remnant of the Miami and Erie Canal.
Hamilton. The seat of Butler County has three historic districts. 
Rossville, to the west across the river bridge, was once a separate 
settlement and retains a small downtown with a good collection 
of older buildings along Main and D Streets. East of downtown, 
the Dayton Lane area retains several large homes of the city’s in-
dustrialists in various 1800s styles. An unusual pair of octagonal 
buildings anchor the nine-­block German Village area just north of 
downtown. The 1863 Lane-­Hooven House (319 North Third St.) is a 
small Gothic Revival gem with fine woodwork and a spiral stair; it 
now houses the Hamilton Community Foundation and is open for 
tours by appointment. The house was built for Clark Lane (a Ham-
ilton industrialist and philanthropist), who commissioned another 
octagon directly across the street a few years later: The Hamilton 
Lane Library was the first free public library west of the Allegheny 
Mountains.

WHITE WATER SHAKER VILLAGE
Several buildings survive from one of thirteen settlements across New 
England and the Midwest founded by the industrious Shakers during 
the first quarter of the 1800s (11813 Oxford Rd., Harrison). Five were in 
Ohio, but this is the only one of those to survive. Twenty buildings are 
preserved as part of Miami Whitewater Forest (a huge county park), includ-
ing some buildings along New Haven Road—­notably the only Shaker meet-
ing house built of brick, and the large North Family Dwelling next door. These 
Shakers, like their brethren in the east and in Kentucky, thrived for decades 
through careful farming methods and successful sales of brooms, seeds, 
and other products.
The eastern walls of Miami Fort, and its southeastern gateway shown here, are among the 
Hopewell world’s most impressive visible remains.
footprints of the manufacturing 
plants, and a long, hermetically 
sealed mound full of buried 
radioactive waste dominates the 
eastern horizon.
The Fernald Mound. When the 
Department of Energy cleaned 
up this contaminated site in the 
1990s and early 2000s, they shipped the most seriously radioactive 
waste out west—­on miles of train cars. But the less-­dangerous de-
bris was entombed on the site, sealed in the long mound that will 
forever dominate the eastern horizon here. Called by its engineers 
the “On-­Site Disposal Facility” (or OSDF), it will be monitored far, 
far into the future for any impacts on the aquifer. The visitor cen-
ter’s diagonal axis points to the Sun’s northernmost rising position 
over the mound’s elevated horizon; the sunrise then moves back 
and forth along the top of the mound throughout the year. So, like 
at Mound City, the eastern horizon is made to calibrate the per-
ceived orders of the cosmos, and in perpetuity. The Sun will swing 
back and forth along the mound, and the DOE’s Office of Legacy 
Management will monitor the mound indefinitely.
Miami Fort Earthworks. One of Ohio’s greatest hilltop enclosures 
crowns a narrow ridge at the extreme western end of a large county 
park, high above the broad flood plain where the Great Miami River 
joins the Ohio. Spectacular annual bird migrations flock to the  
wetlands below.
Orientation. From the entrance to Shawnee Lookout, a large county 
park (2008 Lawrenceburg Rd., North Bend), a long winding drive 
passes a small mound and several historic structures, before 
ending at a parking area from which the Miami Fort Trail climbs 
steeply to a narrow ridge. Here, Ancient Ohioans built and main-
tained for centuries a hilltop enclosure, still well preserved. The trail  
enters the earthwork’s northeastern gateway and continues along the 
northern wall with glimpses of the huge (Teays-­age) Great Miami

William Henry Harrison Tomb. Access to Miami Fort is inevitably 
via the village of Cleves, home to the ninth president of the United 
States (and the father of the twenty-­third), where he is buried be-
neath an imposing monument (41 Cliff Rd., North Bend). Harrison 
began an army career in the 1790s, fought with General Anthony 
Wayne in the decisive Battle of Fallen Timbers, and was present at 
the signing of the Treaty of Greenville, which banished Indigenous 
people to land far north and west of the Ohio River. As governor of
Shawnee Lookout west of Cincinnati presents 
a dramatic view over the broad valley where 
the Great Miami River joins the Ohio.
the Northwest Territory, he encouraged settlement whilemaking 
constant inroads on Native lands. In 1811, he led an army to victory 
at Prophetstown (in Indiana Territory), where people from many 
tribes were hoping to unite under the Shawnee leader Tecumseh 
to drive the American settlers back. After the War of 1812, Harri-
son was elected first to Congress, and then to the US presidency. 
Only a month after taking office in 1841, he died of pneumonia.
RIVER ROAD INTO CINCINNATI
A historical marker (422 South Miami Ave., Cleves) commemorates one of only twelve 
canal tunnels built in the US, cut through a small hill here (Harrison was a canal 
advocate, and this was part of his extensive farm) to connect Cincinnati to Indiana’s 
Whitewater Canal. A remnant of the tunnel’s northern entrance lies mostly buried 
deep in the woods nearby. On the way into Cincinnati from here (via US Route 50, part 
of the Ohio River Scenic Byway), the large elliptical Short Woods Park Mound stands 
at the far edge of a public golf course in the pleasant Sayler Park neighborhood (7000 
Fernbank Ave., Cincinnati).
CINCINNATI
Called “The Queen of the West,” this hilly, picturesque Ohio River 
city was the largest west of the Appalachians—­a hub of canal and 
river traffic until the railroads began to redirect western commerce 
through Chicago instead.
History and Setting. In 1788, Cincinnati was founded on a high ter-
race above the Ohio River where two tributaries flow into it from 
large, ancient Teays-­age valleys. After Portsmouth and Marietta, 
this was the next such landscape available for major settlement 
on the northern banks of the Ohio. And like those other settle-
ments, such prime real estate was already covered with ancient 
earthworks: As Harrison put it, when he first saw this level plain 
in 1791, “it was literally covered in low lines of embankments. . . . 
The number and variety of figures in which these lines were drawn 
was almost endless.” The city’s name derives from the “Order of the 
Cincinnati”—­American Revolutionary War officers whose ideal was 
to return, like the Roman military leader Lucius Quinctius Cincin-
natus, from military achievement to their peacetime vocations, es-
pecially farming. The city grew into an economic powerhouse and 
major cultural hub, the largest west of the Appalachians until after 
the American Civil War, when the new railroads attracted most new 
commercial growth to Chicago.
A series of stepped ponds remain well  
preserved along Miami Fort’s western wall.

THE ORDER OF THE CINCINNATI
After their Revolution, cutting themselves free of the British Empire, the 
officers of George Washington’s army formed what they called the “Order of 
the Cincinnati”; and many of them tried for a kind of geographic new start in 
the Ohio Valley. They came to Marietta, Ohio, and found there the evidence 
of an older order—­a grid, rectilinear earthworks that bespoke a settled and 
disciplined way of arranging buildings, as they intended to arrange a society. 
It was for them a new start, under severe discipline. And to that George 
Washington said he hoped to come to join them, because what he found 
abiding on the East Coast he described as the “rubbish” that he thought 
they had tried to eliminate. A new possibility in the West has always been the 
American aspiration.
—­Roger G. Kennedy, American historian and former director of
the National Park Service
Little remained of Cincinnati’s earthworks 
(highlighted here in black) when Daniel 
Drake recorded them on this map in 1815.
remains of the earthworks here 
were mapped by local physician 
Daniel Drake. But the city was 
growing and prospering rapidly, 
and the “variety of figures” noted 
by Harrison (and the forests that 
had long protected them) were 
gone before any further records 
could be made—­other than the 
exploration of two mounds as 
they were being taken down.
Urban Districts. German immigrants flocked to the city throughout 
the 1800s, undoubtedly appreciating the setting’s resemblance to 
the Rhine or the Moselle Valleys. They created a dense urban neigh-
borhood just north of downtown filled with breweries, drinking 
halls, and vast brick and stone churches adorned with Germanic 
inscriptions. Called Over-­the-­Rhine for its position across the canal 
(now Central Parkway) from the city center, that area is today one 
of the nation’s finest surviving nineteenth-­century urban fabrics 
and most successful urban revitalization zones. Hilltop neighbor-
hoods were once served by inclined railways, and before that cov-
ered with vineyards, including tiny Mount Adams, immediately to 
the east, with its romantically inspired Eden Park.
University of Cincinnati Earthworks. Clifton is another nineteenth-­
century neighborhood, on the hilltop two miles to the north, and 
now home to the University of Cincinnati. Beginning in the 1980s, 
campus leaders began commissioning internationally known 
architects for all new buildings. Some of them are of great inter-
est individually, but all are now also harmonized by the brilliant, 
ancient-­earthworks-­inspired master plan designed the following 
decade by the internationally known landscape architect George 
Hargreaves. Large mounds connect to sinuous and angular berms 
and winding pathways, weaving the various avant-­garde buildings 
into an attractive unity.
Cincinnati’s Earthworks. The city occupies a beautiful setting, 
surrounded by wooded hills and valleys that carve its inner-­ring 
neighborhoods into highly distinct and often picturesque en-
claves. The symbolic heart of the vibrant city center is Fountain 
Square and the adjacent Art Deco masterpiece Netherland Plaza 
Hotel, which occupy the position of a huge elliptical earthwork that 
once dominated this high terrace above the river. In 1815, the scant 
The hills of Devou Park in Covington, Kentucky, offer the prime view of Cincinnati’s  
Ohio River setting.

The Mariemont Earthwork 
follows the terrace edge along 
Miami Bluff Drive in a quiet 
residential neighborhood.
Mariemont. A half-­timbered Tudor inn and a Gothic style church 
help this 1930s suburb to exude English-­village ambience, but 
street names like “Cachepit Way” and “Flintpoint Way” hint at a 
much longer history. An ancient earthen wall parallels Miami 
Bluff Drive, having been recorded by Charles Metz (another Cincin-
nati physician-­archaeologist) long before the development of the 
town. At the southwestern end of the same street, a small park 
and historical marker commemorate the Madisonville Site and the  
importance of this high terrace location in antiquity and in the early 
development of American archaeological knowledge.
Newtown Mounds. Mariemont overlooks the Little Miami River, which 
flows through a broad valley for several miles toward its confluence 
with the Ohio. Just across the river is the village of Newtown, where 
two mounds stand inside Flag Spring Cemetery (3549 Round Bottom 
Rd.). The more prominent mound stands at the center; a much lower 
one is beside the fence near the entry gate. Nearby in the village, 
excavations in the 1980s at what was then the local firehouse and is 
now the Newtown Municipal Center (3537 Church St.) uncovered two 
spectacular shell gorgets from around 400 CE, incised with detailed 
animal figures. An interactive video and artifact exhibit is open to the 
public in the remodeled firehouse, highlighting the finds from that 
site and from a Fort Ancient culture village just outside town, and 
showcasing the very important (though now lost) Turner Earthworks 
that were just up the road.
Turner Earthworks. The greatest of the ancient ceremonial sites 
lining this wide valley was Turner, where two geometric figures 
lay across the high terraces near the intersection of Round Bottom 
and Mount Carmel Roads. From here came some of the Hopewell  
culture’s most beautiful artistry including clay figurines, a mica 
serpent with a pearl eye, and an effigy of a strange horned creature 
Four Museums. Housed beneath and behind the huge semidome of 
the city’s spectacular 1930s railway station, the Cincinnati Museum 
Center presents excellent history, science, and archaeology exhib-
its. The National Underground Railroad Freedom Center down-
town commemorates the significance of the Ohio River boundary 
between slavery and freedom, while other exhibits and programs 
raise issues of contemporary injustice. The Taft Museum of Art 
maintains a small but astonishing collection of masterworks in an 
exquisite 1820s Federal-­style mansion at the foot of Mount Adams. 
The Cincinnati Art Museum’s comprehensive collection compares 
well with any major metropolitan museum; a gallery devoted to 
local furniture makers showcases what happened to all that lumber 
arriving from Newark’s Cherry Valley (see page 88).
Norwood Mound. Mounds occupy prominent sites in several of Cin-
cinnati’s outlying neighborhoods. The largest and most impressive 
is the Norwood Mound (adjacent to 2409 Indian Mound Ave., Nor-
wood), standing in a small hilltop park reached by a narrow alley 
between two houses. The tiny lane circles the unusually steep el-
liptical mound at very close range. The presence of the city’s water 
tank nearby emphasizes the prominence of this high hilltop loca-
tion overlooking an ancient preglacial course of the Ohio River.
THE LITTLE MIAMI VALLEY
The Little Miami Valley suburbs east of Cincinnati retain only a few 
traces of what was once the second-­greatest concentration of Hopewell 
geometric earthworks, though some have remarkable stories to tell.

THE NEWTOWN GORGETS
Two large marine shells from the Gulf of Mexico or southern Atlantic, incised 
with images, were found at the Newtown firehouse site. Archaeologist 
Bob Genheimer reflects on their depictions of a possum and a mountain 
lion: When you think about possums, none of us have warm and fuzzy 
feelings about possums, and it wouldn’t have been our first animal choice. 
But the mountain lion, I think most Westerners can understand that sort of 
symbolism: a very fierce, individualistic animal that used to be here. This 
mountain lion is in plan view, so you’re looking in the belly of the lion, and the 
head of the lion is on top, big bushy tail on the bottom, and then the two front 
paws and the two rear paws, which are enormous—­
the feral cats have these large paws. It’s really 
an eerie thing to look at that gorget. When it was 
first exposed, it was a really spiritual experience to 
see that animal staring you in the face for the first 
time in probably fifteen or sixteen hundred years. 
(Link to video is on page 205.)
Archaeologists from the Peabody Museum at 
Harvard University studied the spectacular  
Turner Earthworks outside Newtown in the  
1880s and 1890s.
Squier and Davis’s 1840s drawing of the 
Milford Works illustrates the site’s most 
unique feature on the hill to the left.
(most are now in the collections of the Peabody Museum of Archae-
ology and Ethnology at Harvard). Inside a large oval enclosure, an 
unusual cluster of mounds was laced together by rings of stone. 
Fires were lit beneath one of them in deep clay-­lined chambers 
that would have attained 1200-­degree temperatures. Plowing of 
the earthworks began as early as 1825, and careful studies were 
made before 1900. But eventually it was all destroyed by a graveling 
operation.
Milford. Atop the small hill just above the town of Milford’s thriv-
ing and historic Main Street lay one of the strangest features of 
any Hopewell earthwork. In Squier and Davis’s words, “Diverging 
lines extend to the south-­west, terminating in a maze of walls un-
like any others which have yet fallen under notice.” The Milford 
Works shared with nearby Turner the unusual combination of 
two enclosures set on different levels and connected by a walled  
passage. They disappeared long ago, although faint traces of its 
open-­cornered square appear on old aerial photographs of a nearby 
cemetery.
TO THE BRUSH CREEK ROUTE
Heading east out of Milford on US Route 50 leads to Hillsboro and the Brush Creek 
Route (see map, page 133), with the option of a short detour via State Route 131 
to the Fort Salem Earthworks—­a unique site midway between the major Hopewell 
concentrations of the Little Miami Valley and the Paint-­Scioto heartland.
TO THE WORLD HERITAGE OR UPPER MIAMI ROUTES
Following the river upstream from Milford involves navigating some backroads and 
small streets. The town of Loveland has a pleasant historic center and is a major hub 
on the Little Miami Scenic Trail. Upscale Montgomery offers more direct access to US 
Route 22 (its Main Street and the early 1800s route to Circleville and beyond), and to 
Interstate 71.
Foster. Frederic Ward Putnam of Harvard University, sometimes 
called the “father” of American scientific archaeology, focused 
most of his late-­1800s Ohio efforts in the southwestern part of the 
state. Besides Serpent Mound and Turner, he investigated another 
Little Miami site—­a hilltop enclosure called Foster (preserved on 
private land but not visitable). Putnam called it “a singular ancient 
work” because of the astonishing action of fire within its walls, 
which he found loaded with heavily burned soil and stone. They 
were capped with vitrified clay—­turned to bright orange glass by 


--- PDF PAGE 74 ---

133
132
Lower Miami Route
intense heat. Clay-­firing ovens at the site were outfitted with flues 
to control the airflow and produce very hot temperatures. The 
wooded hilltop site is directly across the river from the Monkey Bar 
and Grille—­a popular stop for cyclists on the Little Miami Scenic 
Trail.
Stubbs Earthworks. In the late 1990s, archaeologists had the op-
portunity to explore—­albeit hurriedly—­the construction site 
for a new high school west of the village of Morrow (3001 E. US 
Route 22, Morrow). The land held the scant remains of the 
Stubbs Earthworks, once one of southwest Ohio’s grandest geo-
metric enclosures. A low irregular mound still lies today in 
a loop of the school’s driveway, over the remains of a multi 
chambered building. Archaeologist Frank Cowan and a team 
from the Cincinnati Museum Center discovered, about where the 
school’s grandstand is now, a 271-­foot-­diameter circle of postholes. 
This was the first evidence of a giant woodhenge found from the 
Hopewell era, and it coincided with an earthen circle recorded on 
an early-­1800s plan of the Stubbs site. Other examples found since 
(at the Hopeton Earthworks and the Hopewell Mound Group) now 
suggest that perhaps many—­or even most—­earthworks were also 
outlined or preceded by huge open-­air timber constructions.
TO THE WORLD HERITAGE OR UPPER MIAMI ROUTES
From Morrow, both Lebanon and Fort Ancient are nearby; from there join either the 
World Heritage Route (see maps, pages 24 and 72) or the Upper Miami Route (see 
map, page 105).
