Brush Creek Route
Branch off the World Heritage Route between the villages of New 
Vienna and Bainbridge, through Hillsboro and as far south as West 
Union. Along Ohio Brush Creek (its full name) and its upper trib-
utary, Baker Fork, lie two of Ohio’s most beautiful earthworks—­the 
world-­famous Serpent Mound and the pristine hilltop enclosure of 
Fort Hill. Nearby in Highland, Adams, and Ross Counties are many 
exceptional natural preserves—­with rock-­cliff gorges, sweeping 
prairies, idyllic waterways, and rare species.

THE APPALACHIAN PLATEAU
Heading east from Hillsboro, distant hills signal the ecological seam running from 
the Ohio River northeast to the Newark area and beyond. Most of Ancient Ohio’s 
greatest earthwork monuments lie among the overlapping ecologies where the flat 
plains meet these unglaciated Appalachian foothills. For the last mile before Serpent 
Mound, the road crosses the valley of Ohio Brush Creek—­here part of a mysterious 
300-­million-­year-­old cryptoexplosion crater.
SERPENT MOUND
One of the world’s most famous ancient works of art slithers beauti-
fully across the saddle of a hill, its tightly spiraling tail and solstice-­
aligned head both lying atop sheer cliffs.
Orientation. The well-­marked site entrance is in the wide valley 
of Ohio Brush Creek (3850 SR 73, Peebles). Hours vary seasonally, 
so consult the Ohio History Connection website. The access road 
climbs past a small mound before ending in a tiered parking area 
adjacent to restrooms and, ahead, the small museum. Approaching 
the effigy, its spiraling tail is on the left, while the three coils in the 
center are overlooked by a 1908 iron tower. Paved walks surround 
the effigy, and a somewhat rugged trail drops into the valley and 
approaches the stream. Serpent Mound seems surprisingly small 
for visitors accustomed to the gigantic and far older Hopewell 


--- PDF PAGE 76 ---

136
137
Brush Creek Route
Brush Creek Route
version may have existed in Adena-­Hopewell times. Long before 
that, the huge geological anomaly in which the creature rests, with its  
unusual hills and strange rocks, could well have suggested the place 
had special power. In 1883, Harvard archaeologist Frederic Ward 
Putnam first visited the serpent, and began a successful campaign 
to buy and restore it. Harvard gave the property to the state of Ohio 
in 1900, and today it remains a state memorial managed by the Ohio 
History Connection.
1 Museum. This modest wooden building dates from the 1930s  
and houses a well-­stocked gift shop and small exhibit space. Visitors 
can watch an informative video about the site that is updated peri-
odically. A medium-­sized mound stands near the museum, dated by  
archaeologists to the Adena era. Another Adena mound, and a 
smaller one from the Fort Ancient culture, stand farther away near 
the approach road.
2 Spiraling Tail. As the path approaches the effigy, the tail comes 
into view on the left, its tight spiral leaning gently outward  
toward the edge of a cliff that drops sharply to the waters of Brush 
Creek far below. The spiraling tail resembles that of Ohio’s other  
important effigy, Granville’s so-­called Alligator Mound. The Great 
Serpent was a powerful creature of the watery Beneath World in 
widespread and long-­standing Indigenous cosmological traditions. 
The subtle shapes here are Putnam’s restorations, based on the  
records of earlier nineteenth-­century delineators. There were  
several versions, especially of the head, both before and after Putnam 
did his work. To this day they leave room for many interpretations.
The Cryptoexplosion. Views from the tail (and the head) extend to 
the distant southwestern rim of a four-­mile-­wide crater formed 
300 million years ago by what geologists call the “Serpent Mound  
Disturbance.” Either an asteroid collision or an underground  
explosion blew apart more than seven cubic miles of rock—­uplifting 
a central area more than 1,000 feet and dropping an outer rim more 
than 400 feet. After eons of erosion, Serpent Mound and the hills  
behind it lie on the shattered fragments of the central uplift, while 
the distant ridgetops stand high today because they are the much 
harder Ordovician bedrock that was offset by the event. The strange 
geology of this spot was first noticed in modern times by Dr. John 
Locke of Cincinnati, who named it “Sunken Mountain” in 1838.
monuments, yet its elegant beauty and undeniable evocation of 
spiritual power have a vivid allure of their own. The view from the 
creature’s head faces the summer solstice sunset over the rim of 
the distant crater. Serpent Mound is especially alluring early or late 
in the day when shadows are deep.
History. Although some uncertainty persists, both carbon dating 
and iconographic evidence persuasively connect the effigy to the 
widespread Mississippian cultural world (in Ohio called the Fort 
Ancient tradition, from about 900 CE onwards). The shapes of 
the serpent’s head resemble Mississippian symbols as far away as  
Missouri. The nearby Adena-­era mounds indicate this location 
was important for centuries before, and it’s possible that an earlier

Rising from the saddle of the hill, the

John P. MacLean’s 1885 plan of the serpent’s head depicts

The Serpent’s Mysteries. Judging from the 
diversity of its historical interpretations, and 
of its modern visitors, Serpent Mound means 
many things to many people, even as it re-
mains little understood by archaeologists today (compared to the 
older Hopewell sites). Questions persist about its date, its builders, 
and its meanings; Indigenous views also vary widely. It may be the 
Great Serpent of the watery Beneath World, or the Horned Serpent 
of American Indian legend, perhaps in the act of swallowing the 
Sun, as during an eclipse. Its probable construction date could link 
it to the “blazing serpent in the Sky” that we call Halley’s Comet, 
whose brightest known appearance was in 1066. Perhaps most in-
triguing, a recent discovery notes the resemblance of at least one 
of the head’s early plans (John P. MacLean’s—­the most complete in 
this respect) with other Mississippian images of an Indigenous cre-
ation story in which the First Mother receives her creative power by 
mating with the Great Serpent (the oval becomes her womb).
5 River Trail. Below the Serpent winds Brush 
Creek, through the wide bottom of the an-
cient crater. On either side of the snake’s 
body, steep trails lead down through the 
forest to the banks of the stream where, 
especially in the winter, the undulating 
shape of the bluff is visible. Most promi-
nent is a sharply jutting headlike forma-
tion directly beneath the serpent’s head. 
The effigy’s builders may have understood 
from generations of their Ancestors that 
3 Three Coils. The most beau-
tiful part of the effigy is its trio 
of matching coils. They seem so 
refined, and yet defy efforts to 
discover any regular geometries. 
While they seem uniform from 
above, as they unwind across the saddle of the hill, they gently un-
dulate, giving the creature’s body a slithering, lifelike energy. Some 
have linked the individual coils’ roughly symmetrical shapes to the 
solstice and equinox sunrises, but their axial alignments are both 
hard to pinpoint and quite imprecise—­off by as much as eleven 
degrees. Remote sensing investigations have recently revealed the 
trace of a fourth coil at the creature’s neck; it was unknown to any 
of the site’s previous delineators.
Serpentine Form. Nobody is indifferent to snakes. Whether it was 
veneration or trepidation that inspired its builders, this creature’s 
beauty and power are obvious. Like all great art, it appeals to us by 
blending the recognizable with the abstract. Its stylized precision 
speaks of “all serpents,” and also more generally of “the serpen-
tine” as an aesthetic quality. The genius of its designers remains 
apparent, and this blend of beauty, familiarity, abstraction, power, 
precision, and mystery makes Ohio’s Serpent Mound one of the 
great iconic images from all human antiquity.
4 The Head. The far end of Serpent Mound is so beautifully ab-
stracted that it has baffled its various interpreters for decades. Is it an 
egg, a frog, or the Sun? Is it the snake’s open mouth, its giant never-­
blinking eye, or its poison glands? Part of the confusion stems from 
the many versions depicted on nineteenth-­century plans, depending 
on what was visible to each delineator. Squier and Davis described, 
for example, how the builders had modified the ground to create 
symmetrical ten-­foot-­wide surfaces along both sides of the oval fig-
ure. The cliff promontory here points northwest, toward where the 
midsummer Sun sets over the distant rim of the cryptoexplosion 
crater. But again, a soft imprecision reigns, since the true solstice 
alignment is across the center points of the triangular “neck” and 
From the banks of Brush Creek

the effigy’s head.

this formation was already charged with the spiritual powers of

A Sacred Site. Many visitors find Serpent Mound to be a power-
ful, sacred site, inspiring many interpretations for many people, 
groups, and traditions. Recent management efforts are seeking to 
reestablish an authentic link with contemporary American Indian 
tribal ideas, and to discourage the theories and practices that have 
historically detracted from or disrespected its known Indigenous 
origins (for example, that it was created by a race from another 
continent, or another millennium, or even another planet). Yet the 
annual summer solstice sunset celebrations still draw visitors with 
many sometimes-­eccentric ideas about what lies behind the appar-
ent spiritual power of this site.
ZANE’S TRACE NORTH OR SOUTH
Just east of Serpent Mound, State Route 41 follows the path of historic Zane’s Trace. 
Go south from Locust Grove toward West Union for the many natural preserves of 
southern Adams County. North, through Sinking Spring and past Fort Hill, the 
Appalachian Plateau’s dense topography of forested mountains is to the right. Near 
(just above) Serpent Mound, small roads cross the cryptoexplosion’s long-­eroded 
central debris pile, although the strange geology is not readily apparent.
Quiverheart Gorge Preserve. At this protected Arc of Appalachia na-
ture preserve (2199 SR 781, Peebles), two improved but strenuous 
trails offer over three miles of hiking through a narrow, dolomite-­
rock canyon. The exceptional scenery includes waterfalls, dra-
matic rock shelters, and rich spring wildflower displays. An ancient 
prairie remnant adds to the preserve’s high biodiversity. The first 
steam-­powered iron blast furnace west of the Appalachian Moun-
tains was in full operation here by 1815; remains of the large piles 
of charcoal that once fueled the furnace are still visible along the 
trails. Several other Arc of Appalachia preserves offer hiking trails 
within a few miles of here, including Plum Run Prairie, Kamama 
Prairie, and Chalet Nivale. For excellent descriptions and direc-
tions consult the Arc’s website.
Zane’s Trace. State Route 41 in this vicinity was the first official 
road designated in the state of Ohio, having been a section of 
the first pioneer road connecting Wheeling, West Virginia, with 
Kentucky. The route arrived in Locust Grove from Chillicothe, 
then continued southwest toward Maysville, Kentucky—­today 
a beautifully preserved historic river town. Just over a mile past 
Dunkinsville, on the west side of the road, stands one of the  
oldest structures in Ohio—­the John Treber House, built in 1798 as 
the Treber Inn, an early wayside for 
Zane’s Trace travelers (17759 SR 41, 
West Union). The house is privately 
owned and not open to the pub-
lic, but a historical marker stands 
beside a small parking space out 
front.
One of Ohio’s oldest surviving structures, 
the 1798 John Treber House served as 
a coach stop on Zane’s Trace, the early 
pioneer route into Kentucky.
THE SERPENT OF EDEN
Serpent Mound’s self-­evident symbolic form and spiritual power have 
suggested many meanings to many people, usually based on the ideas they 
brought with them. Archaeologist Brad Lepper recounts one especially 
telling example: The Reverend Landen West, who right around 1900, saw the 
serpent, this magnificent serpent, with what he thought was something in the 
serpent’s jaws, said, “This isn’t just any serpent. This 
is the serpent of the Garden of Eden, with the apple 
in its mouth. And this was built by God to mark the 
location of Eden.” Now, Adams County is a beautiful, 
beautiful natural area, natural environment, but I 
think the notion that it’s the Garden of Eden is telling 
us a lot more of what was on the Reverend West’s 
mind than what was in the mind of the people who 
built it originally. (Link to video is on page 205.)

Brush Creek Route
Brush Creek Route
Orientation. The 1,500-­acre Fort Hill Earthworks and Nature Pre-
serve (13614 Fort Hill Rd., Hillsboro) is largely owned by the Ohio 
History Connection, but has been expanded and is currently man-
aged by the Arc of Appalachia, whose website offers up-­to-­date 
information, descriptions, and trail guides. Three well-­improved 
but strenuous trails total eleven miles, mapped and explained on 
a prominent kiosk next to the parking and picnic area. The Fort 
Trail ascends to the 33-­acre Hopewell enclosure, follows its western 
wall, and returns to the parking area via a steep descent. At the far 
southern end of the park, other connecting trails lead to an open 
meadow with a well-­preserved circular earthwork. The three-­mile 
Gorge Trail follows rugged terrain along Baker Fork.
Edge of Appalachia Preserve. The abundant and diverse natural won-
ders of Adams County surround West Union, chief among them the 
21,000-­acre multisite collection of the Edge of Appalachia Preserve 
System (headquarters at 4274 Waggoner Riffle Road, West Union). 
These lands are co-­owned and managed by the Cincinnati Museum 
Center and The Nature Conservancy; and open year-­round, dawn 
to dusk. The most popular among a variety of hiking experiences is 
a large rock promontory with long views called Buzzardroost Rock; 
its trailhead is near the village of Lynx (16299 SR 125, West Union). 
Other sites offer dense forests, dolomite cliffs, expansive vistas, 
and large dry limestone prairie landscapes. Trail maps and infor-
mation are available on the Cincinnati Museum Center’s website.
Ohio Brush Creek. From Serpent Mound to the Ohio River, this idyl-
lic stream is a paddler’s paradise. The forty-­two-­mile scenic and se-
cluded waterway has nine put-­in points allowing for either short or 
multiday trips, with some sections suitable for floating. It is clean 
and full of fish, with practically no visual intrusions on the natu-
ral landscape for the entire distance. Inquire locally for details and 
water level conditions.
Sinking Spring. Heading north from Serpent Mound or Locust 
Grove along Zane’s Trace (State Route 41) passes through the vil-
lage of Sinking Spring, whose architectural highlight is the 1831  
Octagon Schoolhouse (2 SR 124, Sinking Spring), which is unique in 
Ohio. It is set back from the road on well-­maintained grounds, and 
although the doors are kept locked, the large windows offer ample 
views of the furnished interior.
FORT HILL
An ancient hilltop earthwork stands today in one of the Midwest’s larg-
est old-­growth forests, an unparalleled combination of adventurous 
hiking, converging ecosystems, and ancient earthen architecture.
One of Ohio’s several  
octagonal buildings is a  
well-­cared-for, though 
disused, schoolhouse in the 
center of Sinking Spring.

145
Brush Creek Route
Brush Creek Route
The stone and timber picnic area structures at Fort Hill, like those at several other Ohio 
sites, were built by the 1930s Depression-­era Civilian Conservation Corps.
In winter the walls and gateways of Fort 
Hill are clearly visible from below, outlining 
the edge of the plateau.
Hilltop trails at Fort Hill pass along the 
earthwork walls among magnificent 
old-­growth trees; sandstone paving blocks, 
mined from the hilltop, are everywhere.
Ecology and History. The park is a remarkable convergence of ecosys-
tems. Rare plant and animal life thrives in micro-­conditions which 
can vary dramatically with minor changes in elevation and expo-
sure. The lower bedrock at Fort Hill is dolomite, a soluble limestone 
which, when exposed, creates what is called a karst landscape. Fort 
Hill abounds with its characteristic features—­rock arches, palisades, 
gorges, seeps, and sinkholes, plus spectacular spring wildflower 
displays (the peak is early to mid-­April). The earthworks were built 
around 400 CE and survive today as a rare example of a Hopewell-­
era site that has not been disrupted by modern human activities. 
Like Fort Ancient and the other hilltop enclosures, the walls here 
were almost certainly not a fort but rather the massive delineation 
of a grand ceremonial enclosure whose prominent setting ensured it 
would be visible to visitors arriving on foot from all directions. Fort 
Hill has been an archaeological and natural preserve since 1974.
1 Picnic Area. All Fort Hill hiking trails begin at the picnic area 
with its shady parking lot, small stream, and stone and timber struc-
tures erected here in the 1930s by the Civilian Conservation Corps. 
These and similar structures at other Ohio archaeological sites were 
part of the public outreach and facility enhancement initiatives by 
Henry Shetrone, the former newspaper reporter who was then the 
curator of archaeology at the Ohio History Connection. An infor-
mation kiosk welcomes all visitors in front of the picnic area and 
illustrates the preserve’s hiking trails, 
all of which begin at this spot. The 2.2-­
mile Fort Trail heads south, ascending 
the eastern flank of the hill beneath 
the ancient earthwork’s eastern and 
southern walls and gateways (visible 
from below in winter).
2 Western Wall. The Fort Trail attains 360 feet of vertical elevation, 
skirting just outside the enclosure’s southern wall before enter-
ing through the westernmost of the earthwork’s four prominent 
prow-­shaped gateways. The well-­preserved 6-­to-­15-­foot-­high wall 
and its accompanying inner ditch parallel the trail between here 
and the dramatic northern gate. The enclosure’s level interior is on 
the right. Many fine views are offered of the ancient earthworks, 
including several of its twenty-­nine other openings, and the large 
sandstone slabs with which the walls were covered. The giant trees 
of the old-­growth forest (some up to 20 feet in circumference) frame 
views across the landscape. A large pond—­at times brimming with 
frogs—­is visible near the northeastern wall.
Wall Construction. Tangled among the roots of Fort Hill’s giant old-­
growth trees are masses of now-­broken sandstone slabs, once part 
of an elaborate construction process that helped to ensure the wall’s 
durability on this high vulnerable slope. An inner core of stone 
blocks was laid down first along the path of the wall, providing a 
kind of ballast, followed by two parallel lines of Earth and stone. 
Those were then capped and unified 
by a final layer, up to 15 feet high, and 
surfaced with carefully shaped and 
fitted sandstone slabs taken from the 
top of the plateau.


--- PDF PAGE 81 ---

146
147
Brush Creek Route
Brush Creek Route
3 Northern Gate. As the Fort Trail approaches the enclosure’s 
northern end, the level plateau constricts into a narrow promon- 
tory—­a sheer rock-­walled outcrop resembling a ship’s prow. The 
ditch and two sections of wall encircle this stone platform, about 20 
feet below, and between them form the site’s widest gateway. Squier 
and Davis described this as Fort Hill’s “most remarkable” feature—­
for its rock walls, wide vistas, and evidence of fires having burned 
on the stone surface. From another gateway to the right (east), the 
Fort Trail begins its steep descent toward the parking area.
4 Small Circle. Other trail segments, including a piece of Ohio’s 
Buckeye Trail, lead out to a meadow at the southern end of the 
preserve where ancient ceremonial facilities and workshops were 
located. Emerging from the woods, and on the far right across the 
meadow, lies a treasure of Ohio’s ancient earthen architecture—­a 
small earthen ring. Though well preserved, its low outline is most 
visible in winter or when mowed. This may be the best kept of the 
several hundred that were recorded throughout southern Ohio 
during the 1800s. The timber structure preceding the circular 
earthwork, along with another 
nearby building, was part of a 
bustling Hopewell-­era craft and 
ceremonial area.
Arbor and Big House. The circle was partially excavated by Ohio 
History Connection archaeologist Raymond Baby (BAH-­bee) in 
1953. His exploratory trenches uncovered evidence of a double ring 
of posts (he called it an “arbor”) enclosing a 174-­foot-­diameter open 
space. A few hundred feet southeast of the ring is where Baby also 
found evidence (not visible today) of a 120-­foot-­long rectangular 
building. It suggests other known Hopewell-­era ceremonial halls 
like those at the Seip Earthworks. The 2,000-­year-­old prepared floor 
was littered with broken pottery, stone tools, and flint chips; pits 
were filled with debris from flintknapping. The building’s gridded 
timber framework was covered with sheets of bark or skins.
5 Gorge Trail. If time and daylight permit, return to the parking 
area via the challenging, four-­hour Gorge Trail. It follows the deep 
limestone crevice created by Baker Fork, a tributary of Brush 
Creek. The path climbs and descends several promontories, offer-
ing views of the river and karst country rock formations from both 
above and below. The plant life changes to those favoring these 
sheer rock cliffs: Ferns and tree roots cling to cliff faces high above 
stone arches and waterfalls. The trail passes through the shell of 
a preserved log cabin before returning to the parking lot from the 
northwest.
ROCKY FORK GORGE
In the nearby village of Cynthiana, take Barrett Mill Road to its intersection with 
Cave Road. Ahead on Barrett Mill is the trailhead of the Miller Nature Sanctuary State 
Nature Preserve. North on Cave Road is the Barrett’s Rim trailhead. Both lead into 
the cliff-­lined landscapes and habitats of the Rocky Fork Gorge. Barrett’s Rim is one 
of many properties of the Arc of Appalachia preserve system headquartered at the 
Appalachian Forest Museum farther up Cave Road.
HIGHLANDS NATURE SANCTUARY
At 3,000 acres, the Highlands Nature Sanctuary is the largest pre-
serve in the Arc of Appalachia’s system, with a wide variety of hik-
ing trail experiences centered on the beautiful Rocky Fork Gorge 
and the Appalachian Forest Museum.
The Arc of Appalachia. Between Fort Hill and Bainbridge lie many of 
the land holdings comprising this extensive system of natural pre-
serves and earthwork sites. The Arc is a fast-­growing private non-
profit with properties throughout south central Ohio and beyond, 
including a variety of overnight accommodations (which can be 
booked on its website). The organization’s headquarters at the Ap-
palachian Forest Museum (7660 Cave Rd., Bainbridge) is open daily 
from mid-­March through mid-­November (see its website for dates 
and times). The adjacent Rocky Fork Gorge is known for its stun-
ning rock formations, ancient white cedars, spectacular wildflower 
displays, grottos, springs, and stone arches. (The area was formerly 
a for-­profit tourist attraction called “Seven Caves.”) Maps and thor-
ough descriptions of the Highlands Nature Sanctuary’s many trails 
are available on the Arc’s website.
At the southern end of the Fort Hill property, 
an intact small circular 
earthwork appears in an open 
meadow, covering the remains of a 
double-­columned timber “arbor.”

Trails from the Appalachian Forest Museum descend into the narrow canyon of the Rocky

THE ANCIENT FOREST
Arc director Nancy Stranahan suggests how the ancient earthwork builders 
and their monuments add meaning to the Paint Creek region’s natural 
wonders: Here where we live, in the Paint Creek Valley, we really feel like 
we live in the Valley of the Kings. Everywhere we go, we’re walking in their 
footsteps. We know their presence. We feel the honor and the obligation to take 
care of the land and maintain the landscape. These people just didn’t know. 
It wasn’t a postcard, it wasn’t a nature preserve, it wasn’t wilderness. It was 
their home. It was their community. They were 
part of that tapestry. Our organization is partly 
here because of the inspiration of the people that 
came before us. We want to not only maintain 
the memory of those people, and the history of 
those people, and the speculations of what they 
might be able to teach us if they were here today, 
but we also want to maintain their landscape. 
(Link to video is on page 205.)
Appalachian Forest Museum. The museum at the Arc’s headquarters 
provides staff information, a gift shop, and guides to the preserve 
system’s many land holdings and hiking destinations throughout 
the Highlands Nature Sanctuary and the wider region. Exhibits tell 
the story of the eastern broadleaf forest of the United States—­the 
largest of its kind in the world. Although that forest is now torn 
into tiny shreds by settlement, agriculture, and development, vis-
itors are encouraged to grasp the idea that it is still a huge single 
forest, to identify with it, and to help preserve it. A series of large 
murals offer an immersive vision of the forest at various stages of 
its development.
TO THE WORLD HERITAGE ROUTE
Continue one mile north of the Appalachian Forest Museum to where Cave Road joins 
US Route 50, and turn right for Bainbridge. The road enters the western end of the 
beautiful Paint Creek Valley, once home to a spectacular concentration of ancient 
earthworks. On the way, about 1,000 feet east of the intersection of State Route 41 
and US Route 50 (called Hirn’s corner), a large, tree-­covered mound stands near 
the highway.
TO THE LOWER SCIOTO OR LOWER MIAMI ROUTES
From West Union, State Route 125 leads east to Portsmouth and the Lower Scioto 
Route via a huge state forest and Shawnee State Park. Or take State Route 41 (Zane’s 
Trace) south from West Union to its historic destination of Maysville, Kentucky (with a 
fine downtown, and the nearby historic village of Washington), and then downstream 
along the Ohio to Ripley, Ohio (a major Underground Railroad site), and to Kentucky’s 
quaint Augusta (crossing the Ohio on its picturesque ferry), and on to Cincinnati and 
the Lower Miami Route.

from Virginia. I-­houses can be spotted often in towns and along the

Ohio and Erie Canal. About twelve miles north of Mound City, just 
off State Route 104 in the village of Westfall, Canal Road parallels 
the old canal route, including a rare, beautiful section where the 
waterway has been restored and about three miles of the towpath 
have been converted into a grassy walking trail. Canal Park (23352 
Canal Rd., Circleville), at the restored section’s northern end, has a 
shelter, interpretive signage, and the remains of the canal system’s 
stone-­built infrastructure—­traces of a dam across the river and the 
impressive hulks of a feeder channel used to maintain the canal’s 
water level. Between here and the center of Circleville, the canal 
once crossed high above the river on a massive timber aqueduct; 
the bases of its pylons still lie in the river.

North of Chillicothe, the hills defining

the flat till plains en route to Columbus. 
Along the west bank of the river near 
the historic town of Circleville (named

many vibrant urban districts, the state

The Squaring. Within twenty years of the town’s founding, com-
plaints arose about its circular layout: The yard around the court-
house attracted swine, and the circular street plan was inefficient, 
especially with the growing commerce coming into town via the  
new canal. By 1837, the complainers prevailed, and the state-­
chartered “Circleville Squaring Company” began the job of destroy- 
ing the unique town plan, and with it what remained of the earth-
works, although it took 
fifteen years because of 
continued protests. 
The best-­known image of 
Circleville’s former cir-
cularity was made by a 
local candymaker named 
Gottlieb Wittich, whose 
heirs still run the oldest 
family-­owned candy store 
in the country (117 West 
High Street).
Local postmaster Caleb Atwater illustrated the double ring 
and adjoining square at Circleville; its long central axis 
became the town’s Main Street.
Ohio’s Canal System. The Ohio and Erie Canal was one of two ambi-
tious systems created in the 1830s to carry goods between the Great 
Lakes and the Ohio River. The cost nearly bankrupted the state, but 
as the backers had hoped, the channels could carry products from 
Ohio’s farms, mills, and mines to eastern markets (via Lake Erie and 
New York’s newly completed Erie Canal) more easily than wagons, 
and at one-­fifth the cost. Because both the canals and the ancient 
earthworks were built near Ohio’s rivers, they can often be found 
near each other. The canals gradually lost out to the railroads, and 
closed for good in 1913, yet aptly named towns like Lockington  
and Lockbourne still hold the great stone hulks of their locks,  
gates, and channels.
CIRCLEVILLE
Though the ancient earthwork in which it was originally laid out and 
for which it was named did not survive for long, downtown Circleville 
retains its central axis as Main Street, now lined with many grand 
nineteenth-­century buildings.
The Earthworks. Circleville was laid out in 1810, carefully planned 
within a twenty-­two-­acre, double-­walled, ancient earthen ring. 
With its attached square, this was one of the Hopewell culture’s 
greatest earthen masterpieces. Today, downtown’s Main Street 
(US Route 22) follows what had been the earthwork’s central axis.  
Admiring the precise ancient geometry, the founders laid out their 
streets in a radial pattern within the circle, building their small oc-
tagonal courthouse where a central burial mound stood. They began 
with preservation ideals, like their predecessors in Marietta (pages 
195–198), envisioning a novel blend of ancient and modern.
Caleb Atwater. Circleville’s early postmaster was Caleb Atwater,  
a brilliant and eccentric polymath whose 1820 Description of the  
Antiquities Discovered in the State of Ohio and Other Western States was 
the first systematic illustrated account of the earthworks. He advocated 
for the preservation of Circleville’s double circle and square, but it 
was not to be. Despite their loss, downtown Circleville today is well 
endowed with grand old buildings and flourishing commerce. A mural 
depicts the original octagonal courthouse, and a historical marker 
commemorates Atwater and his many remarkable achievements.
Among those most horrified by the 
“squaring” of Circleville was Caleb 
Atwater, author of the first scholarly 
survey of Ohio Valley antiquity.
Gottlieb Wittich’s historic 
drawing still adorns the metal 
candy boxes sold at Wittich’s 
Candy Shop and other historic 
memorabilia around town.

Upper Scioto Route

THE PICKAWAY PLAINS
An excursion south of Circleville along US Route 23 leads into a 
gently rolling prairie landscape—­a region of great fertility, and 
the setting for some of Ohio’s most important American Indian 
settlements.
Mount Oval. Five miles south of the center of town, on a low hill 
just off Emerson Road, stands a classical mansion built in 1832. The 
Renick family had come from Virginia to breed cattle on these fer-
tile hills. From its hilltop site, between two good-­sized streams, the 
sweeping views over the landscape are reminiscent of those from 
Jefferson’s Monticello, and the unusual plan is almost certainly 
derived from a Jefferson sketch entitled “A temple for a garden.”  
A square central room with a pyramidal roof and rounded dormer 
adjoins three corner rooms with open-­air porches between them 
as would have been more common back home in Virginia. The 
grounds are open to visitors, and the Pickaway County Historical 
Society website offers information on events and other opportuni-
ties to tour the house.
Cornstalk Town. East of Mount Oval along Emerson Road, just past 
a grove of trees, a historical marker commemorates the important 
Shawnee towns here on the Pickaway Plains, specifically those led 
by the eloquent Chief Cornstalk and his brilliant sister, Nonhelema, 
in the third quarter of the 1700s. Nonhelema favored peace with 
the settlers, as far as possible during those fraught decades, but  
Cornstalk (Hokolesqua) led the Shawnee forces in Dunmore’s War—­
the struggle against Virginia militias over encroaching settlements 
to the south in Kentucky. He was forced to retreat to here, but 
then agreed to give up Shawnee claims in Kentucky in exchange 
for these Pickaway towns to be spared. The threats from Virginia’s  
expansion into Kentucky had already forced the relocation of Lower 
Shawneetown (the large Indigenous village at the mouth of the  
Scioto; see page 170) to here in 1758.
Logan Elm. The site of that 1775 peace council is less than two more 
miles south, off State Route 361 (4501 SR 361, Circleville). Logan Elm 
State Memorial honors Cornstalk and Nonhelema, and features the 
poignant story of Chief Logan, another American Indian leader 
who befriended settlers and hoped for peace. Inscribed on a stone 
monument are the words of “Logan’s Lament”—­his eloquent cry of 
Historic Center. The corner of Main and Court Streets is the heart 
of Circleville’s historic district. Shops line West Main Street, while 
south on Court Street are two of the town’s grandest buildings: the 
tall and lavishly ornamented Pickaway Masonic Lodge #23 and, one 
block south, the two-­toned classical Pickaway County Courthouse, 
with its oddly positioned tower. The latter was designed in 1861 by a 
local carriage builder named William Doane, who had just taken up 
an interest in architecture. Several fine historic houses line South 
Court Street as far as the huge, medieval-­inspired, stone-­built Cas-
tle Inn. West Union Street has more historic gems including, at the 
corner of brick-­paved Scioto Street, the 1840s Clarke-­May House. It 
serves as the Pickaway County Historical Society’s museum (check 
its website for hours) with exhibits covering every historical period. 
One room showcases many Hopewell-­era flint, stone, and pottery 
tools from the area. Another highlight is a scale model of the Cir-
cleville Earthworks executed by a local dentist using dental plaster.
Octagons and Pumpkins. Though Circleville’s original octagonal 
courthouse is gone, a group of today’s local citizens (called the 
Roundtown Conservancy) have preserved an unusual example of a 
private octagonal house, the 1856 Gregg-­Crites House. They had it 
moved in 2004 from a Walmart building site to a safe location (440 
Crites Rd., where it is still awaiting restoration) and open it on occa-
sion for tours and events. The house is visible from Progress Park-
way on the southern edge of town, not far from the world’s largest 
pumpkin water tower—­the latter symbolizing the annual Circleville 
Pumpkin Show every October, when this pleasant town feels like 
the center of Ohio.
The tall Pickaway Masonic Lodge #23 is one of several extraordinarily exuberant nineteenth-­
century buildings in Circleville’s historic downtown.

The words of Chief Logan’s famous lamentation are inscribed on a stone monument near 
the site of the ancient elm tree under which they were read.
CHIEF LOGAN AND HIS WORDS
Logan was born a Cayuga, in Pennsylvania, where he grew up with many 
settlers as friends. In 1770, he married a Shawnee woman and moved to the 
Ohio Country. He urged peace with settlers until some of them attacked his 
village, killing his mother and sister—­after which he led his people on raids of 
revenge against the settlers. He refused to attend the Pickaway Plains peace 
conference, but sent a speech which was read, according to legend, beneath 
an already ancient spreading elm tree: I appeal to any white man to say that 
he ever entered Logan’s cabin, but I gave him meat; that he ever came naked, 
but I clothed him . . . He will not turn his heel to save his life. Who is there to 
mourn for Logan? No one. The monument to his words stands near the site of 
the original “Logan Elm” (a replacement was planted in the 1960s).
betrayal and despair after the descent into violence. That violence 
had been building for some time despite those who, like Logan and 
Nonhelema, were inclined toward peace. Beginning in the previ-
ous century, Shawnee and Delaware peoples, driven westward by 
colonial settlement in the east, had established villages and farms 
here. But the continued encroachment by new colonists provoked 
the escalating conflicts leading up to Dunmore’s War—­which ended 
here on the Pickaway Plains.
Snake Den Mounds. At this privately owned earthwork site about 
seven miles northeast of Circleville, a set of three large mounds and 
a squircle (rounded rectangle) are defined by mowing and ringed 
by a large ditched enclosure. Magnetometry reveals long rows of 
pits and at least two post-­circles nearby (still to be investigated), 
along with another mound. Views from the gentle ridgetop site ex-
tend all the way to downtown Columbus. The Snake Den Mounds 
Preservation Society offers scheduled public hikes led by expert 
guides as well as group tours by appointment (consult its website).
COLUMBUS
The fast-­growing metro region of Columbus centers on state gov-
ernment and Ohio’s largest research university. The city maintains 
a lively arts and cultural scene and is home to the Ohio History 
Connection and its large museum.
Ohio Statehouse. On Capitol Square in the heart of downtown 
stands the distinctive and relatively modest Ohio Statehouse, begun 
in 1839. Designed in the Greek Revival style dominant at the time, 
it reflected the state’s—­and the nation’s—­democratic idealism. The 
austere cupola was modeled on the circular Tholos of Delphi, then 
just recently discovered in Greece. Its lower-­level walls contain rub-
ble footers taken from a large nearby mound; its destruction also 
provided clay for many of the bricks in the building’s inner walls. 
Huge murals in the rotunda commemorate 
scenes from the state’s history, including the 
1795 Treaty of Greenville, which forced the 
tribes to give up much of the land that would 
become the state of Ohio eight years later.
Urban Districts. Lively urban districts and his-
toric residential neighborhoods now stretch both north and south 
along High Street from downtown Columbus. To the south is the 
picturesque German Village, where brick streets and sidewalks 
connect block after block of small brick houses, interspersed with 
shops and eateries. More historic districts spread northward, with 
much lively commerce and entertainment stretching out along 
High Street to the huge campus of The Ohio State University. Ohio’s 
The Ohio Statehouse is a good example of Greek 
Revival monumental architecture, reflecting the 
democratic aspirations of the nation in the early 1800s.

flagship research university boasts renowned departments in many 
disciplines, prodigious research programs, and top-­tier sports 
teams. The campus is also home to the world-­famous Wexner Cen-
ter for the Arts (with changing exhibits) designed by architect Peter 
Eisenman in the 1980s.
Ohio History Center. Several blocks east of the Ohio State University 
campus (800 East 17th Ave.) is the Ohio History Center, home of 
the Ohio History Connection with its collections, exhibits, shops, 
and offices. It is housed in a massive Brutalist concrete edifice—­a 
fine example of that often-­despised style from the 1970s. Exhibits 
both permanent and temporary portray many themes in the state’s 
history—­including settlement, transportation, industry, education, 
and sports—­and a justly famous mastodon named “Conway.” An ex-
hibit installed in 2022 called Indigenous Wonders of Our World high-
lights the UNESCO-­inscribed earthworks and includes a display of 
ancient artistry co-­curated with significant tribal consultation. The 
large Ohio History Connection campus also includes Ohio Village, 
a collection of historic structures assembled from throughout the 
state and filled with shops, crafts, interpretive displays, and living 
history programs.
Ohio History Connection. In 1885, a group of dedicated individu-
als founded the Ohio State Archaeological and Historical Society. 
The national significance of Ohio’s earthwork sites was well-­known 
through important early publications—­most notably Squier and 
Davis’s Ancient Monuments of the Mississippi Valley—­and this created 
a problem. Eastern universities like Harvard were sending people 
out west to study the sites, who would then take collections back 
east. The goal was to have an Ohio-­based organization to do archae-
ological research and maintain collections in the state. Within just 
a few years, property acquisitions were also underway, notably 
at Fort Ancient and Serpent 
Mound. A name change in the 
1950s dropped “archaeological” 
from the title; another in the 
2010s established the current 
“Ohio History Connection.” Supported today by both state and pri-
vate funds, the organization manages over fifty historic sites and 
museums statewide. Since 2009, it has implemented active consul-
tation with the tribes descended from those who signed the Treaty 
of Greenville concerning artifact repatriation and all matters to do 
with the state’s Indigenous history.
Jeffers Mound. Surviving on a residential side street in the pleasant 
suburb of Worthington (in a small park across from 6430 Plesen-
ton Dr.), the twenty-­foot-­high Jeffers Mound was the focal point of a 
larger eight-­acre complex atop this sixty-­foot bluff overlooking the 
Olentangy River (pronounced oh-­lun-­TAN-­jee). The whole ensem-
ble consisted of a rectangular enclosure wall about three feet high, 
aligned to the cardinal points, and two circles. In the late 1970s, 
archaeologists located the postholes of a Hopewell-­era building 
whose rounded-­corner-­rectangular plan is now outlined on the 
ground near the mound. The site is maintained by the Worthington 
Historical Society, and visitors are urged to respect the quiet neigh-
borhood surroundings.

Highbanks Metro Park preserves beautifully 
carved headstones.

spot where the trail passes through them.

TO THE WORLD HERITAGE ROUTE
From greater Columbus, it is a short, direct connection to the World Heritage Route at 
Granville (page 85), either by taking State Route 161 from the northern part of the city 
via New Albany, or by heading directly out from downtown on Broad Street through 
Bexley (State Route 16).
TO THE UPPER MIAMI ROUTE
Heading southwest from greater Columbus, through London and South Charleston, 
leads to Cedarville, Clifton, and the Upper Miami Route (see map, page 105). Along 
the southwestern edges of the Columbus metropolitan region, large expanses of 
parkland line Big Darby Creek (a designated scenic river). Battelle Darby Creek Metro 
Park includes forests, wetlands, many amenities, and fields of ranging bison.
Highbanks Metro Park. A large park just north of Columbus (en-
trance at 9466 Columbus Pike, Lewis Center) contains two Adena-­
era mounds and a semicircular earthen enclosure reached by trails 
from the park’s Nature Center. The name “Highbanks” refers not to 
the ancient earthwork but to the 110-­foot exposed shale precipice 
falling from the site to the Olentangy River. The 2.3-­mile Overlook 
Trail passes through dense woods, through steep ravines, and be-
side a preserved cemetery plot, before entering the earthwork en-
closure across its watery outer ditch. The wall’s 1,500-­foot length 
was designed to encircle the promontory, diminishing on both 
sides along steep-­sided ravines. A long extension of the wall is visi-
ble to the north and west; the circuit was probably a village bound-
ary between eight and fourteen centuries ago.
Ferris-­Wright Park and Earthworks. North of the city, in a newly de-
veloped park (4400 Emerald Pkwy., Dublin), lie the partially visible 
remains of at least three small earthwork enclosures of Hopewell 
vintage. Prominent undulations north of the property’s restored 
farmhouse outline a 160-­foot-­diameter circle and its interior ditch. 
Just to the northeast of this circle, differential plantings present a 
130-­by-­300-­foot rectangular enclosure that once stood 6 feet high. In 
the far northeast corner of the park (and just half on the property), 
a third enclosure is also worn down by farming and not easily seen 
today. The park’s signage and infrastructure designs feature some 
of the best-­known symbols from Hopewell ceremonial artistry.
Shrum Mound. About five miles northwest of downtown Columbus 
(3141 McKinley Ave.), the well-­preserved 20-­foot-­tall Shrum Mound 
stands in the center of tiny Campbell Memorial Park. Despite the 
surroundings, the immediate setting provides a sense of tranquil-
ity, bordered by a low, symmetrical stone wall and a line of trees. 
This is one of the last ancient mounds left in the Columbus area, 
now named after the family that donated it to the Ohio History Con-
nection. Its conical form and isolated position suggest an Adena-­
era burial mound, although there have been no archaeological 
investigations to confirm that. The park, named after former Ohio 
governor James E. Campbell, is suspended between a gigantic old 
limestone quarry on one side, and rushing road traffic on the other, 
suggesting how close the mound came to being destroyed.

Ancient Teays Valley. Southeast of Chillicothe rise the steep, widely 
spaced bluffs of the Scioto River’s ancient preglacial valley. Eons 
ago, North America’s largest river flowed northward through here 
before the glaciers gradually dammed it up, producing a huge lake 
and then today’s Ohio River channel. Follow the valley on Three 
Locks Road, or take US Route 23 directly to Waverly. The historic 
Emmitt House Hotel, this town’s premiere historic site, burned 
down in 2014, but Canal Park (211 Ford Ave.) preserves a bit of the 
old waterway’s stonework, and the Pike Heritage Museum (110 
South Market St.) occupies an 1859 former German Evangelical 
church with a tall brick tower. Between Waverly and Piketon, the 
valley’s eastern bluffs open where another equally wide Teays-­age 
river channel comes in from the east. Vast, and also visible from 
space (or Google satellite view), it is now drained only by the tiny 
meandering Big Beaver Creek.
Mound Cemetery. Leave Waverly on US Route 23 and, just past 
Piketon, go east on State Route 32, and then turn left on Mound 
Cemetery Road. The cemetery is dominated by a large Adena-­era 
conjoined mound topped by a flag and surrounded by graves both 
old and new. Early settlers often recognized the ancient mounds as 
sacred sites of burial, and one way of attempting respect and ensur-
ing preservation was to plan new cemeteries around them—­here as 
at Newtown, Marietta, and elsewhere. The large primary mound 
8
Lower Scioto Route
The broad Scioto Valley heads south from Chillicothe to Waverly and 
the Tremper Mound, then to Portsmouth with remains of a huge 
complex of earthworks at the most dramatic river confluence in the 
region; well-­preserved sections stand across the river in Kentucky. A 
loop to Jackson enters the Hanging Rock region with unusual glacial-­
era geology, a restored landmark of Ohio’s early iron industry, and 
the Indigenous petroglyphs near the village of Leo.
A large cemetery just south of Piketon preserves a complex, conjoined, Adena-­era mound.

archaeological record confirms that this 
majestic ancient earthen portal is now

The reconstructed remains of the Tremper Mound are enclosed by an oval earthwork,  
both enhanced today by differential mowing.
links to smaller segments by an unusual undulating “apron” form. 
Somewhere just south of here along the road to Portsmouth was the 
location of the Piketon Graded Way, strikingly illustrated by Squier 
and Davis, but long since lost to road construction.
TREMPER MOUND
This irregularly shaped Hopewell-­era mound is known for the 
exquisitely carved effigy smoking pipes found beneath it in 1915; 
today, in its grand valley setting, it is beautifully presented by inter-
pretive mowing and thoughtful signage.
Orientation. Along the western (right) bank of the Scioto, State 
Route 104 traces the Ohio and Erie Canal’s abandoned channel to-
ward Portsmouth. Just south of the intersection with State Route 73, 
Tremper Mound rises in an open field, now owned and managed by 
the Arc of Appalachia (consult its website for visitor information). 
A well-­marked entrance leads to a parking area, and a kiosk fea-
turing paintings by noted muralist Robert Dafford announces the 
path into the sacred precinct. Differential mowing clearly defines 
the mound’s subtle forms and oval enclosure wall, while wooded 
hills and giant shade trees frame this high, open terrace site mag-
nificently. The aura of an ancient landscape can be felt powerfully 
here, free of modern intrusions.
The Earthwork. One of the earliest and most important Hopewell 
sites, Tremper dates from the late first century BCE. The mound 
vaguely suggests some kind of animal, but more likely corresponds 
(as at other sites) to the floor plan of the complex, multichambered 
mortuary building whose ritual remains it was built to cover. That 
structure’s floor held twelve fire basins and the accumulated, cre-
mated remains of about 300 people. More than 500 items were 
laid down in a communal offering, all of them purposely broken.  
In 1915, archaeologist William Mills of what is now the Ohio His-
tory Connection excavated the mound, made detailed notes about 
the building and its contents, and then restored the earthwork to an 
approximation of its original form.
The Tremper Pipes. Tremper is best known for its 136 stone-­carved 
smoking pipes. Sixty of the pipe bowls were elegantly carved to 
portray the creatures common in Ohio Valley woods, meadows, 
and skies—­turtles, bears, frogs, wolves, birds, and more. The de-
tails of their faces, paws, bills, and wings are both intricate and en-
dearing: A bobcat’s whiskers are delicately incised; a beaver’s eyes 
are inset pearls. Many of the pipes have near twins in the set found 
six decades earlier by Squier and Davis at Mound City, forty miles 
upstream along the Scioto. Expert analysis has recently suggested 
that pipes in the two collections may be the work of the same four 
or five master artists. The stone came from Minnesota and Illinois, 
even though there was an available source just a few miles away. 
The pipes are now in the collections (though not on exhibit) at the 
Ohio History Connection.

Lower Scioto Route
Shops, cafés, a brewery, and historic 
storefronts line the Boneyfiddle District 
near Portsmouth’s riverfront.
PORTSMOUTH
A huge and elaborate Hopewell earthwork complex once sprawled 
across the grandest river confluence in the region; here at the mouth 
of the Scioto, parallel-­walled roadways headed off in all directions.
History and Setting. Portsmouth occupies the spectacular set-
ting where two of the region’s largest valleys converge. The Ohio 
River is joined here by the Scioto flowing down from the Hopewell 
heartland. A multi-­component earthwork complex once filled this 
dramatic valley, linked by wide walled roadways. At this major 
crossroads, especially for river traffic, settlement seems to have 
been continuous; early European traders found a thriving Shawnee 
town along these riverbanks. Today, the city retains some historic 
urban fabric, with shops and cafés, in its Boneyfiddle District and 
nearby downtown. The important cultural sites include a fine mu-
seum, a long series of justly famous riverside murals, and (across 
in Kentucky) a well-­preserved geometric earthwork. A small park 
at the end of Front Street overlooks the Scioto confluence. The his-
tory of the city and the surrounding region is told in a thorough 
website and mobile app created by Shawnee State University, called 
Scioto Historical.
Mound Park. The Portsmouth Earthworks once stretched for miles 
across terraces on both sides of the Ohio River. The best-­preserved 
section on the Ohio side stands in Mound Park (1817 Grant St.), 
where a single large horseshoe-­
shaped earthwork opens to the 
south and rises to a height of ten 
feet at its closed northern end. 
The 1848 map shows that there 
was a pair of these, with a circle 
around them, from which earthen lines extended in every direc-
tion, even across the Ohio River in what is now Kentucky. At least 
ten miles of these walled roadways connected several remarkable 
features in this giant valley. Two ringed mounds once stood up-
stream on the Kentucky side. A square called the “Old Fort” is still 
visible on a downstream terrace, also in Kentucky. Another pair of 
lines headed northwest toward 
the Scioto and could once have 
led to ancient points north, in-
cluding the Tremper Mound.
Floodwall Murals. Portsmouth’s ancient earthworks are illustrated 
on an ambitious series of murals decorating the city’s floodwall—­a 
heroic act of compensation for the loss of its riverfront setting. 
Monumental, detailed, immersive paintings by Robert Dafford 
stretch for nearly half a mile, vividly telling the history of this lo-
cation from antiquity to the present. The chronological sequence 
begins at the eastern end (629 Front St.), where the first panel por-
trays the Sun rising over the ancient earthworks, and the second 
overlooks the large Indigenous settlement here, Lower Shawnee-
town. Many of the images are trompe l’oeil—­with period views to 
the river as if the wall weren’t there at all. A nearby opening offers 
access to riverbank parklands below, with a historical marker com-
memorating Portsmouth’s river history, and good views of the steep 
Kentucky hills and the suspension bridge.
A virtual tour of the ancient Portsmouth 
landscape reveals the many mounds, 
enclosures, and walled roadways that 
once spread out across the terraces of the 
Scioto and Ohio River confluence.

Southern Ohio Museum. An im-
pressive classical bank building 
downtown is now the home of 
the Southern Ohio Museum and 
Cultural Center (825 Gallia St., 
consult its website for hours), with both permanent and changing 
exhibits on local history and the visual arts, and a performing arts 
schedule. In the center’s Wertz Gallery, an exhibit entitled Art of 
the Ancients showcases over 10,000 objects from the Adena and 
Hopewell cultures, and from the area’s other Indigenous inhabi-
tants. Portsmouth residents Charles and William Wertz, father and 
son, gathered this massive collection from nearby plowed fields 
throughout much of the twentieth century. Many of the items are 
elegantly displayed and interpreted, though equally impressive in a 
way are the shelves at the back crammed with prodigious numbers 
of stone axes, spear points, and other ancient tools.
Old Fort Earthworks. The largest 
surviving part of the Portsmouth 
Earthworks complex is its south-
western extension: an 800-­foot, 
diagonally oriented square en-
closure across the river in Kentucky. The site was preserved with 
help from the Archaeological Conservancy, and although not yet 
outfitted for visitors it is clearly visible from the highway in two 
spots. About 3,000 feet southwest from the US Route 23 bypass 
bridge, a large mound appears next to the road on the right—­it is 
the square’s eastern corner. After another 1,000 feet or so, a ceme-
tery on the left (Collier Memorial Gardens, 1805 Kentucky Route 8, 
South Portsmouth) offers the only place to pull off the road, across 
from a historical marker about Shannoah (Lower Shawneetown). 
Across an open field, the square’s southwestern wall stands promi-
nently on the right beneath a line of trees. The whole figure shows 
Muralist Robert Dafford’s depiction of the 
sun rising over ancient Portsmouth, with 
its two horseshoe mounds, begins the 
riverfront’s historical mural series.
The Southern Ohio Museum and Cultural 
Center presents a huge and rare collection 
of ancient Indigenous artistry in its 
Wertz Gallery.
At least two walls of the Old Fort Earthworks’ diagonal square are essentially intact today 
beneath tree lines.
LIGHT AND SHADOW
Archaeologist A. Gwynn Henderson considers earthwork design as an 
exercise in working with light and shadow, as well as site and position: 
These are people who live in the outdoors all the time. They see the interplay 
of light and shadow, moonlight and sunlight and starlight, and so forth. And 
these people have had millennia of understanding what those stars, and 
the celestial bodies, are doing. . . . If the individuals who were in charge of 
planning this took into consideration this power of 
light and shadow—­well, that’s what architects do, 
isn’t it? Take a look at the spot, think about the goal 
that you have in building that structure, and play it 
off against the land and what you’re building. That’s 
what architects do. That’s what these people were. 
(Link to video is on page 205.)
clearly in Google terrain view, providing a good orientation to the 
portions that are visible from the highway. The northwestern wall 
turns right in the distance near the railroad line. (The earthwork is 
on private land and not otherwise accessible.)

Lower Scioto Route
Lower Scioto Route
Huge, eroded sandstone blocks stand strangely on ridgetops in the Ohio Hanging Rock 
Preserve northeast of Portsmouth.
Lower Shawneetown. By the mid-­ 
1700s, a large American Indian 
town stood overlooking the Scioto-­
Ohio confluence. It was a place of 
meeting and interchange for the 
tribal groups arriving from the east—­the Shawnee, Delaware, and 
others—­who were being driven out by advancing colonial settlement. 
The population reached at least 1,200, which at the time made it one of 
the largest towns in the West. The convergence of Indigenous routes 
and two great rivers made it a major center for early trade with the 
Europeans, especially in furs. The size and diversity of its population 
also made it the setting for diplomatic maneuverings by the French 
and British as they brokered tribal alliances in support of their own 
conflicts. Initially the town was on the south bank just below the Old 
Fort Earthworks, but satellite towns also formed across the river, and 
the sector where Portsmouth now stands soon became the largest.
Raven Rock. Directly across the Ohio River and just a bit to the left, 
is a steep 500-­foot bluff. A sandstone promontory is flanked by two 
long ridges; Google terrain view vividly reveals the formation’s re-
semblance to a bird in flight. With sweeping views of the Scioto 
confluence and several miles of the Ohio River in each direction 
(and today the city of Portsmouth), this high lookout was used by 
Indigenous people to monitor traffic on the rivers, including set-
tlers arriving on their flatboats. The rugged, forested land is now 
a state nature preserve visitable by permit from the Ohio Depart-
ment of Natural Resources.
TO THE BRUSH CREEK ROUTE
State Route 125 between Portsmouth and West Union (the Brush Creek Route) passes 
through the 63,000-­acre Shawnee State Forest; its rugged lands comprise one of the 
region’s largest dark-­Sky zones. At its center, Shawnee State Park has an extensive 
trail system, several lodging options, and layered-­ridge vistas earning it the nickname 
“Little Smokies.”
TWO ROUTES TO JACKSON
East of Portsmouth and north out of Sciotoville, State Routes 335 and 776 follow 
another of the huge ancient valleys of the preglacial Teays River system. Like the 
Scioto Valley to the west, its form is prominent on terrain or satellite maps. A more 
circuitous route to Jackson heads northeast from Sciotodale on State Route 140, 
winding through narrow, wooded hollows, and giving access to the spectacular, 
challenging trails of the Ohio Hanging Rock Preserve.
Ohio Hanging Rock Preserve. One of the Arc of Appalachia’s most 
remote and astonishing preserves (2067-­1143 Frederick Rd., Wheel-
ersburg) offers a challenging 4.4-­mile loop trail with several stren-
uous climbs and descents leading to some of Ohio’s most unusual 
geology. Huge blocks of eroded rock stand implausibly on the high 
hillsides. These ridgetops were once islands in the huge lake cre-
ated across the region when, for at least 6,000 years, the glaciers 
held back the waters of the Teays River as it flowed north out of the 
high Appalachians. Over time, the lake’s redirected waters carved 
the Ohio River’s current route. (Consult the Arc’s website for trail 
information.)
Jackson. As early as 1795, a small settlement here was called “Salt 
Lick Town” for the rare salt deposits in the area. This essential 
mineral had been drawing animals and people here for millennia, 
Portsmouth’s riverfront mural 
series includes a depiction of Lower 
Shawneetown, a large American Indian 
trading center in the mid-­1700s.

173
Lower Scioto Route
Lower Scioto Route
The restored iron-­smelting complex at 
Buckeye Furnace illustrates mid-­1800s 
industrial techniques.
The Buckeye Furnace charging shed, 
with its giant scoop, was used to feed the 
smelting fires from above.
and the earliest area roads were repurposed ancient buffalo trails. 
The settlers stripped the forests to fuel salt production by boiling 
saltwater from the licks. This industrial-­scale approach depleted 
not only the salt but also the forests as early as 1810. But by 1830, 
Welsh immigrants to the area had discovered coal deposits nearby. 
From their experience back home, they knew what to do with it, 
and southeast Ohio’s important nineteenth-­century iron industry 
was born.
Buckeye Furnace. The story of Ohio’s iron industry is best told today 
at the beautifully reconstructed Buckeye Furnace State Memorial 
(123 Buckeye Park Rd., Wellston), reached via State Route 124 east 
out of Jackson. The approach road passes watery lowlands before 
the massive stone blast furnace complex appears on the left. The 
mouth of the charcoal-­fired oven is visible behind an open-­air 
casting shed; an engine house to the left used steam heat from the 
furnace to drive air compressors, increasing the heat of the fire.  
A timber superstructure above the chimney (reachable by a sepa-
rate trail) was the charging shed where the iron ore and limestone 
were measured in from above. A large open-­air shed, also on the 
hilltop, stored the huge quantities of materials needed to keep the 
furnace running continuously. The former company store now  
offers visitor orientation, while the ironmaster’s house and a covered 
bridge stand nearby on the grounds.
Ohio’s Iron Industry. Buckeye Furnace is typical of many such fa-
cilities that dotted Ohio’s Hanging Rock Iron Region during its 
prosperous decades in the mid-­1800s. Many small company towns 
of 300–­400 residents operated the furnaces, producing iron for 
America’s early industry and for the Union forces in the Civil War. 
All the raw ingredients were locally plentiful: wood for fires, lime-
stone for flux, and the iron ore itself. Vast tracts of forest were de-
pleted in the process, and families were conscripted in servitude. 
These isolated, self-­contained communities had their own schools, 
churches, and commerce; even currencies were wholly controlled 
by the company store.
Leo Petroglyphs. Between Jackson and Chillicothe, leave the main 
highway (US Route 35) to visit the Leo Petroglyphs State Memorial 
(about a half mile northwest from the village of Leo, at 400 Park Rd., 
Ray). Centuries ago, Indigenous people carved thirty-­seven images onto 
the top of an exposed dome of sandstone, including outlines of birds, 
fish, feet, and hands, as well as human and composite creatures. From 
the weathering of the rock and the nature of the symbols, experts think 
the petroglyphs are less than a thousand years old. Indecipherable 
today, the Leo Petroglyphs suggest what may well have been a visual 
language with specific meanings. In 1780, the Moravian missionary 
David Zeisberger observed that the Indigenous people along the 
Muskingum River left marks in charcoal or red pigment on forest 
trees, using symbols that others 
could read, about hunting or 
fighting successes, or just having 
passed by.
TO THE HOCKING ROUTE
From Buckeye Furnace, return via Berlin Crossroads to take either State Route 93 
through Wellston and McArthur en route to Zaleski, or State Route 32 (called the 
James A. Rhodes Appalachian Highway) to Athens, Nelsonville, and the Hocking Hills 
region (see map, page 174).
The Leo Petroglyphs are thought to date 
from the Fort Ancient culture that lived in 
this region between 900 and 1650 CE.

Rock cliffs, hemlock groves, rushing streams, and waterfalls help make the Hocking Hills 
region a hiker’s paradise.
Logan. This thriving town serves as the commercial anchor for 
the Hocking Hills region—­with many restaurants and bars lining 
its Main Street plus a brewery and a winery on the edge of town. 
The Hocking Hills Regional Welcome Center (13178 SR 664 Sce-
nic) offers information, directions, and lodging availability. Local 
history is presented at the Hocking County Historical and Gene-
alogical Society’s museum (64 North Culver St.), though the most 
unique attraction in town may be the tiny but nationally famous 
Paul A. Johnson Pencil Sharpener Museum (on the welcome center 
grounds), with over 3,000 units collected by a local minister over 
several decades. The Hocking River offers seventy miles of rela-
tively unspoiled scenery and boating between Logan and its con-
fluence with the Ohio; inquire locally about conditions and water 
levels.
Hocking Hills State Park. The several discontinuous units of this 
park provide amenities and access to the best of the region’s sce-
nic destinations. All are open from dawn to dusk, with trailhead 
parking, signage, and facilities. Many trails are one-­way, some are

Hocking Route
Southeast from Lancaster, the Hocking River enters a region of espe-
cially beautiful scenery, nature preserves, and geological wonders, 
several of which are units of Hocking Hills State Park. The area is 
rich in tourism amenities, with many inns and cabins, and the towns 
of Logan and Nelsonville are commercial hubs. The route features 
the circuitous, scenic State Route 374, interesting ancient mounds 
in Zaleski and The Plains, and finally Athens, the home of Ohio Uni-

Ohio Department of Natural Resources website offers guides to the 
various units as well as links to other parks, preserves, and state

Cantwell Cliffs. A trailhead park-

gives access to this less-­visited 
northernmost unit of the state

along the clifftop with long vistas,

UNIQUE GEOLOGY
This well-­developed tourism region, between the Hocking and Scioto 
rivers, has dramatic topography. It deserves its popularity among scenery 
lovers, adventurers, day trippers, families, and history buffs. The landscape 
here results from its 200-­foot-­thick layer of Black Hand Sandstone, laid down 
by the shallow sea that once covered Ohio and then was uplifted by pressure 
from the formation of the Appalachian Mountains. Over the 300 million years 
since, the many small streams draining this uplifted mass of soft, layered rock 
have carved a maze of cliff-­faced canyons, waterfalls, caves, and rock shelters—­
still full of remnant cold-­climate plant species like birch and hemlock left from 
when the glaciers loomed just twenty miles to the west.
The sandstone cliffs and narrow passages 
at Cantwell Cliffs have been sculpted by 
eons of erosion.
The eroded sandstone galleries of Rock House create a stunning natural effect analogous 
to a Gothic portico, framing vistas out over the stream and the ancient forest.
among sheer cliffs and rock shelters. The one-­mile one-­way loop 
(about an hour and a half, with many steps) descends quickly 
from a shelter house to its narrowest portion, called “Fat Woman’s 
Squeeze.”
Rock House. A mile-­long loop trail (again with many steps) leads 
to the most unique cliffside formations in the region—­in fact, the 
only true cave (16350 SR 374, Laurelville). Deeply sculpted, colorful 
canyon walls lead to hanging gardens of native plants and to the 
amazing “house” itself—­eroded chambers within the middle layers 
of the sandstone cliff face that have been formed into a covered 
corridor. The effect is of a row of natural arches, evoking the side 
aisle of a Gothic cathedral, and framing views out to the forest and 
the stream below. American Indians used small recesses in the 
back wall as ovens and cut water-­holding pits in the floor, which 
still fill with spring water in rainy seasons.

Hocking Route
Hocking Route
Hocking Hills State Park’s largest cascade 
is Cedar Falls, about a three-­mile hike 
southeast from Old Man’s Cave.
Conkle’s Hollow. The trailhead at Conkle’s Hollow State Nature Pre-
serve (24858 Big Pine Rd., Rockbridge) leads to another rugged, 
rocky gorge—­one of the deepest in the region. Ferns and wildflow-
ers grow in profusion in the dark reaches beneath thick, towering 
stands of birch and hemlock. The lower trail (three-­quarters of a 
mile, wheelchair accessible) penetrates the chasm between verti-
cal cliffs towering more than 200 feet high and narrowing to less 
than 300 feet wide by the end of the gorge’s half-­mile length. A 
strenuous one-­way rim trail is two miles long, and especially beau-
tiful during fall leaf season.
Old Man’s Cave. Adjacent to its lodge and visitor center, this is the 
state park’s most popular unit (19988 SR 664 Scenic, Logan). Several 
trails and bridges connect to a giant rock shelter partway up the 
face of a cliff, to upper and lower waterfalls, and to both rims of the 
half-­mile-­long canyon. The popular Grandma Gatewood Trail goes 
from here to Cedar Falls and Ash Cave. The “old man” of the cave’s 
name was a hermit, Richard Rowe, who arrived in the area in 1796 
and is buried nearby. Also adjacent to the park’s visitor center, the 
John Glenn Astronomy Park offers weather-­dependent programs in 
night Sky viewing and astronomical interpretation. A circular plaza 
tracks the sun’s marking of the seasons. This rural location is free 
of light pollution, so visitors are invited to bring telescopes, or just 
their eyes, to appreciate the views of the night Sky. The park is a 
project of the Friends of Hocking Hills State Park, who maintain its 
independent website.
Cedar Falls. A half-­mile trail leads from a parking area (21560 SR 
374, Logan) to the largest-­volume waterfall in the state park, pass-
ing through another rock-­walled 
canyon with grottoes and stands 
of hemlock. A highlight of Cedar 
Falls is the artist and mathema-
tician Akio Hizume’s Democracy 
Steps—­a 100-­step section of the 
descending trail carefully de-
signed to vary and choreograph 
the rhythms of movement and the patterns of foot placement, mak-
ing the visitor experience more interesting as well as relaxing. En 
route to Ash Cave from here, the iron-­framed Ash Cave Fire Tower 
(erected in 1934) is open for intrepid climbers. From a small park-
ing lot at State Route 374 and Chapel Ridge Road, a 550-­foot section 
of the Grandma Gatewood Trail leads south to the base of the tower. 
Open stairs climb 80 feet for wide views from well above the tree-
tops, but the room at the top is kept locked.
Ash Cave. A relatively short and easy trail leads from a parking 
area to the most breathtaking feature in Hocking Hills State Park 
(27292 SR 56, South Bloomingville). Its quarter-­mile length follows 
a narrow wooded gorge until the 
700-­foot-­wide curved overhang 
suddenly appears. A stream of 
water falls from its rim 90 feet 
above into a small pool, echo-
ing within the huge cave’s eerie 
acoustics. The name derives 
from the nearly 100 cubic yards 
of ash that had been left in the 
cave by untold centuries of 
American Indian fires, along 
with the remains of food prepa-
ration. Ash Cave was a stopping 
point on a principal Indige-
nous trail (now State Route 56) 
connecting the Kanawha Valley 
(in West Virginia) with the Shaw-
nee towns of the Scioto Valley 
and points west.
ZALESKI STATE FOREST
Ohio’s second-­largest state forest wilderness covers nearly 28,000 
acres of winding ridgetops and remote hollows, with long single-­ 
or multiday backpacking trails from Lake Hope State Park or the 
village of Zaleski.
The splendor and shelter of Ash Cave 
has made it a favored destination 
since time immemorial.

Hocking Route
Hocking Route
The largest survivor of the Zaleski Mound Group stands in a beautiful grove of trees near 
the headquarters of the Zaleski State Forest.
The Moonville Tunnel’s many tragedies during its use by a now-­long-­abandoned 
railroad have inspired persistent local legends about paranormal activities.
Zaleski Mound Group. Remote state and county roads link the Hock-
ing region with the small village of Zaleski and the state forest of the 
same name. From a group of six mounds, three remain today, two 
of which are visible to the public. The largest, called Ranger Station 
Mound, is one of the most beautiful in Ohio, and stands adjacent to 
the state forest facility (114 Broadway St., Zaleski). The 15-­foot-­tall 
mound has a graceful profile—­a subtle variation on spherical and 
conical form—­and is ringed by a gravel path and a partial circle of 
young trees. A small veterans memorial is nearby. About 1,000 feet 
south along Broadway Street stands the Methodist Church Mound, 
about half the size, behind a small church building. The mounds 
at Zaleski were built by people of the Adena culture who thrived in 
this area of abundant game, fish, and water transport—­as well as a 
prized local Zaleski Flint.
Lake Hope State Park. Four miles north of Zaleski, this state park 
surrounds a 120-­acre lake and offers boating, mountain biking, 
beaver habitat, many cabins, ruins of the Hope Furnace, and pit-­
barbecue locavore dining at the Lake Hope Lodge (27331 SR 278, 
McArthur, inquire for hours). Just across Shea Road is the rebuilt 
Hope Schoolhouse (27800 Wheelabout Rd., McArthur), now an 
interpretive center telling the stories of the area’s history and its 
Hope iron industry settlement. The Selinde Roosenburg Memorial 
Backpack Trail offers long loops (a ten-­mile day hike or multiday 
routes) throughout the Zaleski State Forest, with trailheads and in-
terpretive materials at the park and schoolhouse.
Moonville Tunnel. Adjacent to the stream just north of the Hope 
Schoolhouse, a repurposed 1800s railroad bed—­the Moonville Rail 
Trail—­extends for miles in both directions. A two-­mile walk east (or 
a three-­mile drive on winding, densely forested Shea Road) reaches 
the celebrated Moonville Tunnel. Its brick-­faced, brick-­lined, 300-­
foot cavern is one of many abandoned rail tunnels in this remote, 
rugged region, but now unique both for its accessibility and for the 
appeal of the local legends associated with its tragic history (read-
ily found online). From the ample trailhead parking, an iron bridge 
(laden with padlocks like Florence’s Ponte Vecchio) leads to the tunnel 
entrance. Nearby in the forest are scant remains of the Moonville 
settlement, whose population never exceeded 100, and its cemetery. 
Another three miles east along the Moonville Rail Trail is the King’s 
Hollow Tunnel, framed out in massive one-­foot-­square oak timbers 
instead of brick or stone, though not easily reachable by car.
ATHENS
Ohio University at Athens was the first to be established west of the 
Alleghenies, in a section of the Hocking Valley filled with promi-
nent Adena-­era mounds and earthworks.
History and the University. The New Englanders who came to set-
tle southeast Ohio in 1788 believed in education. Their Ohio Com-
pany of Associates, when founding Marietta, set aside two whole 
townships of their remaining lands for the “American Western Uni-
versity” to be modeled on the ones back home—­Yale and Harvard.
