Hocking Route
Hocking Route
THE PLAINS
This village immediately northwest of Athens occupies an unusual 
flat terrace above the Hocking River, among abruptly rising hills; it 
was once one of the largest concentrations of Adena-­era mounds 
and earthworks.
History and Setting. Two prominent mounds remain in this valley, 
standing among suburban streets and houses, the surviving rem-
nants of the Wolf Plains Mounds and Earthworks. After about 300 
BCE, people gathered here and covered this level ground with at 
least thirty earthen mounds and circles. There is no evidence of an-
cient dwelling sites on the terrace, suggesting that they lived in the 
surrounding hills (where there are more mounds) and descended 
to the plain for ceremonies. Today, 
only two large mounds can be read-
ily seen; other low mounds and cir-
cles are barely detectable in fields or 
beneath buildings.
Hartman Mound. The largest of the 
two mounds remaining in The 
Plains is the Hartman Mound, 
standing 40 feet high and 140 feet 
in diameter (26 Mound St.). Its 
unusually steep profile has never 
been excavated but probably con-
tains graves similar to the one found 
during the 1800s excavation of a 
smaller mound nearby, where the 
deceased lay in a log tomb, sur-
rounded by 500 rolled copper beads 
and a tubular pipe. Although there 
is an implied pathway to the top, 
climbing is not encouraged, out of 
respect for both the ancient graves 
and the private landowner.
Reflecting their idealism, the founders named their university town 
“Athens” after the Greek birthplace of Western civilization. It was the 
first college west of the Allegheny Mountains, and in 1804, the year 
after statehood, the new Ohio legislature renamed it “Ohio Univer-
sity.” The oldest campus building, dating from 1816, is Cutler Hall, 
named after one of Marietta’s (and the university’s) founders. Athens 
has long nourished a local food culture; products from innovative 
growers, chefs, and craftspeople are showcased in its uptown area 
and in nearby Nelsonville. Local history is told at the Southeast Ohio 
History Center (24 West State St.). Athens County also originated the 
“quilt barn” phenomenon, whereby owners paint large distinctive 
quilt squares on their barns (inquire locally for quilt barn tour routes).
The Ridges. On a hill across the Hocking River from the university, 
the Kennedy Museum of Art (100 Ridges Circle, Athens) houses im-
pressive collections including significant Southwest Native American 
art, weaving, and jewelry, given by alumni and benefactors Edwin 
and Ruth Kennedy. The museum and other university functions are 
housed at The Ridges—­the sprawling and ominous Victorian rem-
nants of a huge nineteenth-­century mental health asylum. On the 
same hill is the Dairy Barn Arts Center (8000 Dairy Ln.), another of 
Athens’s outstanding arts venues, and home to the most significant 
art quilt show in the nation: Quilt National, held every two years. 
From the Dairy Barn, a walking path leads to Athens’s (and the 
old asylum’s) historic cemeteries, and to the top of Radar Hill with  
broad views.
Several of Ohio University’s earliest 
buildings still enclose the campus 
green, including a central one named 
after co-­founder Manasseh Cutler.
This lively university town centers on 
Court Street, with its shops and bars, as well 
as a tradition of innovative locavore 
markets and restaurants.
The large Hartman Mound in The 
Plains has an unusually steep and 
spherical form rather than conical.
The mounds and rings at The Plains 
exemplify the earth-­building and  
settlement traditions that archaeologists 
call Adena.

ADENA AND HOPEWELL
Archaeologist Brad Lepper considers the modern names “Adena” and 
“Hopewell”—­what they do and don’t refer to, and how much of a distinction 
they represent: My feeling on it is that the Adena are simply early Hopewell, 
and Hopewell could be thought of as late Adena, and that there is a seamless 
cultural tradition, that there is no bright line separating one from the other. 
All of the interaction-­sphere elements have their beginnings in the Adena; 
they simply get elaborated. The extent of that interaction sphere broadens 
and the quantities of material increase. Earthworks 
are similarly configured; they just get bigger and 
grander in scale. It’s not to say that there weren’t 
cultural differences. Clearly, the Adena had practices 
that were altered, abandoned, or supplemented by 
later Hopewell developments, but the Hopewell do 
represent at least a quantitative explosion. (Link to 
video is on page 205.)
A collection of log structures at Robbins 
Crossing Historical Village sets the scene for 
interpretive programming about early-­
1800s life in southeast Ohio.
Woodruff-­Connett Mound. A few blocks away (424 Adena Dr.) stands 
the town’s other well-­preserved mound. At 15 feet high with a base 
diameter of 90 feet, the Woodruff-­Connett Mound is the largest of 
what was once a cluster of three. Along part of its base, a slightly 
elevated “apron” of Earth may belong to the ancient design; a slight 
swell in the grass nearby marks where a second mound once stood, 
about half the size. The mound sits on land preserved as open space 
by the Athens County Historical Society. Like the Hartman Mound, 
the Woodruff-­Connett has not been excavated, but is probably also 
a burial mound.
Research at The Plains. Investigations have revealed that the people 
who built these circles and mounds lived among the surrounding 
hills, descending to the plain below to work with their neighbors 
to build the earthworks and to join gatherings both ceremonial 
and social. In the 1980s, archaeologist Elliot Abrams of Ohio Uni-
versity studied a nearby mound (as it was being destroyed for a 
housing project) and found a central burial of a man in his fifties, 
surrounded by fourteen cremations. About forty shallow fire pits 
at various levels in the mound suggested people revisited it at in-
tervals, burning things and adding soil, for as long as four to six 
generations. This suggested to Abrams that the hill dwellers were 
bringing their already-­cremated kin to join that central figure—­
perhaps an honored Ancestor or leader. Small objects in the depos-
ited soil showed these people had connections with the Hopewell 
tradition, although in this valley more of the earlier Adena prac-
tices continued to dominate.
WAYNE NATIONAL FOREST
Along the highway between The Plains and Nelsonville, the visitor center for this 
huge well-­tended wilderness offers detailed historical and ecological information, 
as well as excellent maps of its many miles of every kind of trail in all three of the 
national forest’s large sections—­north and east of Athens, another east of Marietta, 
and another southeast of Portsmouth.
Hocking College. Students in two of this small college’s renowned 
programs welcome visitors to a pair of unique amenities. Robbins 
Crossing Historical Village (3301 Hocking Pkwy., Nelsonville), oper-
ated by the college’s Parks and Museum Education program, pres-
ents several early-­1800s shops and houses relocated from the area, 
plus programs and events on arts, crafts, and culture, such as log 
cabin construction, gardening, animal husbandry, and period med-
icine. (Check its website for events and opening times.) Students in 
the school’s Hospitality and Events Management program staff the 
nearby Lodge at Hocking College (15770 SR 691, Nelsonville). Its im-
pressive modern building is well appointed with furniture made by 
students and alumni using wood sourced on the campus property.
Nelsonville. The heart of this lively tourist town is the historic Public 
Square (at the corner of Hocking and Columbus Sts.), surrounded by 
galleries, cafés, restaurants, and the restored Stuart’s Opera House. 
The town was named for Daniel 
Nelson, who bought 200 acres 
here in 1814. After coal was found 
locally, the Hocking Valley coal 
industry thrived, with Nelsonville 
as the hub of the “Little Cities of 
Black Diamonds” (mining and

Hocking Route
industry towns to the north and east). When coal mining shifted to 
larger-­scale operations in West Virginia and Kentucky, Nelsonville 
turned to brickmaking. Bricks made with local clay are visible in 
streets and sidewalks around town, imprinted with their distinc-
tive marks (names, stars, and other shapes). A few of the large kilns 
and chimneys stand, in various states of ruin, in Nelsonville Brick 
Park at the west edge of town (580 Lake Hope Dr.). Nelsonville hosts 
many festivals and arts events, as well as the main station for the 
Hocking Valley Scenic Railway.
Haydenville. Upstream along the left bank of the Hocking River, 
Johnny Appleseed Roadside Park (18115 Haydenville Rd., Nel-
sonville) preserves the stone walls of Hocking Canal Lock 19.  
Approaching Haydenville, a curious little round house appears on 
the right (17092 Haydenville Rd.), built of red clay tiles. The village 
began as an ironworks company town like Buckeye Furnace, but 
by the 1870s, it had transitioned to clay products like the rest of 
this area. Haydenville’s bricks and tiles are showcased as cladding 
and unique ornamentation on a variety of other local house types, 
and especially on the town’s Methodist Church (16798 Haydenville 
Rd.). North of the village, near the US Route 33 interchange, Hock-
ing Canal Lock 17 and Aqueduct Park showcases more of the canal 
builders’ beautiful masonry, including an arched tunnel that car-
ried the waterway and its towpath across a small stream. Take US 
Route 33 to return to Logan or Lancaster.
TO THE MUSKINGUM ROUTE
A short scenic drive east from Athens reaches historic Marietta and the Muskingum 
River confluence: Take US Route 50 and State Route 7 to enjoy a portion of the Ohio 
River Scenic Byway. Or use State Route 550 through Amesville and Bartlett; about four 
miles north of Amesville, a mound stands near a weathered old church in the remote 
Mound Hill Cemetery (16971 SR 329, Amesville, photo on page xiv).
Public Square is the heart of Nelsonville and a focal point for the arts, with an opera house, 
shops, restaurants, and galleries.

Muskingum Route
Newark lies among the upper tributaries of the Muskingum River. 
Nearby in Licking County are Blackhand Gorge, with remains of 
a canal and early rail transport, and Flint Ridge, the source of An-
cient Ohio’s most valuable resource. Near the early Zane’s Trace 
settlement of Somerset is the stone hilltop of Glenford Fort. En 
route to historic Marietta, with its impressive earthworks, the 
winding, scenic course of the Muskingum has been canalized:  
A series of old hand-­crank locks still operate in the quaint villages 
along its banks.
An ashlar stone canal towpath was built against the cliff of Blackhand Gorge, even as 
the namesake Black Hand was being destroyed.
BLACKHAND GORGE
The Licking River has carved an unusual narrow chasm through a 
high sandstone ridge east of Newark, named after an American In-
dian petroglyph—­a Black Hand—­left on one of its cliff faces long ago.
Orientation. The Blackhand Gorge State Nature Preserve is ten miles 
east of Newark, via State Route 16 (2200 Gratiot Rd. SE, Heath). Its 
namesake Black Hand petroglyph, though now lost, has inspired 
many Indigenous legends: One proclaimed this was the boundary 
of long-­sacred lands, where no man could raise his “hand” against 
another. The river here would have been a route for the valuable 
Flint Ridge flint, on its way down from the nearby ancient quarries 
to the earthworks at Newark and beyond. The Black Hand has given 
its name to the type of sandstone bedrock stretching south from 
here through the Hocking Hills.
Transportation Relics. In 1828, this section of the river became part 
of the Ohio and Erie Canal. The Black Hand was destroyed when the 
stone towpath was constructed along the riverbank. Later, the Ohio

The hiking trails at Flint Ridge 
display beautiful outcrops of the 
rainbow-­colored rock, prized here for 
thousands of years.
The former Perry County Courthouse anchors 
Somerset’s Pennsylvania-­style town square.
Central Railroad cut a deep crevice through the ridge here—­now the 
park’s Blackhand Trail, stained by soot from steam locomotives. An 
electric interurban trolley line also came through a tunnel here on its 
way from Zanesville to Newark, Columbus, and Indianapolis. A high 
trail skirts the large quarry where Black Hand Sandstone was taken 
for many of central Ohio’s monuments, bridges, and canal locks.  
A separate trail (across the river from the parking lot) passes a nearly 
intact canal lock hidden in the woods on its way to the abandoned in-
terurban tunnel, whose 327-­foot length is simply a hole cut through 
solid rock, with no other framing materials or reinforcements.
EN ROUTE TO SOMERSET
From Blackhand Gorge, follow small winding roads for about seven miles through 
scenic countryside to the quarries at Flint Ridge, then about another ten miles to 
the stone hilltop of Glenford Fort and the well-­preserved early town of Somerset just 
beyond.
Flint Ridge. Less than a day’s walk from Newark, this was one of 
ancient America’s greatest treasure sources. At the Flint Ridge An-
cient Quarries (15300 Flint Ridge Rd., Heath), a deposit of beautiful 
rainbow-­colored flint lies close to the surface, where Native people 
have been mining it for thousands of years. The forested ridgetop 
is covered with the water-­filled pits where generations of Indige-
nous people obtained the hard, sharp stone for shaping into weap-
ons and tools, as many as 10,000 years before Newark’s earthworks 
were built—­perhaps significantly—­on the nearest flat river terrace. 
Over millennia, visitors and trade spread Flint Ridge flint all across 
North America. A museum interprets the site, exhibits samples, 
and organizes public events where modern expert “flintknappers” 
demonstrate the ancient techniques of chipping the hard glassy stone 
into beautiful shapes. (Licking County is being transformed now by 
huge new investments from the 
microprocessor industry, but 
local leaders quip that this area 
“has been the center of chip pro-
duction for 10,000 years!”)
Glenford Fort. About ten miles south of Flint Ridge, near the small 
village of Glenford (11995 SR 757, Glenford), lies one of Ohio’s most 
unusual and best-­preserved hilltop enclosures. Although still on 
private land, permits for visitation may be requested online from 
the Perry County Soil and Water Conservation District. A steep 
quarter-­mile climb through beautiful old-­growth forest and re-
stored pine groves is rewarded 
with views of an intact, mile-­
long, all-­stone enclosure wall 
surrounding the hilltop. A stone 
ceremonial mound stands in the 
center. The walls are built on top 
of natural outcrops whose open-
ings create dramatic passages.
Somerset. Of all the towns developed along Zane’s Trace, tiny Som-
erset retains the most authentic form of an early nineteenth-­century 
agricultural market town. While Zanesville and Lancaster were on 
rivers, which meant waterpower and transportation, Somerset’s 
high ridgetop location limited its economic growth. The Old Perry 
County Courthouse (built in 1829) is the best surviving example of 
the once-­common pyramid-­on-­cube design. It anchors one recessed 
corner of the town’s Pennsylvania-­style main square—­laid out on the 
same principle as the one in Lancaster (and in both Somerset and 
Lancaster, Pennsylvania). A second square, three blocks west and 
now called Pigsfoot, was for markets. Two miles southeast, on Old 
Somerset Road, is the Catholic Priory of Saint Joseph, (5796 SR 383, 
Somerset). An 1840s brick structure stands today where Dominican 
friar Edward Fenwick (who was from Kentucky) consecrated the first 
Roman Catholic church in Ohio; 
his original 1818 log structure 
attracted settlers coming along 
Zane’s Trace. (Several round barns, 
dating from the early 1900s, are 
within a few miles of Somerset; in-
quire locally.)
The stone enclosure walls of the 
Hopewell-­era Glenford Fort stand atop 
natural outcrops in old-­growth forests.

McConnelsville. Far from the major highways, the villages of the 
Muskingum Valley preserve their early ambience with small local

south, contains many well-­preserved wood-­

Big Bottom Massacre. Along a peaceful stretch of riverbank (and 
State Route 266) southeast of Stockport, a park and historical 
marker commemorate the place where a small band of early set-
tlers were killed by the Indigenous inhabitants in January of 1791. 
The group had moved upriver from the new colony at Marietta and

MUSKINGUM ROUTE
THE PENNSYLVANIA DUTCH
Many German-­speaking people passed through this region of Ohio, from 
Pennsylvania or directly from Europe, following Ebenezer Zane’s new 
highway. (The term “Dutch” referred to speakers of Germanic languages—­
Deutsch—­before it came to be applied to people from the Netherlands.) 
Especially Rheinlanders, after the Napoleonic Wars, brought their language, 
customs, and various religions (Mennonite, Catholic, Lutheran, Jewish) with 
them. Many were commercial agriculturalists open to innovative large-­scale 
operations to grow abundant crops to sell for profit. Around the turn of 
1800, the fact that Ohio was “free country” also gave opportunities to poor 
American whites to become farm workers, which was not possible in 
the pre–­Civil War South due to slavery. The land was cheap, the soil was 
superb, and both Germans and Americans came pouring in along Zane’s 
Trace. Somerset and the route’s other early towns thrived.
The Muskingum River was canalized in 1841 
by a series of hand-­operated locks, still 
functioning in each of its riverside towns; 
this one is at Devola, just above Marietta.
The Twin City Opera House, where the archaeologist Moore-
head lectured about his findings in the area, still dominates 
McConnelsville’s diagonally oriented town square.
New Lexington. The more direct route from Somerset to the 
Muskingum River passes through the town of New Lexington, whose 
ridgetop Main Street has a good collection of historic buildings and 
local commerce. Two-­tone Romanesque details adorn the late-­1800s 
Perry County Courthouse and its tall turreted tower. New Lexington 
is also the home of a huge building products factory still making the 
world-­famous Ludowici roof tiles and continuing the deep history 
of clay products manufacturing in this part of Ohio. An alternate 
route to the river would be via Zanesville, named for its founder 
Ebenezer Zane in 1800. Travelers on his road needed to ferry across 
the Muskingum, here at its confluence with the Licking River. Today, 
the two rivers are crossed by a unique Y-­shaped bridge. Zanesville 
became an important manufacturing center in the early 1800s and 
since then has been especially well-­known for its art pottery.
The Muskingum River. As part of Ohio’s canal-­building program, the 
Muskingum River’s entire 112-­mile length was “canalized” by the addi-
tion of a series of eleven dams with their accompanying channels and 
locks. Opened in 1841, the system created a navigable route between 
the Ohio River (at Marietta) and the Ohio and Erie Canal (at Dresden, 
east of Newark), and from there on to Lake Erie. The hand-­crank 
lock-­control mechanisms are the only such system still functioning 
in the country. Boaters can navigate the river today using this same

MOOREHEAD’S MOUND REPORT
In 1896, archaeologist Warren King Moorehead and a few aides traveled 
this valley in search of Indigenous sites. His notes describe many things 
now lost, including pictographs on a large boulder depicting bird tracks 
and other figures, plus a great face carved in a rock. He noted that the 
large Sprague Mound stood “directly in the heart of McConnelsville,” 
and that the valley had at least sixty more mounds—­some on the river 
terraces, some with rings, and some high above on the bluffs. The 
locals told him, probably in error, that most of the mounds were places of 
observation rather than burial. His descriptions suggest that, like the 
grouping at The Plains near Athens, they were mainly of the Adena 
tradition. He gave a lecture about his findings in the McConnelsville 
opera house.
WARREN KING MOOREHEAD
Although the young Moorehead began by digging mounds to find artifacts, he 
did come to respect Native Americans and their culture. Historian Jeff Gill tells 
the story: He gets a chance to go west, and ends up literally on the doorstep of 
the Wounded Knee Massacre. What makes Warren’s story so fascinating is that 
he wasn’t there for the massacre. The night before, the commanding general 
personally orders him off the site because, for the previous month, Moorehead 
was the only person who was actually going into the Sioux encampments, 
spoke rudimentary Sioux, and was welcomed into their homes. And so they 
hustle him off the site, and the next day the massacre takes place. After that 
experience, he goes to Washington and tries to literally scream bloody murder 
asking for justice for the Sioux. And his attitude toward 
Native Americans and how to properly handle Native 
American remains—­at least certainly the skulls and 
bones, if not the artifacts—­radically changes. And so 
he goes from being a looter to being an admittedly 
unschooled but very passionate defender of the 
richness and complexity of Native American culture. 
(Link to video is on page 205.)
One of the many tragedies during 
the early years of settlement 
took place here at Big Bottom, on the 
banks of the Muskingum.
The expansive Marietta Earthworks spread across a high terrace above the Ohio and 
Muskingum Rivers.
the known risk that the land’s 
current residents would not wel-
come their presence, they left 
themselves vulnerable. A small 
Lenape and Wyandot war party, 
hoping to prevent further en-
croachment on their lands, killed about a dozen of the settlers. The 
incident illustrates the tragic circumstances of the early settlement 
and displacement process; repeated conflicts and then all-­out war 
continued, and ultimately the tribes were largely expelled from 
much of Ohio following the Battle of Fallen Timbers in 1794.
MARIETTA
Here stood the easternmost of Ohio’s monumental ancient earth-
work complexes. Its unusual plan included a ringed burial mound, 
two large rectangular enclosures, a set of platform mounds, and a 
wide graded way down to the river.
History and Setting. Marietta is a well-­preserved river town, with 
a deeply layered history laid across a high terrace overlooking the 
confluence of the Muskingum and Ohio Rivers. Veterans of the 
American Revolution laid out the town in 1788—­the first settlement 


--- PDF PAGE 106 ---

196
197
in honor of the French queen, 
Marie Antoinette. They preserved 
many of the impressive Adena and Hopewell earthworks, already at 
least fourteen centuries old, as they built among them. A thriving 
downtown, fine museums, and beautiful residential and historic 
districts make this a rewarding destination. (Check the Marietta-­
Washington County Convention and Visitors Bureau website for 
information, or stop by in person at 241 Front Street.)
The Ohio Company. After the Revolutionary War, the new American 
Congress was in financial straits, and unable to pay all the soldiers. 
A few enterprising New Englanders suggested that land in the West, 
won from the British in the war, would be a good source of reve-
nue for the new government. They persuaded Congress to let them 
form “The Ohio Company of Associates” to broker the deal. Led by 
Rufus Putnam and Benjamin Tupper, two brigadier generals from 
Massachusetts, and the Reverend 
Manasseh Cutler, the company 
bought a huge tract of land along 
the Ohio River at a bargain price, 
then advertised the lots. Enthu-
siasm for the project ran high 
among veteran officers; even 
George Washington, though un-
able to go, wrote, “No colony in 
America was ever settled under 
such favorable auspices.”
Earthworks Preservation. Putnam and his colleagues planned 
their settlement carefully, establishing the earliest preservation 
law in the West to ensure that the main earthworks would be kept 
for “public benefit.” They appointed a committee to determine, 
in their words, “the mode of improvement for ornament, and in 
what manner the ancient works shall be preserved.” They gave

the first forty-­eight men, US Fort Harmar already stood across the

to trade, although incursions into their homelands were already

The ancient Adena-­era Conus Mound is now accompanied by the graves of Marietta’s

MANASSEH CUTLER’S EXCAVATION
The only archaeological investigation of the Conus was done by one of the 
town’s founders, the Reverend Manasseh Cutler, who wrote of his early 
attempt: An opening being made at the summit of the great mound, there 
were found the bones of an adult in a horizontal position, covered with a 
flat stone. Beneath this skeleton were three stones placed vertically at small 
and different distances, but no bones were discovered. That this venerable 
monument might not be defaced, the opening was closed without further 
search. Cutler’s respectful closing of the mound reflected the Marietta 
founders’ admiration for the monuments among which they were building 
their town.
2 Capitolium Mound. Ancient Marietta’s larger square enclosure 
contained four flat-­topped rectangular platform mounds—­an un-
common shape in Hopewell architecture: the Quadranaou Mound 
with four ramps, the Capitolium with three ramps, another with two 
ramps, and a final unramped mound. The Capitolium still stands be-
side Fifth Street near Washington, where a principal gateway once 
opened into the now-­lost large enclosure. In 1918, the Capitolium 
was co-­opted as the foundation of the Carnegie-­funded Washington 
County Public Library, whose 
founders insisted this met the 
criterion of “public benefit” as 
specified in the town’s earth-
works preservation ordinance.
The Anti-­Ramp. One of the Capitolium’s three ramps carries the li-
brary’s front steps to the sidewalk. Another swells in the narrow yard 
behind. The best-­preserved ramp slopes to the alley on the right 
side of the building. On the left side, the ancients designed only a 
kind of hollow between two lobes of Earth (archaeologist Bill Pick-
ard has called this the “anti-­ramp”). In 1990, when the library was 
adding an elevator in the anti-­ramp, excavations confirmed that the 
Capitolium was indeed from the Hopewell era. A buried hearth con-
tained charcoal from many different kinds of trees: Such a collection 
the main features respectful Latin names, evoking their mysteri-
ous antiquity. While inroads were made on the earthworks over 
the years, the town’s vigilance has kept several of them as gems in 
its historic crown. A large ringed mound they named the “Conus” 
and developed their own cemetery around it. One rectangular plat-
form mound became the “Capitolium” and a second, larger one, 
the “Quadranaou.” A broad ramp down to the river they called the 
“Sacra Via”—­and it still suggests grand and solemn processions.
1 Conus Mound. Marietta’s Mound Cemetery (Fifth St. at Scam-
mel) is centered on the ancient Conus Mound. It is a burial struc-
ture, and the best surviving example of the Adena tradition’s large 
conical mounds surrounded by a ditch and ring. A stone stairway 
(added in 1837) ascends to its thirty-­foot summit; the surrounding 
earthen circle and its inner ditch accentuate the mound’s height 
and lovely profile. Toward the northwest, the wall and ditch level 
out to suggest a passage—­an apparent entryway. From this point, 
a subtle swell in the ground extends to where the neighboring 
twenty-­seven-­acre square enclosure once was. Nearby in the cem-
etery are memorials to soldiers of the American Revolution, along 
with later graves.
The ramped platform of the ancient 
Capitolium mound is clearly visible 
beneath Marietta’s public library.

WORLD RENEWAL
The cyclical continuity between life and death is dramatized every winter 
when life seems to die, or hides in the earth like buried seeds, and then 
reawakens every spring. Many Indigenous groups still place this theme of 
World Renewal at the center of their ceremonial life. Human beings have a 
responsibility in this renewal: Without their ceremonies, the world might 
die. Sacred fires are extinguished each year and rekindled. Sometimes 
ancestral spirits are reincarnated in adoptees who recycle the names, 
roles, and responsibilities of the deceased. The Earth Diver creation story, 
in which an animal creates the land by bringing up a bit of mud from 
beneath the water, suggests that mound and earthwork construction 
can also be seen as World Renewal—­a ritual reenactment of the Earth’s 
creation.
The large Quadranaou Mound, with its four symmetrically spreading ramps, stands in a 
park at the head of the ancient Sacra Via.
DATING BY TREE RINGS
Cutler’s curiosity about the age of the earthworks led him to this method: 
When I arrived, the ground was in part cleared, but many large trees 
remained on the walls and mounds. The only possible data for forming any 
possible conjecture respecting the antiquity of these works, I conceived must 
be derived from the growth upon them. By the concentric circles, each of 
which denotes the annual growth, the age of the trees might be ascertained.
He counted 463 rings on one old stump, but then noticed it was coming out 
of another, older ancestor, so he doubled the number. It was a good early 
effort to use a scientific method—­what we now call “dendrochronology.” 
He did not know that the number needed to be doubled again, nor did the 
makers of some much more recent city plaques, which still declare the 
earthworks to be 900 years old instead of closer to 1,800.
This early illustration of the Sacra Via by 
Charles Sullivan suggests the grandeur of 
its tall constructed embankments as they 
approached the riverbank.
recruits. The large park surrounding the Quadranaou is directly adja-
cent to the broad incline of the ancient Sacra Via. It was here or nearby 
where Manasseh Cutler studied tree rings to determine the age of the 
earthworks, whose origins still lay shrouded in mystery at the time.
4 Sacra Via. Old maps and drawings show the Sacra Via as a long 
broad ramp with high earthen walls on both sides, leading up 
from the Muskingum River to Marietta’s large rectangular enclo-
sure. Its monumental 150-­foot width survives as a park linking 
the Quadranaou with the river. The builders excavated the slope 
out of the terrace and riverbank to a depth of at least 15 feet and 
then raised the walls another 8 feet above that. Even without its 
walls today, this is the best evoca-
tion of the monumental, graded 
ways that welcomed river-­borne 
visitors to many of the Ohio 
earthwork complexes. The ramp 
was carefully engineered—­its 
width was crested in the center 
like a modern highway, and its 
suggests a type of Indigenous ritual called “World Renewal” in which 
collections or representations of elements from the natural world, 
or things from the people’s lives, are ritually burned or buried in the 
belief that it helps to ensure the balance and continuity of nature.
3 Quadranaou Mound. The largest of Marietta’s earthworks is the 
well-­preserved Quadranaou. Its broad, rectangular form stands in 
a park at Fourth and Warren Streets—­10 feet tall and nearly 200 feet 
long. Elegant, wide ramps climb to the centers of its four sides. Grand 
gatherings, dances, and games are easily imagined on its level surface. 
During the American Civil War, the Union army used it for mustering

FROM BERMS TO BRICKS
Marietta’s first town council made a special resolution to preserve the 
Sacra Via, in their words, “as common ground . . . never to be disturbed or 
defaced.” But by 1882, when J.P. MacLean visited, the walls were gone. He 
wrote, On inquiring what had become of these walls I was informed that the 
material had been moulded into bricks; that a brick-­maker had been elected 
a member of the town council, and he had persuaded the other members 
to vote to sell him the walls. The bricks ended up in a unitarian church 
down the street. Yet even with only hints of its original walls, this ancient 
roadway remains impressive.
Most spectacular of Marietta’s grand houses 
is the Castle—­a large, turreted, Gothic 
Revival mansion built in 1838 and now open 
to the public.
David Putnam’s stone house was finished 
in 1805 in the typical New England style, 
only seventeen years after the first crude 
settlers’ cabins.
The W.P. Snyder Jr., the last surviving 
steam-­powered sternwheel tugboat, 
is maintained as an attraction in 
association with the Ohio River Museum.
Revolutionary War, and who visited the town in 1825 during his 
grand tour of the newly liberated and expanding nation. Up where 
the ancient earthen walls once stood, wide brick streets are now 
lined with huge trees and beautiful houses in many nineteenth-­
century styles—­most notably the Gothic Revival Castle (418 Fourth 
St., open for tours) and the House of Seven Porches (331 Fifth 
St.)—­as well as monumental churches. The oldest houses in Mar-
ietta are in the New England style familiar to the town’s founders, 
like that of David Putnam (519 Fort St.) on the Fort Harmar side 
of the Muskingum, reached today by a repurposed railroad bridge.
Harmar Historic District. In 1785, three years before Putnam and the 
settlers arrived, Lieutenant Colonel Josiah Harmar had been put in 
military charge of the Ohio Country for the newly formed nation. 
One of his first jobs was to keep illegal settlers out while Congress 
developed their plans to sell off the land. But squatters came any-
how, and were aggravating the tribes in the area, who had agreed 
to an earlier peace treaty. Colonel Harmar ordered a pentagonal 
stockade fort to be built at the mouth of the Muskingum, and the 
officer in charge named it after his commander. Despite its stated 
purpose to keep settlers out (if only temporarily), almost immedi-
ately the new fort encouraged settlement, as the new founders of 
Marietta felt safer in its shadow. The fort is gone, but a small his-
toric district retains the name.
Two Museums. The Ohio History Connection operates two history mu-
seums in Marietta. The Campus Martius Museum tells the story of the 
Ohio Company and the town’s early settlement. It preserves the land 
office where the deeds were signed as new settlers claimed their piece 
of the West. The museum stands on the site of the original Campus 
Martius fortification, preserving a 
section with its rough-­sawn boards 
and meticulously pegged joints. 
Rufus Putnam’s own house also 
provides a glimpse into the lives of 
the first settlers. Nearby, the Ohio 
River Museum (reopening in 2027, 
20-­foot-­high walls were lined with clay to help them survive flood-
ing. Its central axis is aligned with the winter solstice sunset atop 
the high hill across the river.
Historic Architecture. The town of Marietta is a charming place. 
The downtown is large, by turns quaint or elegant, and thriving; 
near the riverfront stands the proud Lafayette Hotel (101 Front 
St.), named after the French officer who helped the US win its

check the website for updates) tells the stories of the river’s ecologies 
and cultures plus its many means of transport—­from dug-­out canoes 
to the largest and finest of the steam-­powered riverboats. Outside on 
the water, the restored sternwheeler W.P. Snyder Jr. offers tours in con-
nection with the museum. The Ohio River Sternwheel Festival every 
September regularly draws 100,000 people to this lovely river town.
Blennerhassett Island. About fifteen miles downstream from Marietta, 
Blennerhassett Island Historical State Park (West Virginia) is a 
worthwhile side trip. The Palladian mansion and gardens of an English 
aristocrat and his wife, it dates originally from the first decade of 
the 1800s. The lavish setting they created here in the wilderness was 
astonishing but short-­lived, as the owner became involved in the Aaron 
Burr treason scandal, and the estate burned to the ground in 1811. 
Tours of the restored house and grounds begin with a sternwheeler 
riverboat ride from downtown Parkersburg, West Virginia.
OHIO RIVER SCENIC BYWAY
The north bank of the Ohio River, for its entire 943-­mile length, is traced by a scenic 
highway route—­upstream from Marietta to Wheeling (West Virginia) and Pittsburgh 
(Pennsylvania), and downstream to Portsmouth, Cincinnati, and beyond. It passes 
through other interesting Ohio towns such as Gallipolis (of French origins) and Ripley 
(of Underground Railroad fame). Forested hills, winding roads, charming towns, local 
food and culture, and nearly continuous river views abound.

RESOURCES
Days 9–­11 in Granville: visit the Columbus area, the Newark sites, then via Flint 	
	
Ridge and the Muskingum Route to Marietta.
Days 12–­13 in Marietta: the earthworks and museums, then Athens and Zaleski 	
	
en route to the Hocking Hills.
Day 14: Return via Hocking Hills highlights.
FURTHER READING
Hall, Robert L. An Archaeology of the Soul: North American Indian Belief and Ritual. 
Urbana: University of Illinois Press. 1997. A powerful, detailed presentation 
of the richness, complexity, and continuity of Native American spiritual belief 
across the Midwest and Great Plains, examined through the scholarship of  
history, archaeology, anthropology, linguistics, and mythology.
Hancock, John E., with Marti L. Chaatsmith, Bradley T. Lepper, and Bret J. Ruby. 
Hopewell Ceremonial Earthworks. Washington DC: Smithsonian Books, 2026.  
A richly illustrated, general audience presentation of Ohio’s eight UNESCO 
World Heritage–­inscribed earthworks, blending historical, archaeological,  
architectural, landscape, and Indigenous knowledge and perspectives.
Kennedy, Roger. Hidden Cities: The Discovery and Loss of Ancient North American  
Civilization. New York: The Free Press, 1994. A history of the encounters 
between the United States’ founders and the earthworks, how the sites were 
interpreted, and their fate since, by the former director of the Smithsonian 
National Museum of American History and the National Park Service.
Lepper, Bradley T. Ohio Archaeology: An Illustrated Chronicle of Ohio’s Ancient 
American Indian Cultures. Wilmington, Ohio: Orange Frazer Press, 2005.  
A general audience survey of archaeological practice and knowledge covering 
all periods of Ohio’s Indigenous inhabitation, including detailed accounts of 
the Adena, Hopewell, and Fort Ancient–­era earthworks and artistry.
Lynott, Mark J. Hopewell Ceremonial Landscapes of Ohio: More than Mounds and 
Geometric Earthworks. Oxford: Oxbow Books, 2015. A thorough survey of the 
archaeological studies of the principal Hopewell earthwork sites by one of the 
National Park Service’s foremost archaeologists, featuring the detailed excava-
tions at the Hopeton Earthworks carried out under his direction.
Redmond, Brian G., Bret J. Ruby, and Jarrod Burks, editors. Encountering 
Hopewell in the Twenty-­First Century, Ohio and Beyond (Volume One:  
Monuments and Ceremony). Akron, Ohio: University of Akron Press, 2019.  
A collection of essays showcasing the state of the art in Hopewell studies  
since the turn of the current century; volume one covers remote sensing,  
archaeoastronomy, Native ontologies, ritual remains, and iconography.  
Available online.
Redmond, Brian G., Bret J. Ruby, and Jarrod Burks, editors. Encountering 
Hopewell in the Twenty-­First Century, Ohio and Beyond (Volume Two:  
Settlements, Foodways, and Interaction). Akron, Ohio: University of Akron 
Press, 2020. Volume two follows the above, highlighting settlements,  
food production, raw material acquisition, and local and inter-­regional  
interactions. Available online.
SAMPLE ITINERARIES
There may be a month’s worth of travel experiences covered in this book, but 
time limits and preferred travel styles suggest the need to prioritize. Besides the 
star ratings provided, here are a few sketch suggestions for how to visit the  
most important places, some at the optimal time of day, and also to enjoy some 
of the region’s most distinctive lodgings.
Long Weekend Highlights Tour
Day 1: 	Fort Ancient in the morning, lunch in Hillsboro, Serpent Mound by late  
	
afternoon. Dinner and overnight at Adams County’s Murphin Ridge Inn.
Day 2: 	Seip Earthworks and the Hopewell Mound Group loop trail in the morning.  
	
Lunch in Chillicothe and afternoon visit to Adena, dinner and overnight at 	
	
a historic Chillicothe bed-­and-­breakfast.
Day 3: 	Mound City in the early morning, lunch in historic Lancaster or Granville.  
	
Afternoon and early evening visits to Newark’s Great Circle and Octagon 	
	
Earthworks. Dinner and overnight at one of Granville’s historic inns.
One-­Week Highlights Tour with Single-­Night Lodgings
Day 1: 	Cincinnati to Miamisburg Mound, then SunWatch Village. Lunch in Dayton 	
	
or Yellow Springs, afternoon hike in Clifton Gorge. Dinner and overnight at  
	
Lebanon’s Golden Lamb Inn.
Day 2: 	Fort Ancient in the morning, lunch in Hillsboro, Serpent Mound by late  
	
afternoon. Dinner and overnight at the Murphin Ridge Inn.
Day 3: 	Fort Hill hike in the morning, the Appalachian Forest Museum and  
	
Seip Earthworks in the afternoon. Dinner and overnight in downtown 		
	
Chillicothe.
Day 4: 	Hocking Hills highlights (Rock House, Cedar Falls, Ash Cave), overnight in  
	
a cabin.
Day 5: 	Athens or Zaleski in the morning, Marietta’s museums and earthworks  
	
in the afternoon and early evening. Dinner in Marietta and overnight at the 	
	
riverfront Lafayette Hotel.
Day 6: 	Up the Muskingum River, with lunch in Stockport or Somerset. Newark’s 	
	
Great Circle by late afternoon. Dinner and overnight at one of Granville’s 	
	
historic inns.
Day 7: 	Early morning tour of Newark’s Octagon Earthworks, afternoon in  
	
Columbus and the Ohio History Center exhibits.
Two-­Week Highlights Tour from Five Main Lodgings
Days 1–­3 in Cincinnati: Lower Miami Route, Miamisburg, then Dayton area  
	
en route to Lebanon.
Days 4–­5 in Lebanon: Upper Miami Route, Fort Ancient, then Serpent Mound 		
	
en route to Chillicothe.
Days 6–­8 in Chillicothe: the Hopewell Culture National Historical Park sites,  
	
Fort Hill and the Highlands Nature Sanctuary, Portsmouth and the Lower 	
	
Scioto Route, then Circleville and Lancaster en route to Granville.

Squier, Ephraim, and Edwin Davis. Ancient Monuments of the Mississippi Valley: 
Comprising the Results of Extensive Original Surveys and Explorations.  
Smithsonian Contributions to Knowledge 1. Washington, DC: Smithsonian 
Institution, 1848. The indispensable, magisterial volume filled with beautiful 
lithographs of the earthworks and some of the artistry, with descriptions of 
their form and condition in the mid-­1800s. Reprinted with an Introduction by 
David J. Meltzer, Smithsonian Institution Press, 1998.
Townsend, Richard V., and Robert V. Sharp, editors. Hero, Hawk, and Open Hand: 
American Indian Art of the Ancient Midwest and South. Chicago and New Haven: 
The Art Institute of Chicago and Yale University Press, 2004. A beautifully 
illustrated presentation of artistry from the ancient American Midwest and 
Southeast, interpreted within their cultural contexts and with current  
Indigenous themes and perspectives.
